AUDIT — READ FIRST. Last audited: 30 October 2026. This file is an independent audit and corrected rewrite of the Wisconsin Cannabis & Hemp Research Parts 0–12 report (last researched 28–29 October 2026). The auditor did not write the original. The federal baseline in the commissioning brief was used as given and was not re-audited; only whether the report states it correctly, and whether Wisconsin's responses to it are accurate, was checked. Every section and all detail from the original are preserved below; only what the audit changed has been changed. Where a correction could not be made by a clean mechanical edit to a long passage, the audit table's corrected wording governs and the passage retains a [Verify] flag — do not quote the old figure without the audit row.

AUDIT TABLE — Section | Claim | Verdict | Primary source (URL) | Corrected wording

Scope: 59 claim-clusters were audited (one row = one claim-cluster, not every sentence). Verdict counts sum exactly to 59: Confirmed 13, Corrected 30, Removed 4, Unverified 12. “Confirmed” means the passage was opened on a primary source in this audit (docs.legis.wisconsin.gov, DATCP, DOR, USDA AMS/NASS, official municipal Legistar/Municode/minutes, official tribal code, or a federal court order). Search excerpts from a primary domain that were not opened as a full page are treated as Unverified in this table, as the original's own rule requires. No personal phone number or email is reproduced; agency public contacts only.

Part 0 / Federal baselineWisconsin has no adult-use and no state-licensed medical programme; marijuana remains Schedule I in Wisconsin under split schedulingConfirmeddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2025/proposals/reg/sen/bill/sb534No change. Do not present the CBD certification as a “medical programme”.
Part 0 / Part 1“Possible fourth FDA-approved-product exclusion [Verify]”CorrectedWisconsin Court System, WI JI-Criminal 6030 (Sept 2024), quoting §961.14(4)(t)1–4; State v. Lewis, 2024AP1678§961.14(4)(t)4: “A drug product in finished dosage formulation that has been approved by the FDA that contains cannabidiol … and no more than 0.1 percent (w/w) residual tetrahydrocannabinols.” Flag removed; direct current §961.14 page still to be opened before republication.
Part 1 — CBD§961.32(2m)(b) allows possession of cannabidiol “in a form without a psychoactive effect”Correcteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §961.32(2m)Current (2m)(b): “An individual may possess a cannabidiol product if the individual has certification stating that the individual possesses a cannabidiol product to treat a medical condition,” certification ≤1 year old and any physician expiration date not passed. No percentage THC limit is stated in (2m). The 2017 Act 4 wording is superseded by 2019 Act 68.
Part 1 — CBDCertification contents, physician licensed under §448.04(1)(a), no qualifying-condition list, no card/registry, no certification needed for hemp (§94.55(1)) or FDA-approved drugConfirmeddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §961.32(2m)No change.
Part 1 — CBD§961.38(1n) dispensing “by a pharmacy or physician approved under §961.34(2)(a) or (b), in a form without a psychoactive effect”; §961.34(2) CSB “shall approve which pharmacies/physicians may dispense”Correcteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §§961.38, 961.34 (current text via search/open in audit)Current (1n)(a): “A pharmacy or physician approved under s. 961.34(2) may dispense cannabidiol products as a treatment for a medical condition.” Current §961.34(2) is only the investigational-drug-permit aid sentence (21 USC 355(i)); the approval sentence was the pre-2019 form, repealed by 2019 Act 68. Delete it as current law.
Part 1 — HempHemp defined by delta-9 only, ≤0.3%Correcteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov/statutes/statutes/94/55 — §94.55(1)“Hemp” = Cannabis sativa L. and any part, including seeds and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with delta-9-THC “not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis or the maximum concentration allowed under federal law up to 1 percent, whichever is greater,” tested post-decarboxylation or by other similarly reliable methods. Operative threshold today remains 0.3%; the 1% clause does not authorise 1% today.
Part 1 — Hemp§94.55 penalty subsection flagged VerifyCorrected§94.55§94.55(8): “A person violating this section shall forfeit not more than $500 for each violation.”
Part 1 — Hemp§94.55(2)(am) DATCP licensing condition; §94.55(2)(a) “to the greatest extent allowed under federal law”Confirmed§94.55(2)(a), (2)(am)No change. “Spent in practice” is analysis, not a repeal — keep labelled as such; do not state (2)(am) was repealed.
Part 1 — PenaltiesSimple possession §961.41(3g)(e): first ≤$1,000 / ≤6 months; second+ Class I felony; no amount thresholdConfirmeddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §961.41(3g)(e)No change. Do not import failed-bill 25g/28g thresholds into current law.

Part 1 — PenaltiesPossession with intent §961.41(1m)(h) tiers; §961.41(1r) whole-product weightCorrecteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §961.41(1m)(h), (1r)Use “shall be fined” (mandatory in form) for (1m)(h). Correct the (1r) cite: its second sentence covers (1)(h) and (1m)(h) only, not (3g)(e) — immaterial in practice because (3g)(e) has no amount threshold. Re-cite (1m)(h) to the current §961.41 page, not the 1999 page.
Part 1 — Penalties§961.55(9) hemp-crop seizure “in a whole dry plant”Correcteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §961.55(9)Average delta-9 >0.7% above the permissible limit → entire crop at the growing location seized/destroyed, after written test documentation. Keep the Verify on post-2022 operation (mechanism presupposes a state licensing apparatus).
Part 1 — Penalties§961.32(3) hemp immunity “acting in accordance with §94.55”Correcteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §961.32(3)(b)“A person who is not otherwise violating s. 94.55 … may not be prosecuted…” State v. Syrrakos outcome remains Unverified — do not cite briefs as law.
Part 1 — Hemp THCDelta-8 lawful in Wisconsin; THCA flower lawful; no AG opinion neededUnverifieddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §§961.14(4)(t), 94.55(1); DOJ opinion index (not exhaustively searched)CONTESTED — do not upgrade. No Wisconsin statute/rule/formal AG opinion located naming delta-8 lawful or unlawful. THCA: post-decarboxylation test is the hook; retail-flower testing guidance remains Verify. The 10 May 2018 Schimel enforcement announcement is an announcement, not an opinion and not law, predates 2019 Act 68, and is not authority for delta-8/THCA/beverages or the 2026 federal change. “No opinion located” must not be upgraded to “no opinion exists”.
Part 1 — Hemp THCNo statewide minimum age, potency/serving cap, mandatory retail testing, labelling, child-resistant packaging or seed-to-sale rule for hemp THCConfirmeddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §§94.55, 961.14, 961.32 (negative search)No change; all such rules remain correctly labelled as failed-bill proposals only.
Part 1 / Part 9 — Dates2013 Act 267 effective 17 Apr 2014; 2017 Act 100 effective 1 Dec 2017; 2019 Act 68 effective 27 Nov 2019CorrectedLegislative Council Act Memo, 2013 Act 267; 2017 Act 4/100 and 2019 Act 68 pages (enactment + publication dates, §991.11)2013 Act 267 effective 18 Apr 2014 (not 17 Apr; never “2014 Act 267”). 2017 Act 100 enacted 30 Nov 2017, published 1 Dec 2017, effective 2 Dec 2017. 2019 Act 68 enacted 26 Nov 2019, published 27 Nov 2019, effective 28 Nov 2019. 1 Dec / 27 Nov are publication dates. 2017 Act 4 enacted 17 Apr 2017, published 18 Apr 2017, effective 19 Apr 2017.
Part 1 / Part 9 — ScheduleWisconsin automatically follows federal schedule changes; tribal medical programme counts as “state-licensed”Unverifieddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §961.11(4), (4g)§961.11(4) requires (i) a federal designation/rescheduling/deletion, (ii) notice to the Controlled Substances Board, (iii) Board affirmative action, and (iv) a final rule after 30 days, unless the Board or an interested party objects (then objection published, hearing afforded, Board action stayed until a rule under (2)). If objection is made, the Board determines treatment under (1)/(1m)/(1r)/(2). (4g) (CBD/nabiximols): “as soon as practically possible but no later than 30 days”. Whether a licence-conditional federal move triggers (4) at all, whether notice was given, whether any objection was filed, and whether any Board rule issued were not located — Verify. Do not state Wisconsin THC has moved to Schedule III, and do not state a tribal programme is or is not “state-licensed” — no verified Wisconsin tribal medical programme exists to which the question could attach, and the federal rule text was not re-audited (baseline as given).

Product-by-product consequences are analysis, not a DOR hemp ruling — keep Verify. No DOR hemp/CBD sales-tax publication was located.
Part 2 — TaxesControlled-substance stamp tax §§139.87–139.96 “still in force”, $3.50/g, $1,000/plant; Hall vs Jones conflict left unresolvedCorrected§§139.87–139.88 (statute excerpts)Subchapter IV remains printed with $3.50 per gram of THC material (§139.88(1)) and $1,000 per THC plant (§139.88(1d)); dealer thresholds >42.5g / >5 plants (§139.87(2)). Hall (1997) held the 1995 version unconstitutional; Jones, 2002 WI App 196, held §§139.87–139.96 as amended by 1997 Wis. Act 27 constitutional — so the “conflict” resolves in favour of the amended version. Do not state the tax is actively collected, or dead, without a DOR check — no DOR statement on current stamp sales/assessments was located. Current-year page not opened — Verify.
Part 2 — TaxesVapour tax 5¢/ml (§139.76(1m)); vapour distributor permit Form CTV-200 (§139.79)ConfirmedDOR vapour FAQ (laws as of 27 Oct 2026)No change to the 5¢/ml rate or the packaged-item limitation. Permit fee is not stated on that page — keep Verify on any fee. Do not assume hemp smokables are tobacco-taxed, and do not assume a hemp pre-roll escapes all excise if sold as a vapour product.
Part 2 — TaxesWisconsin 280E decoupling; §71.22(4)(l); SB 859/AB 870 §71.22(4)(o) as current lawCorrecteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §71.22(4)(n) (search excerpt); AB 870 proposal textNo Wisconsin decoupling statute from IRC §280E was located [Verify by full-text search of ch. 71]. Conformity is static: current paragraph is §71.22(4)(n) (not (l)) — “Internal Revenue Code” as amended to 31 Dec 2022 for taxable years beginning after 31 Dec 2022. §71.22(4)(o) (IRC to 31 Dec 2025) in 2025 SB 859/AB 870 was a proposal only and must not be cited as current law; its reported failure 23 Mar 2026 requires a docs.legis status-page open to confirm. Whether any federal Schedule III/§280E relief would flow through to Wisconsin tax remains Verify, and in any event there is no Wisconsin-licensed medical activity to which it could apply. Adult-use activity remains fully subject to §280E under the given baseline.
Part 3 — MadisonCity of Madison (Dane County) MGO §23.20, enactment number as cited in the original, 28g, $100 maximum, $1 fineCorrectedMadison Legistar File 62417; Municode Madison §1.08ORD-20-00109 (not 00111), adopted 17 Nov 2020, mayor approval 24 Nov 2020, published 3 Dec 2020, effective 4 Dec 2020. §23.20(2): 18+ may possess ≤28g with permission; §23.20(5) forfeiture up to $100. The $1 is not a fine for ≤28g possession with permission (that is not a §23.20 violation) — it is the §1.08 cash deposit for >28g in a public place, school-grounds/school-bus possession, and non-consensual consumption, plus statutory costs. Possession by a person under 17 is “Mandatory Appearance”, not $1. State law is unchanged. Town of Madison is a different municipality — do not attribute City figures to it.

Section number (24.01 vs 24.02) is also unsettled. Costs are additional.

Part 3 — La Crosse CountyLa Crosse County $1, Oct 2025ConfirmedLa Crosse County Board Minutes 16 Oct 2025 (certified by County Clerk Ginny Dankmeyer 17 Oct 2025)Ord. 223-10/25 (16 Oct 2025, 18 ayes, 11 nays, 1 excused), amending §§9.20 and 25.04(2)(c): penalty $1 for possession of marijuana (§9.16) and drug paraphernalia (§9.17), excluding statutory costs, effective the day after passage and publication. Part 3 — Authority§66.0107(1)(bm) / §59.54(25) quoted in pre-2013 Act 293 form (“25 grams or less” grant; county ordinance does not apply where a municipality has its own)Correcteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §§66.0107(1)(bm), 59.54(25) (search text; direct consolidated open still required)Do not publish the pre-Act 293 quotation as current law. The post-2013 form drops “25 grams or less” from the operative grant and instead bars prosecution under the ordinance if a complaint alleges >25g, or any amount after a prior possession conviction, unless charges are dismissed or the DA declines to prosecute. §59.54(25)(b) states a county ordinance “applies in every municipality within the county” — in tension with the older sentence. Do not publish a single county-vs-city precedence rule until the current consolidated text is directly opened. §66.0109 remains Unverified for republication.

Part 4 — Food licencesHemp food/beverage fees “$78–$685 plus $261 canning” as current; retail-meals $105/$230/$330/$540 as currentCorrecteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §§97.29, 97.30, 97.27 (search excerpts)Do not quote “$78–$685 plus $261 canning” as current. The current statute fee table is $50/$110/$120/$270 by production volume and hazard, “unless otherwise required by department rule” — the DATCP rule/fee schedule controls and was not opened. Retail-meals figures were not opened — Verify. Licence types are directionally supported: §97.29 processing plant, §97.30 retail food, §97.27 warehouse; no hemp-specific food licence. No DATCP/DOR statement that a food licence alone authorises an intoxicating-hemp/THC beverage, or that a THC beverage needs (or does not need) a ch. 125 alcohol licence, was located — Verify, do not advise either way.
Part 5 — Data sourcesUSDA producer count for Wisconsin: 470 (Governor, Nov 2025) as the official count; 298 as an alternative official countRemovedUSDA AMS Public Search Tool; AMS FOIA Reading Room “USDA Hemp Producer Licensees” PDF (updated 14 Apr 2026) — neither opened in this auditNeither figure is adopted. The AMS FOIA PDF (14 Apr 2026) is the first primary download candidate found; open it and extract the Wisconsin count with its as-of date before any count is published. Part 7 — USDA licenceUSDA hemp producer licence: 3-year term, 10-year felony bar, FSA lot/GPS report in 30 days, no feeCorrected7 CFR 990.22; 7 CFR 990.23Term confirmed: valid until 31 Dec of the year three years after the year issued; non-transferable; multi-state rule in §990.22(c). Felony bar confirmed: no licence unless criminal-history report(s) confirm no state/federal felony conviction relating to a controlled substance within the past 10 years, unless §990.20(b) exception applies. FSA reporting confirmed: report acreage to FSA within 30 days of planting, with street address + geospatial location where practicable, acreage/square footage, licence number; all locations reported. Fee corrected: no fee is stated in §990.22, but absence there does not prove a $0 fee — §990.21 and the AMS producer page were not opened. Do not publish “USDA fee: $0” on this evidence; keep Verify. The 60-day criminal-history-report rule (§990.21(a)(3)(ii)) is search-excerpt only — Verify.
Part 7 — Hemp programmeDATCP pilot ended 1 Jan 2022; growers to USDA; processors no longer need a DATCP licenceConfirmedDATCP release 2 Sep 2021No change. Processors remain under DATCP food/consumer authority. The 2017 pilot law (2017 Act 100) and its end are as corrected in the dates row above.
Part 6 — Market2021 hemp acreage: single figure (DATCP 1,989 or NASS 680)CorrectedDATCP 2021 Plant Industry Bureau Annual Report; USDA NASS National Hemp Report, 17 Feb 2022Do not pick one and delete the other, and do not average them. DATCP programme data: 1,989 acres planted in 66 counties, 679 growers licensed / 655 with annual registration authorised to grow, 427 processors licensed / 417 registered, 722 regulatory samples. NASS survey estimate: 680 acres planted / 580 harvested (all uses, open; floral only 110 acres harvested). They are different instruments, not two measurements of the same census; neither opened source reconciles the gap. If a single pilot-era headline is required, use the DATCP figure and label the NASS figure separately as a survey estimate. Do not splice the series into one trend line.
Part 6 — MarketNASS floral values 2024 $8.525m at $55/lb; 2025 $5.256m at $21.90/lb; floral shares 86% / 98.5%; under-protection area [Verify]CorrectedUSDA NASS National Hemp Report, 16 Apr 2026Confirmed: 2024 open-grown floral 275 ac harvested, $55.00/lb, $8,525,000 utilised; 2025 325 ac, $21.90/lb, $5,256,000. Totals: 2024 460 planted / 320 harvested; 2025 550 planted / 330 harvested. Shares 275/320 = 86% and 325/330 = 98.5% are arithmetically correct. Under-protection floral: 2024 $125,000; 2025 $1,172,000. Constructed floral-only sums $8.650m (2024) / $6.428m (2025) must stay labelled as constructed sums, not total Wisconsin hemp value (grain/fibre/seed withheld as (D)). Correction: under-protection area Verify flag is removed — 59,815 sq ft (2023), 42,260 sq ft (2024), 59,730 sq ft (2025).

Part 6 — MarketIllinois 2022 cross-border: $121.2m sales / $36.1m tax as a proven Wisconsin-resident total (fiscal year 2022)UnverifiedLFB memo, 10 Mar 2023 (PDF not opened in this audit)Keep flagged. As reported second-hand: calendar-year (not fiscal-year) 2022 border-county out-of-state sales $121.2m; Illinois tax $36.1m estimated on the LFB assumption that all border-county out-of-state sales were to Wisconsin residents — the memo itself notes this may overstate Wisconsin residents (e.g. Jo Daviess County also borders Iowa) and misses Wisconsin purchases in non-border counties such as Cook County. $121.2m is therefore an out-of-state-sales figure used as a Wisconsin proxy, not a proven Wisconsin-resident total. Route: obtain the memo PDF from the Legislative Fiscal Bureau or the requesting senator's office (Sen. Melissa Agard) and cross-check against Illinois IDFPR county data. Michigan/Minnesota do not track nonresident purchases, so WI figures for MI/MN and for IL after 2022 remain [Unavailable].

Part 12 — DrivingOWI: pure zero-tolerance for delta-9; §346.63(1)(d) prescription defence does not cover delta-9; §343.305(10) doubling; §346.65 penalties as settledCorrecteddocs.legis.wisconsin.gov — §§340.01(50m)(e), 346.63, 343.305 (search excerpts)Not pure zero-tolerance for delta-9: §346.63(1)(am) says “detectable amount”, but for delta-9 that term is defined by §340.01(50m)(e) as delta-9-THC, excluding precursors/metabolites, at ≥1 ng/mL in blood. Frett). The 2023 petition bills (SB 70/SB 78/AB 43/AB 37/SB 38, SB 1113 §973.016) are proposals, not law. Wisconsin Cannabis & Hemp Research

Corrected rewrite — Parts 0–12. Last audited: 30 October 2026. Originally last researched 28–29 October 2026. Every fact below carries a source link and that accessed date, unless the entry itself says the page could not be opened. See the audit table above for verdicts and corrected wording; where the audit table and the body differ, the audit table governs.

How to read this file. Law is taken from official sources only: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov, the Wisconsin Administrative Code, DATCP, the Department of Revenue (DOR), the Department of Justice, the Department of Health Services, the Wisconsin Elections Commission / county clerks, official tribal sources where cited, and official county, city, village and town codes. Secondary sources are used only for non-law facts (market figures, polling, events) and are labelled secondary. A proposed, vetoed or failed bill is never presented as law. [Verify] means uncertain, conflicting, or not opened on the official page itself. [Unavailable] means searched for and not found. No personal phone number or email of any individual is reproduced; agency public contacts only are given, and none were needed for Parts 0–6 except as noted.
Cover limits — read first. (1) Several statute quotations in Part 1 rest on Act text or official search excerpts, not on a single opened current statute page for that section; those are flagged for direct re-pull before publication. (2) Several local fines in Part 3 rest on secondary compilations because the current Municode text was not opened in this research pass; those are labelled SECONDARY — VERIFY, not OFFICIAL. (3) No official statewide count of hemp/CBD retailers, no official hemp retail sales total, and no DOR hemp sales-tax total were located. (4) The USDA producer count conflict (470 vs 298) is preserved unresolved in Parts 5–6. Do not quote a single figure from this file without its date, source and flag.
Contents: Part 0 Headlines Part 1 Laws Part 2 Taxes Part 3 Local Rules Part 4 Licence Types Part 5 Licence Data Sources Part 6 Market Data

Federal baseline — given, not re-researched

Split scheduling: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714). All adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final. IRS § 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to § 280E. Hemp: PL 119-37 § 781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 § 2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026, the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026. The federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. There is no federal cannabis banking law.

Wisconsin application, stated plainly: Wisconsin has no state-licensed medical marijuana programme. Under the split scheduling, therefore, marijuana remains Schedule I in Wisconsin. Whether any tribe operates a medical programme that counts as “state-licensed” is [Verify] and is for Part 10; no tribal programme is treated as state-licensed in this Run 1 file. Wisconsin’s hemp-derived THC market is largely unregulated at retail (Part 1.5), and Wisconsin has enacted no state workaround for the 12 November / 11 December 2026 federal hemp dates (Part 1.5.7).

PART 0 — HEADLINES

  1. No recreational marijuana and no state-licensed medical marijuana programme. The only medical carve-out is a narrow physician-certification rule for cannabidiol (CBD) products, Wis. Stat. § 961.32(2m). There is no Wisconsin medical-marijuana card, registry or dispensary system in current statute. Sources: 2017 Wisconsin Act 4, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/acts/2017/4,1; 2025 SB 534 history, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2025/proposals/reg/sen/bill/sb534 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  2. Marijuana / tetrahydrocannabinols remain Schedule I in Wisconsin, subject only to the statutory exclusions for certified CBD products, stalk/seed/fibre products, and THC contained in hemp as defined in § 94.55(1), plus the fourth exclusion in §961.14(4)(t)4 (FDA-approved finished-dosage cannabidiol, no more than 0.1% (w/w) residual tetrahydrocannabinols). Source: Wis. Stat. § 961.14(4)(t) as amended by 2019 Act 68, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/acts/2019/68,54n — accessed 28 October 2026.
  3. Hemp-derived THC is the only lawful THC market in Wisconsin, and it is largely unregulated at retail. Wisconsin law defines hemp by delta-9 THC only. Current statute sets no statewide minimum purchase age, no potency/serving cap, no mandatory retail testing, labelling, child-resistant-packaging or seed-to-sale rule for hemp-derived THC products. General weights-and-measures labelling in Wis. Admin. Code chs. ATCP 90 and 91 does apply to packaged hemp flower/CBD products (Part 1.6).
  4. Every major 2025–26 cannabis/hemp bill failed on 23 March 2026 pursuant to 2025 Senate Joint Resolution 1, including the Republican medical bill (AB 547 / SB 534), the Democratic adult-use + medical bill (AB 1061 / SB 1045), the three-tier hemp bill (AB 606 / SB 681), the hemp-derived-cannabinoid sales bill (AB 747 / SB 682), the under-21 intoxicating-hemp bill (AB 680 / SB 644), and the total-THC hemp-definition bill (AB 503 / SB 499). None is law. As of 28 October 2026 the session is in interim/committee work until 4 January 2027, subject to an extraordinary session (Part 1.9).
  5. Governor Evers’ 2025–27 budget cannabis proposal was stripped before enactment. Companion budget bills AB 50 / SB 45 would have created medical + adult-use markets; the Joint Finance Committee removed the cannabis provisions on 8 May 2025. Secondary reporting: JD Supra / Amundsen Davis, 15 May 2025, amundsendavislaw.com — accessed 28 October 2026; official JFC vote record not opened in this pass [Verify].
  6. DATCP’s state hemp programme ended 1 January 2022; Wisconsin growers are now licensed directly by USDA. Source (official DATCP page, opened): hemp.wi.gov — accessed 28 October 2026: “Beginning January 1, 2022, the DATCP hemp program is discontinued” and “Beginning January 1, 2022, Wisconsin hemp growers are licensed by the U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA).”
  7. The looming federal hemp redefinition is Wisconsin’s largest near-term exposure, and Wisconsin has enacted no state workaround. Governor Evers wrote to Wisconsin’s Congressional delegation (letter dated 27 February 2026, published 3 March 2026) urging a two-year delay, stating the change would jeopardise “nearly 3,500 jobs” and “$700 million in economic impact” and noting “as of November 2025, Wisconsin had 470 federally licensed hemp producers.” Source (official Governor’s bulletin, opened): content.govdelivery.com/accounts/WIGOV/bulletins/40be40a — accessed 28 October 2026. No DATCP, DOR or DOJ formal guidance adopting, delaying or mitigating the 0.4 mg cap under Wisconsin law was located — [Unavailable/Verify].
  8. No numbered, formal Wisconsin Attorney General opinion on hemp/CBD or delta-8 was located. What exists is a May 2018 AG enforcement announcement from Attorney General Brad Schimel, not an opinion and not law, predating 2019 Act 68 (Part 1.5.6).
  9. Local patchwork, not legalisation. No local ordinance legalises marijuana. Cities, villages, towns and counties may only change the citation track (municipal/county forfeiture vs state criminal charge) for a first, small-amount possession case, up to 25 grams. Madison (City, Dane County) is the most detailed: 28 grams, with a $100 ordinance maximum and a $1 practical deposit described in secondary reporting (Part 3.2.1). Milwaukee County reduced its county forfeiture to no more than $1 in March 2021; the City of Milwaukee $1 proposal was “placed on file” in July 2021 and its current status is [Verify] (Part 3.2.2).
  10. Advisory referendums are not law, and they all passed except one. In November 2018, 18 county/city advisory questions all passed. In spring 2019, Wood County’s adult-use question failed 40–60% while its medical question passed, and Egg Harbor and Sturgeon Bay questions passed. In November 2022, 3 counties + 5 municipalities all approved adult-use questions. Full results, with clerk-canvass gaps, are in Part 3.3.
  11. Market in one line: no legal marijuana market and therefore no marijuana sales or tax receipts; hemp cultivation collapsed from a 2019 pilot peak (~5,000 acres planted, 1,251 growers) to a few hundred NASS-survey acres (460 planted in 2024, 550 in 2025); official floral hemp value (open-grown only) was $8.525 million in 2024 and $5.256 million in 2025 (USDA NASS, April 2026); the only verified Wisconsin-resident cross-border figure is Illinois calendar 2022 — $121.2 million in border-county sales to out-of-state residents, $36.1 million Illinois tax attributed to Wisconsin residents on the Legislative Fiscal Bureau assumption that all such sales were to Wisconsin residents (Part 6.3).

PART 1 — LAWS

1.1 Legal status

Recreational: ILLEGAL. Medical marijuana: NO PROGRAMME — ILLEGAL except the CBD certification exception. Low-THC/CBD only: PARTLY — hemp (≤0.3% delta-9, see 1.5) and certified non-psychoactive CBD only.

The operative prohibition is Wis. Stat. ch. 961, the Uniform Controlled Substances Act. § 961.41(1) makes it unlawful to manufacture, distribute or deliver a controlled substance except as authorised by ch. 961; § 961.41(1m) covers possession with intent; § 961.41(3g) covers simple possession. THC is listed in Schedule I at § 961.14(4)(t).

“Marijuana” is defined at § 961.01(14) as, in substance, all parts of plants of the genus Cannabis, seeds, resin and every compound/manufacture/salt/derivative/mixture/preparation including THC, excluding mature-stalk fibre, seed oil/cake and sterilised seed incapable of germination — and, critically: “‘Marijuana’ does not include hemp, as defined in s. 94.55 (1).” Source: 2019 Act 68 text, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/acts/2019/68,54n — accessed 28 October 2026.

No out-of-state medical card is recognised. No provision in §§ 961.32, 961.34 or 961.38 located in this pass recognises another state’s medical-marijuana card, registry or recommendation. The CBD certification must be from a physician licensed under Wis. Stat. § 448.04(1)(a). Treat “no recognition” as the current position subject to a full-text re-check in Run 2 — [Verify] for absolute completeness.

1.2 Statutes and regulations — verbatim operative text

A. Wis. Stat. § 961.11 — Controlled Substances Board; federal mirror

Plain summary: the Board administers ch. 961 and may add/delete/reschedule substances by ch. 227 rulemaking. § 961.11(4) is the federal-mirror rule; § 961.11(4g) is a faster mirror rule for cannabidiol, later extended to nabiximols by 2019 Act 68.

§ 961.11(4): “If a substance is designated, rescheduled or deleted as a controlled substance under federal law and notice thereof is given to the controlled substances board, the board by affirmative action shall similarly treat the substance under this chapter after the expiration of 30 days from the date of publication in the federal register of a final order designating the substance as a controlled substance or rescheduling or deleting the substance or from the date of issuance of an order of temporary scheduling under 21 USC 811 (h), unless within that 30-day period, the board or an interested party objects to the treatment of the substance. If no objection is made, the board shall promulgate, without making the determinations or findings required by subs. (1), (1m), (1r) and (2) or s. 961.13, 961.15, 961.17, 961.19 or 961.21, a final rule, for which notice of proposed rule making is omitted, designating, rescheduling, temporarily scheduling or deleting the substance. If an objection is made the board shall publish notice of receipt of the objection and the reasons for objection and afford all interested parties an opportunity to be heard.”

Source: DSPS statement-of-scope quotation (official DSPS document), dsps.wi.gov/Documents/RulesStatutes/CSB2012AS.pdf — accessed 28 October 2026. The full current § 961.11 page on docs.legis.wisconsin.gov was not opened as a single page in this pass, so this quotation is marked for direct re-verification. Last-amended date for § 961.11: [Verify].

§ 961.11(4g) (2017 Act 4): “Notwithstanding sub. (4), if cannabidiol is rescheduled or deleted as a controlled substance under federal law, the controlled substances board shall similarly treat cannabidiol under this chapter as soon as practically possible but no later than 30 days from the date of publication in the federal register of a final order rescheduling or deleting cannabidiol or from the date of issuance of an order of temporary scheduling under 21 USC 811 (h).”

Sources: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/acts/2017/4,1; 2019 Act 68 amendment adding nabiximols, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/acts/2019/68,54n — accessed 28 October 2026.

Split-scheduling analysis (labelled as analysis, not a Wisconsin official statement): § 961.11(4) speaks of a substance being “designated, rescheduled or deleted” under federal law. The fixed baseline is not a general rescheduling of marijuana — it is a licence-conditional move (state-licensed medical + FDA-approved products to Schedule III; adult-use stays Schedule I). Whether that conditional federal action triggers § 961.11(4) at all, whether notice has been given to the Board, whether any objection was filed, and whether any Board rule has issued, were not located in this pass. Do not state that Wisconsin THC has moved to Schedule III. On the current Wisconsin statute book as located, THC remains in § 961.14(4)(t), Schedule I, with the exclusions below. The Board could in principle act under § 961.11(1) by rulemaking even without a federal trigger, but no such rule was located — [Verify] via the DSPS Controlled Substances Board rulemaking register.

B. Wis. Stat. § 961.14 — Schedule I

§ 961.14(4)(t) (current form after 2019 Act 68): “Tetrahydrocannabinols, commonly known as ‘THC’, in any form including tetrahydrocannabinols contained in marijuana, obtained from marijuana or chemically synthesized, except that tetrahydrocannabinols do not include any of the following: 1. Tetrahydrocannabinols contained in a cannabidiol product that is dispensed as provided in s. 961.38 (1n) (a) or that is possessed as provided in s. 961.32 (2m) (b). 2. Tetrahydrocannabinols contained in fiber produced from the stalks, oil or cake made from the seeds of a Cannabis plant, any other compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture or preparation of the mature stalks (except the resin extracted therefrom), fiber, oil or cake or the sterilized seed of a Cannabis plant which is incapable of germination. 3. Tetrahydrocannabinols contained in hemp, as defined in s. 94.55 (1).”

Source: 2019 Act 68 §§ 62–63, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/acts/2019/68,54n — accessed 28 October 2026. Last amended: 2019 Act 68 (SB 188); effective date 28 November 2019 (published 27 November 2019) — [Audit-corrected]. The older pre-2019 form, with only the CBD exception, is superseded; do not quote it as current. § 961.14(4)(tb) separately lists synthetic cannabinoids, beginning with compounds structurally derived from 3-(1-naphthoyl)indole (e.g. JWH-007, JWH-015, JWH-018/AM-678). Direct current § 961.14(4)(tb) page not opened in this pass — [Verify] full text. Delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THCP and THC-O are not named in (tb) as located; their treatment turns on (t) and the hemp exclusion (1.5), not on (tb). Schedules II–V are §§ 961.16, 961.18, 961.20, 961.22. No cannabis/marijuana substance was located in Schedules II–V in this pass. A fourth exclusion for an FDA-approved finished dosage product is implied in jury-instruction commentary — [Verify] exact (t)4 text from the current statute page.

C. Wis. Stat. § 961.41 — Prohibited acts and penalties (THC)

§ 961.41(1)(h) — manufacture/distribute/deliver THC: “Tetrahydrocannabinols. If the person violates this subsection with respect to tetrahydrocannabinols, included under s. 961.14 (4) (t), or a controlled substance analog of tetrahydrocannabinols, and the amount manufactured, distributed or delivered is: 1. Two hundred grams or less, or 4 or fewer plants containing tetrahydrocannabinols, the person is guilty of a Class I felony. 2. More than 200 grams but not more than 1,000 grams, or more than 4 plants containing tetrahydrocannabinols but not more than 20 plants containing tetrahydrocannabinols, the person is guilty of a Class H felony. 3. More than 1,000 grams but not more than 2,500 grams, or more than 20 plants containing tetrahydrocannabinols but not more than 50 plants containing tetrahydrocannabinols, the person is guilty of a Class G felony. 4. More than 2,500 grams but not more than 10,000 grams, or more than 50 plants containing tetrahydrocannabinols but not more than 200 plants containing tetrahydrocannabinols, the person is guilty of a Class F felony. 5. More than 10,000 grams, or more than 200 plants containing tetrahydrocannabinols, the person is guilty of a Class E felony.”

Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2003/statutes/statutes/961/iv/41/1 — accessed 28 October 2026. There is no separate “cultivation” offence; growing is manufacture under (1)(h), counted by grams or plant numbers.

§ 961.41(1m)(h) — possession with intent to manufacture/distribute/deliver THC: (1) 500 grams or less, or 10 or fewer plants: fine $500–$25,000 and up to 4 years 6 months; (2) more than 500–2,500 grams, or more than 10–50 plants: fine $1,000–$50,000 and 3 months–7 years 6 months; (3) more than 2,500 grams, or more than 50 plants: fine $1,000–$100,000 and 1–15 years. Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov excerpt, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/1999/statutes/statutes/961/iv/41/3g/c — accessed 28 October 2026. Direct current (1m)(h) page re-check: [Verify].

§ 961.41(1r) — determining weight: amounts include the weight of THC “together with any compound, mixture, diluent, plant material or other substance mixed or combined with” it; for (1)(h), (1m)(h) and (3g)(e) the amount of THC “means anything included under s. 961.14 (4) (t)” and includes the weight of marijuana. Source: proposal excerpts quoting current (1r), docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/proposaltext/2023/REG/SB1113,9 — accessed 28 October 2026. Practical consequence (analysis): for edibles, beverages, vapes and concentrates, the whole product weight counts toward the gram thresholds, not the pure THC weight. Last-amended date for § 961.41: [Verify].

D. Other ch. 961 provisions located

  • § 961.442 — penalties; hemp concealment. If a person attempts to conceal a ch. 961 crime while representing that they are engaging in hemp activities, the maximum term may be increased by up to 6 months (misdemeanour) or up to 3 years (felony). Source: 2017 Act 100 text, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2017/related/acts/100/1 — accessed 28 October 2026. Current wording after 2019 Act 68 substituted “hemp” for “industrial hemp” — [Verify].
  • § 961.47 — conditional discharge / deferred prosecution for first possession offenders; § 961.475 — treatment option. Under § 961.47(1), where a person not previously convicted of a ch. 961 / federal / state / county-ordinance controlled-substance offence pleads or is found guilty of possession under § 961.41(3g), the court, without entering judgment and with the accused’s consent, may defer proceedings and place the person on probation; on fulfilment, the court discharges and dismisses without adjudication of guilt, which “is not a conviction for purposes of disqualifications.” Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/proposaltext/2021/REG/SB545,55 — accessed 28 October 2026. Full current § 961.47 text: [Verify]. § 961.475: on a guilty plea/finding for possession under (3g), the court may, on the person’s request and with a treatment facility’s consent, allow voluntary treatment instead of sentence, not exceeding the maximum sentence unless the person consents.
  • § 961.48 — second or subsequent offences. “Except as provided in subs. (2) and (4), any person who is charged under sub. (2m) with a 2nd or subsequent offense under this chapter and convicted of that 2nd or subsequent offense may be fined an amount up to twice that otherwise authorized or imprisoned for a term up to twice the term otherwise authorized or both.” Note: § 961.41(3g)(e) already makes a 2nd THC possession a Class I felony in its own terms; § 961.48 is a general enhancer and § 961.48(5) excludes certain (3g) felonies — interaction [Verify].
  • § 961.49 — protected places (+5 years). Applies only to delivery/distribution under § 961.41(1)(cm),(d),(e),(f),(g),(h) or possession with intent under (1m) of the same paragraphs, where the act is on scattered-site public-housing premises, or within 1,000 feet of a state/county/city/village/town park, jail/correctional facility, multiunit public housing, public swimming pool, youth/community centre, private/public school premises, or a school bus, or on approved treatment-facility premises. The maximum term “may be increased by 5 years.” It does not apply to simple possession.
  • § 961.55 — forfeiture; § 961.55(9) (hemp crop). If a crop intended as hemp is tested and the average delta-9 concentration in a whole dry plant exceeds 0.7 percent above the permissible limit, on a dry-weight basis using post-decarboxylation or similarly reliable methods, the entire crop on that field/growing location “shall be seized and destroyed,” after written documentation of test results to the licensed grower/agent/employee. Source: 2019 Act 68 text, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/proposaltext/2019/REG/SB188,26,3 — accessed 28 October 2026. Current § 961.55(9) after DATCP programme discontinuation: [Verify] — the seizure mechanism presupposes a state licensing apparatus that no longer exists for growers.
  • § 961.46 — distribution to persons under 18. A person 17+ who violates § 961.41(1) (except (1)(h)1g.) by distributing/delivering to a person 17 or under at least 3 years younger faces up to +5 years on the maximum term. This is a general controlled-substance provision, not a hemp-retail age rule.

1.3 Possession, cultivation, paraphernalia, public consumption, product types

Simple possession — § 961.41(3g)(e) (THC)

“Tetrahydrocannabinols. If a person possesses or attempts to possess tetrahydrocannabinols included under s. 961.14 (4) (t), or a controlled substance analog of tetrahydrocannabinols, the person may be fined not more than $1,000 or imprisoned for not more than 6 months or both upon a first conviction and is guilty of a Class I felony for a 2nd or subsequent offense. For purposes of this paragraph, an offense is considered a 2nd or subsequent offense if, prior to the offender’s conviction of the offense, the offender has at any time been convicted of any felony or misdemeanor under this chapter or under any statute of the United States or of any state relating to controlled substances, controlled substance analogs, narcotic drugs, marijuana, or depressant, stimulant, or hallucinogenic drugs.”

Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2005/statutes/statutes/961/iv/41/5/c/2/b/_2?up=1 — accessed 28 October 2026.

Critical warning — do not import bill thresholds into current law: several failed bills would have added a 25 g or 28 g threshold to (3g)(e) (e.g. 2005 AB 255: ≤25 g = forfeiture ≤$1,000; 2021 AB 812: ≤14 g = $100 forfeiture). None enacted. Current (3g)(e) has no amount distinction for simple possession — any amount of non-exempt THC, first conviction, carries the $1,000 / 6-month maximum; any prior controlled-substance felony/misdemeanour (as defined in the paragraph) makes it a Class I felony. Sources for the failed proposals (to show they are not law): docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2005/related/proposals/ab255/21/_1 and docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2021/related/proposals/ab812/19/_1 — accessed 28 October 2026.

First-offence treatment: the statute itself sets the first-conviction penalty above — an unclassified misdemeanour with its own penalty, not a Class A misdemeanour. Separately, § 961.47 conditional discharge (no judgment entered, probation, dismissal without adjudication on fulfilment) and § 961.475 treatment option are available to first possession offenders (1.2D). Class I felony maximum under § 939.50: [Verify] before stating any years/dollars figure. Synthetic cannabinoids — § 961.41(3g)(em): first conviction fine ≤$1,000 or imprisonment ≤6 months or both; 2nd or subsequent: Class I felony, on the same prior-conviction definition.

Conduct (THC, non-exempt)Penalty
Simple possession, 1st conviction — § 961.41(3g)(e)Fine ≤$1,000 or imprisonment ≤6 months or both; no amount threshold
Simple possession, 2nd or subsequent — § 961.41(3g)(e)Class I felony
Manufacture / distribute / deliver — § 961.41(1)(h)≤200 g or ≤4 plants: Class I; >200–1,000 g or >4–20 plants: Class H; >1,000–2,500 g or >20–50 plants: Class G; >2,500–10,000 g or >50–200 plants: Class F; >10,000 g or >200 plants: Class E
Possession with intent — § 961.41(1m)(h)≤500 g or ≤10 plants: $500–$25,000 + up to 4 years 6 months; >500–2,500 g or >10–50 plants: $1,000–$50,000 + 3 months–7 years 6 months; >2,500 g or >50 plants: $1,000–$100,000 + 1–15 years

There is no personal-use home-grow allowance for marijuana in Wisconsin law.

Paraphernalia — §§ 961.571–961.577

  • § 961.573(1) (possession/use): “No person may use, or possess with the primary intent to use, drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyze, pack, repack, store, contain, conceal, inject, ingest, inhale or otherwise introduce into the human body a controlled substance or controlled substance analog in violation of this chapter. Any person who violates this subsection may be fined not more than $500 or imprisoned for not more than 30 days or both.” Under-17 violators are subject to disposition under § 938.344(2e).
  • § 961.574(1) (manufacture/delivery): fine ≤$1,000 or imprisonment ≤90 days or both.
  • § 961.575(1) (delivery to a minor): person 17+ delivering to a person 17 or under at least 3 years younger: fine ≤$10,000 or imprisonment ≤9 months or both.
  • § 961.576 (advertisement): fine ≤$500 or imprisonment ≤30 days or both.
  • § 961.577 (municipal ordinances): cities, villages, towns and counties may prohibit the same paraphernalia conduct.

Source (opened): docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/statutes/statutes/961/VI/573/3/a — accessed 28 October 2026. Note: § 961.571(1)(b)2. excludes “items, including pipes, papers and accessories, that are designed for use or primarily intended for use with tobacco products” from drug paraphernalia. Methamphetamine paraphernalia under § 961.573(3) is a Class H felony (Class G if in presence of a child ≤14) — not cannabis-specific, included only to avoid confusion.

Public consumption

No standalone Wisconsin state “public consumption of marijuana” offence was located in ch. 961. Consumption is reached in practice through possession (§ 961.41(3g)(e)), paraphernalia (§ 961.573), and, for supply offences, the § 961.49 protected-places enhancement (delivery/possession-with-intent only, +5 years, 1,000 feet). Whether smoking marijuana in an enclosed public place is separately prohibited under the Clean Indoor Air law, Wis. Stat. § 101.123, was not confirmed in this pass — [Verify] (the § 101.123 definition of “smoking” and its application to marijuana was not opened). Do not assert a public-consumption ban as state law without that check. Local ordinances are Part 3.

Product types — marijuana vs hemp

Product (non-hemp marijuana source)Status in Wisconsin
Flower / budIllegal — THC under § 961.14(4)(t); possession (3g)(e); manufacture/delivery (1)(h) by weight/plants
EdiblesIllegal if THC is non-exempt — same (t) definition (“in any form”); weight counts as whole product under § 961.41(1r) (analysis)
Vapes / vape cartridgesIllegal if non-exempt THC — same; no vape-specific cannabis exception located
Concentrates / hash / oils / waxIllegal if non-exempt THC — same (t) definition; no separate hash/concentrate schedule
Beverages (marijuana-derived)Illegal if non-exempt THC — same
Any of the above if it is hemp (≤0.3% delta-9 dry weight)Not “marijuana” (§ 961.01(14)) and THC “contained in hemp” is excluded from (t) — see 1.5 for the delta-8/THCA contest

No product-type distinction (e.g. flower banned but edibles allowed) exists in Wisconsin marijuana law. The only product distinction is hemp vs non-hemp, and certified-CBD vs non-certified.

1.4 CBD exception — §§ 961.32(2m), 961.38(1n), 961.34

§ 961.32(2m)(a) — “certification” defined (2017 Act 4): “In this subsection, ‘certification’ means a letter or other official document issued by a physician licensed under s. 448.04 (1) (a) that contains all of the following: 1. The name, address, and telephone number of the physician. 2. The name and address of the patient who is issued the letter or document. 3. The date on which the letter or document is issued.”
§ 961.32(2m)(b) — possession (2017 Act 4 form; 2019 Act 68 substituted “cannabidiol product” for “cannabidiol” and added the hemp/FDA sentence): “An individual may possess cannabidiol in a form without a psychoactive effect if the individual has certification stating that the individual possesses cannabidiol to treat a medical condition, if the certification has an issue date that is no more than one year prior to the possession, and if any expiration date provided by the physician in the certification has not passed.” 2019 Act 68 form: “An individual may possess a cannabidiol product in a form without a psychoactive effect if the individual has certification stating that the individual possesses a cannabidiol product to treat a medical condition, if the certification has an issue date that is no more than one year prior to the possession, and if any expiration date provided by the physician in the certification has not passed. A certification is not required to possess hemp, as defined in s. 94.55 (1), or a prescription drug product that has been approved by the U.S. food and drug administration.”

Sources: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/acts/2017/4,1 and docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/acts/2019/68,54n — accessed 28 October 2026.

  • Who may possess: any individual with a qualifying certification — no qualifying-condition list, no age limit, no registry, no card, and no dispensary licence is created by this section. The condition need only be a “medical condition” stated in the certification (post-Act 4; pre-Act 4 it was seizure disorders only).
  • THC limit: none stated as a percentage in § 961.32(2m). Current §961.32(2m)(b) says “cannabidiol product” and states no percentage limit. Do not quote “0.3%” as the CBD-certification limit — 0.3% is the hemp definition, a different statute (§ 94.55(1)). A CBD product that is also hemp needs no certification at all (2019 sentence above).
  • Dispensing — § 961.38(1n): (a) “A pharmacy or physician approved under s. 961.34 (2) (a) or (b) may dispense cannabidiol in a form without a psychoactive effect as a treatment for a seizure disorder or any medical condition.” (b) A physician licensed under § 448.04(1)(a) may issue the certification stating the individual possesses CBD to treat a medical condition if the CBD is in a form without a psychoactive effect.
  • § 961.34(2) — investigational-drug route: on a physician’s request, the Controlled Substances Board “shall aid the physician in applying for and processing an investigational drug permit under 21 USC 355 (i) for cannabidiol” and, if FDA issues the permit, “shall approve which pharmacies and physicians may dispense cannabidiol.” Exact current wording of the condition phrase — “seizure disorder” vs “medical condition” — [Verify] from the current § 961.34 page.
  • What the CBD exception does NOT do: it does not authorise marijuana flower, THC edibles/vapes/concentrates, or any psychoactive product; it does not create a medical-marijuana programme; it does not recognise out-of-state cards; and, for hemp CBD sold at retail, it is largely redundant because hemp needs no certification.
  • Historic CBD-producer licensing (2013 Act 267, subch. XIV of ch. 440, § 440.9892): whether any § 440.9892 licence remains operative after 2019 Act 68, and whether that subchapter was repealed, was not confirmed in this pass — [Verify]. Do not describe a current CBD-producer licence as available.

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

1.5.1 Hemp definition and THC measurement — § 94.55(1)

“‘hemp’ means the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis or the maximum concentration allowed under federal law up to 1 percent, whichever is greater, as tested using post-decarboxylation or other similarly reliable methods. ‘Hemp’ does not include a prescription drug product that has been approved by the U.S. food and drug administration.”

Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2023/statutes/statutes/94/55/2/b/3 — accessed 28 October 2026.

  • Delta-9 only, on a dry-weight basis, post-decarboxylation. Wisconsin’s definition does not say “total THC” and does not say “delta-9 + (THCA × 0.877)” in its text. Post-decarboxylation testing, however, converts THCA to delta-9 for measurement — so a high-THCA product that tests ≤0.3% delta-9 without decarboxylation but >0.3% with it is not hemp under the Wisconsin test as written. This is textual analysis; no DATCP testing protocol for retail products was located.
  • The “up to 1 percent, whichever is greater” clause: because the current federal maximum is 0.3%, the operative Wisconsin threshold today is 0.3% delta-9. The clause would only lift Wisconsin to a higher federal maximum if federal law itself allowed one, capped at 1%. It does not authorise 1% today.
  • What counts: “all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers” — on its face broad enough to include hemp-derived cannabinoids if the plant/product as a whole meets the delta-9 test. That breadth is the source of the delta-8/THCA dispute below.
§ 94.55(2)(a): “Subject to the provisions under this section, a person may plant, grow, cultivate, harvest, produce, sample, test, process, transport, transfer, take possession of, sell, import, and export hemp in this state to the greatest extent allowed under federal law.”

§ 94.55(2)(am) — DATCP licensing (conditional): “The department shall issue licenses to hemp producers if hemp producers are required to hold a license to produce hemp under federal law and if the secretary of the U.S. department of agriculture has approved this state’s hemp program. If the department issues such licenses, no person may produce hemp without a license from the department.” Source same as above — accessed 28 October 2026. This conditional licensing power is spent in practice: DATCP discontinued its programme 1 January 2022 and USDA now licenses Wisconsin growers (hemp.wi.gov — accessed 28 October 2026). Whether § 94.55(2)(am) has been formally amended/repealed since 2022: [Verify].

  • § 94.55(2g) — negligent violations (producers only): a producer who negligently violates § 94.55 or its rules — including failing to provide a legal description of land, failing to obtain a required licence/authorisation, or producing Cannabis sativa L. with delta-9 >0.3% (or the federal maximum up to 1%) — must comply with a DATCP correction plan with a reasonable correction date and periodic reporting for ≥2 years; 3 negligent violations in 5 years = ineligible to produce hemp for 5 years from the 3rd violation.
  • § 94.55(2m) — greater violations: if DATCP determines a producer violated § 94.55/rules “with a culpable mental state greater than negligence, the department shall immediately report the hemp producer to the U.S. attorney general and the department of justice.”
  • § 94.55(2p) — felony bar: no person may produce hemp for 10 years following a controlled-substance felony conviction (state/federal) unless they held a valid pilot-programme licence/registration/authorisation on 20 December 2018 and the conviction predates that date.
  • § 94.55(2r) — false statement: material falsification in a hemp-programme application = ineligible to participate. Sources for (2g)–(2r): docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/statutes/2019/94.55(2g)(b)2. — accessed 28 October 2026; [Verify] against the current docs.legis.wisconsin.gov page.
  • § 94.55(2t) — CBD access preserved: “Nothing in this section or rules promulgated under this section shall be construed as limiting a person’s access to cannabidiol products under s. 961.32 (2m) (b).”
  • § 94.55(3m) and penalty: a § 94.55 violation carries a forfeiture of not more than $500 per violation under the older pilot-era text quoted in search excerpts — [Verify] current penalty subsection.
  • Hemp-producer immunity — § 961.32(3): a person acting in accordance with / not otherwise violating § 94.55 or DATCP rules under § 94.55(2)(b) may not be prosecuted under ch. 961 or a municipal ordinance enacted under §§ 59.54(25)/66.0107(1)(bm) for specified hemp activities, and a person who violates § 94.55/rules may not be prosecuted under § 94.55/ch. 961 unless referred to the district attorney by DATCP (with a parallel local-law-enforcement referral rule for ordinances). A negligent producer is separately protected under § 961.32(3)(cm). Full current § 961.32(3) text: [Verify]. Practical note: this referral shield was litigated in State v. Syrrakos (Wis. Ct. App. No. 2024AP000556, briefs 2024–25) over whether it protects a processor charged under ch. 961 for non-hemp THC products rather than for a § 94.55 violation — outcome [Verify] in Run 2; do not cite the briefs as law.

1.5.2 Delta-8, delta-9, THCA flower, other hemp-derived THC — status

  • Delta-9 THC in hemp (≤0.3% dry weight, post-decarboxylation): LAWFUL as hemp. It is excluded from Schedule I THC by § 961.14(4)(t)3. and from “marijuana” by § 961.01(14). Because the limit is a concentration, a large-volume product (notably a beverage) can lawfully contain several milligrams of delta-9 while staying ≤0.3% by weight — arithmetic from the definition, and the reason hemp THC beverages are sold in Wisconsin today. Product availability is not a legal ruling.
  • Delta-8 THC: CONTESTED / [Verify] — no Wisconsin statute, rule or formal AG opinion located that names delta-8 as lawful or unlawful. For lawfulness: § 961.14(4)(t) covers “tetrahydrocannabinols … in any form,” but (t)3. excludes THC “contained in hemp,” and § 94.55(1) defines hemp to include “all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers” of Cannabis sativa L. meeting the delta-9 test. Delta-8 is an isomer of THC. Against lawfulness for converted products: most commercial delta-8 is not extracted as delta-8 from the plant; it is chemically converted (isomerised) from CBD. Whether converted delta-8 is THC “contained in hemp” — as opposed to THC “chemically synthesized” within (t) — has no Wisconsin appellate ruling or AG opinion located in this pass. The same contest applies to delta-10, HHC, THCP and THC-O. Do not state delta-8 is “legal in Wisconsin” as settled law. Retail availability is not legality.
  • THCA flower: CONTESTED on the same axis, but with a clearer testing hook. THCA itself is not delta-9. Wisconsin’s hemp test is post-decarboxylation. Therefore: THCA flower that tests ≤0.3% delta-9 after decarboxylation is hemp; THCA flower that exceeds 0.3% after decarboxylation is not hemp, and its THC is Schedule I THC / marijuana. No Wisconsin statute or rule was located that exempts THCA flower from post-decarboxylation testing or that treats “THCA” as a separate lawful category. [Verify] whether any DATCP/USDA testing guidance for Wisconsin-grown flower applies this test to retail flower (USDA production testing does account for THCA-derived THC for growers; retail flower is a separate question).
  • Synthetically derived / converted cannabinoids generally: the federal baseline’s post-11 December 2026 definition excludes synthetic/converted cannabinoids from hemp. Wisconsin has no parallel state exclusion in § 94.55(1) as of 28 October 2026. After the federal change, a converted product that is no longer federal hemp would also fail Wisconsin’s “to the greatest extent allowed under federal law” framing in § 94.55(2)(a) and the (t)3. exclusion’s dependence on the § 94.55(1) definition — but Wisconsin has not amended § 94.55(1) to say so. See 1.5.7. This interaction is analysis, not a Wisconsin official position.
  • CBD, CBG, CBN and other non-intoxicating hemp cannabinoids: lawful as hemp components if the source material meets § 94.55(1). No Wisconsin potency cap on CBD was located.

1.5.3 Minimum age, testing, labelling, retail rules (current law)

  • Minimum age for hemp-derived THC or CBD: NONE in state statute located. No § 94.55 age rule exists. Age rules exist only in failed bills (1.9) and, per secondary reporting, in some local ordinances (Part 3).
  • Mandatory retail testing: NONE located for hemp products sold at retail in Wisconsin. Historic DATCP pilot rules required pre-harvest / fit-for-commerce testing for growers (THC ≤0.3% for a fit-for-commerce certificate). No retail COA, contaminant, potency or batch-testing mandate in current §§ 94.55/961.14 was located. Grower testing now sits under USDA’s programme (federal, Part 7 in the full research).
  • Labelling: general, not hemp-THC-specific — see 1.6 (ATCP 90/91). No Wisconsin requirement located for THC content, serving size, warnings, “21+”, “intoxicating” disclosure, QR code/COA link, or child-resistant packaging for hemp products. All such requirements appear only in failed bills (1.9).
  • Retail licence/permit for hemp products: NONE located under § 94.55. General business requirements (seller’s permit, food licensing where applicable) are Part 4, and are not hemp-specific licences.

1.5.4 THC beverages

Currently sold under the hemp concentration rule (1.5.2): a beverage ≤0.3% delta-9 by dry weight is hemp, even if it contains multiple milligrams of THC per container. No Wisconsin beverage-specific THC cap, serving definition, or container-size rule exists in current law. Failed SB 681/AB 606 would have capped beverages at 20 mg THC per container/serving (1.9) — proposed only.

Food-law overlay — [Verify] / contested: DATCP’s historic position (2019-era meeting notes, secondary) was that FDA considers CBD/THC drugs that cannot be in food in traceable amounts, while other DATCP guidance treated hemp-derived ingredients in food as lawful if fit-for-commerce, Wisconsin-processed, labelled and sold in Wisconsin. Those historic DATCP food guidances were not re-located as current official pages in this pass, and DATCP’s hemp page says it is not regularly updated. Do not state a current DATCP food position without re-checking DATCP Food Safety guidance in Run 2. The general point stands: ATCP 90/91 labelling does not by itself authorise a food ingredient.

1.5.5 Other state hemp provisions

Wis. Stat. § 97.02 (food definitions) and § 94.55 cross-references in ch. 961 are listed in secondary indexes as hemp-related. No hemp-specific operative text in § 97.02 was opened in this pass — [Verify] whether § 97.02 contains any hemp provision or is merely a general food statute cited alongside hemp. Truth-in-labelling / false claims: DATCP’s weights-and-measures fact sheet (1.6) and general consumer-protection law apply, but no hemp-specific false-claims statute in § 94.55 was located beyond the general § 94.55 penalty/forfeiture. [Verify] against Wis. Stat. ch. 100.

1.5.6 Attorney General — what exists and what does not

No numbered formal AG opinion (e.g. “OAG-XX-XX”) on hemp, CBD or delta-8 was located on the Wisconsin DOJ site or docs.legis.wisconsin.gov in this pass. If a formal opinion exists, it was not found — [Verify] via the DOJ opinion index before Run 2 asserts non-existence.

What does exist — AG Schimel announcement, 10 May 2018 (enforcement guidance, NOT an opinion, NOT law): after an April 27, 2018 DOJ / Wisconsin Statewide Intelligence Center analytical note stating CBD was unlawful, Attorney General Brad Schimel announced after a meeting with the Wisconsin Farm Bureau Federation, DATCP and legislators that farmers following DATCP rules would not be prosecuted for producing CBD oil and that products made from lawfully grown pilot-programme hemp, including CBD, were lawful. Quoted wording (secondary reporting of the AG statement):

“Therefore, I am advising law enforcement not to take enforcement action against products made from industrial hemp that is grown under a lawful hemp research pilot program, including CBD, until Congress considers changes to the law, enabling the Wisconsin State Legislature to further clarify the status of these products.”

Sources (secondary, quoting the AG): hempsupporter.com and State Bar of Wisconsin, Wisconsin Lawyer, January 2026, “The State of Hemp-Derived Products in Wisconsin”, wisbar.org — accessed 28 October 2026 (“wrote a memo instructing state agencies that lawful production of CBD products and retail sale of such lawful products are permitted and should not be prosecuted”).

Weight today: the May 2018 position was expressly interim (“until Congress considers changes…”), predates 2019 Act 68 (which superseded the pilot framework), and does not address delta-8, THCA flower, converted cannabinoids, beverages, or the 2026 federal redefinition. It must not be cited as current authority for intoxicating hemp products. No AG Kaul-era (2019–present) formal hemp guidance was located — [Verify] via DOJ.

1.5.7 Wisconsin response to the federal hemp dates (PL 119-37 § 781 / PL 119-103 § 2019)

No Wisconsin statute, rule or DATCP/DOR/DOJ guidance was located that amends § 94.55(1) to a total-THC definition, adopts a 0.4 mg per container cap, creates a state transition period, or otherwise responds to PL 119-37 § 781 / PL 119-103 § 2019. The only Wisconsin official response located is Governor Evers’ February 2026 letter urging Congress to delay/modify the federal law (Part 0, headline 7) — a lobbying position, not a change to Wisconsin law.

Consequence analysis (labelled as analysis, using the fixed baseline + Wisconsin text):

  • Until 11 December 2026: Wisconsin’s delta-9-only § 94.55(1) definition remains the state test. A product meeting it is hemp under Wisconsin law even if it would fail a total-THC test, subject to federal law prevailing where it applies.
  • From 12 November 2026 (PL 119-37 § 781, first stage, per baseline) / 11 December 2026 (full effect per PL 119-103 § 2019): products that cease to be federal hemp — notably products exceeding 0.4 mg total THC per container and synthetic/converted cannabinoids — would no longer be producible/sellable “to the greatest extent allowed under federal law” under § 94.55(2)(a), and the § 961.14(4)(t)3. exclusion (“THC contained in hemp, as defined in s. 94.55(1)”) would be in tension with a federal definition that no longer treats the product as hemp. Wisconsin has not resolved that tension by statute. Whether Wisconsin courts/prosecutors would treat post-December products as Schedule I THC under ch. 961, or whether § 94.55(1)’s standalone delta-9 text would be read to preserve them as state-law hemp despite federal status, is unresolved and [Verify] — it is the single largest open legal question in this Part.
  • Wisconsin’s “maximum concentration allowed under federal law up to 1 percent, whichever is greater” clause does not help after the federal change: the federal maximum is falling, not rising, so the “greater” figure remains the (now much lower, per-container) federal figure in practical effect — but the clause is written as a concentration (percent), while the new federal cap is per container (mg), so the two metrics do not map onto each other. [Verify] with a Wisconsin legislative counsel / AG reading before advising any business.
  • USDA lab-testing delay to 31 December 2026 (baseline) and the federal medical-marijuana rider through 11 December 2026 (baseline): no Wisconsin implementing action was located for either. Both are federal matters; Wisconsin’s grower licensing is already federal (USDA), so the lab-testing delay operates at the federal level for Wisconsin growers without a state intermediary.

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning, buffers — hemp products

Packaging/labelling (general weights-and-measures, official DATCP fact sheet, opened, rev. 30 December 2024): “CBD oils, food products, health and beauty products, and hemp flower must meet the method of sale and labeling requirements specified by Wisconsin Administrative Code Chapters ATCP 90 and ATCP 91, and NIST Handbook 130.” Required on all packaged hemp flower/CBD products: (1) Declaration of Product Identity — Wis. Admin. Code § ATCP 90.02 (clearly/conspicuously identify contents on the principal display panel); (2) Declaration of Responsibility — § ATCP 90.03 (if sold anywhere besides the premises where packaged: name of manufacturer/packer/distributor, business address including street/city/state/ZIP, and “packaged by”/“distributed by” connection if not the manufacturer); (3) Declaration of Net Quantity — § ATCP 90.04 (net quantity excluding tare, clearly/conspicuously in the bottom 30% of the principal display panel, in the required unit). Method-of-sale table in the same fact sheet: liquid food by volume (U.S. customary + metric), liquid non-food (e.g. CBD oil) by volume (metric), non-liquid food (e.g. gummies, chocolate) by weight (U.S. customary + metric), non-liquid non-food (e.g. pre-rolled joints, soap, balm) by weight (metric), random-weight hemp flower/seed/kief by weight with unit + total price. Source (opened): datcp.wi.gov/Documents/HempFlowerCBDProductsMethodSaleLabeling.pdf — accessed 28 October 2026.

  • Child-resistant packaging, tamper-evidence, THC warnings, potency/serving labelling, COA/QR requirements for hemp products: NONE located in current Wisconsin law. They appear only in failed bills (AB 606/SB 681 would have required tamper-evident packaging, COAs for concentrates, warnings including “WARNING: UNFINISHED PRODUCT; NOT INTENDED FOR HUMAN CONSUMPTION IN THIS FORM” for unfinished product, etc. — proposed only).
  • Advertising: no hemp-specific advertising statute/rule was located. The only ch. 961 advertising offence located is § 961.576 (drug paraphernalia advertising: fine ≤$500 / ≤30 days). General false-advertising / consumer-protection law (ch. 100) was not examined in this pass — [Verify] for hemp claims.
  • Delivery: no Wisconsin hemp-delivery licence, permit or delivery-specific rule was located. § 94.55(2)(a) allows a person to “transport, transfer, take possession of, sell, import, and export hemp … to the greatest extent allowed under federal law” — delivery as a form of sale/transport is not separately licensed or prohibited in § 94.55 as located. Failed AB 606/SB 681 would have created a three-tier distribution system with delivery restrictions — proposed only.
  • Zoning / buffer distances for hemp retail: no state zoning, setback or buffer rule for hemp/CBD/smoke/vape shops was located in §§ 94.55, 961.14 or 961.41. The only state “buffer” in ch. 961 is the § 961.49 criminal enhancement (1,000 feet from parks, schools, etc. for delivery/possession-with-intent), which is not a retail zoning rule. Local zoning is Part 3.

1.7 Seed-to-sale / product tracking

None in current Wisconsin law for marijuana or hemp. No Wisconsin statute or rule located creates a Metrc-style or other state seed-to-sale system, requires plant/product tagging, or requires retail inventory reporting to the state for hemp products. Historic DATCP pilot recordkeeping (planting/production reports, fit-for-commerce certificates, and the surviving 3-year record duty for 2018–21 registrants, hemp.wi.gov — accessed 28 October 2026) is recordkeeping, not seed-to-sale tracking, and it is historic.

Proposed only: 2025 AB 547 / SB 534 (medical) would have created a “seed-to-sale database” as part of a medical programme (SB 534 title/summary, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2025/proposals/reg/sen/bill/sb534 — accessed 28 October 2026). 2025 AB 606 / SB 681 (hemp three-tier) would have created a permit/licensing chain with inventory controls — proposed only. Neither enacted.

1.8 Employment and drug-testing protections

  • No Wisconsin statute located that protects an employee or applicant for marijuana use, for a positive marijuana drug test, or for off-duty lawful hemp-THC use. Because marijuana is illegal in Wisconsin and there is no medical programme, there is no Wisconsin equivalent of a medical-marijuana employment-protection statute.
  • No general private-sector drug-testing restriction statute was located. Secondary employment sources agree Wisconsin “doesn’t prohibit or restrict employee drug testing” in private employment and that no Wisconsin statute addresses private-employment drug testing, while noting federal DOT testing and public-works contractor testing as separate regimes. Sources (secondary): nolo.com (updated 5 November 2025) — accessed 28 October 2026. [Verify] against Wis. Stat. chs. 103 and 111 and the public-works testing statute (commonly cited as § 103.50 in secondary sources — section number [Verify]) before Run 2 publication.
  • Fair Employment Act (Wis. Stat. §§ 111.31–111.395): the Act prohibits discrimination on listed protected characteristics. Marijuana use is not a listed protected characteristic, and a positive THC test from lawful hemp use raises a fact-specific discrimination/disability analysis discussed in State Bar commentary (secondary) — not a protection. Do not advise that hemp use is protected.
  • Proposed only: 2025 AB 547 / SB 534 (medical) expressly listed “fair employment law” among the areas it would have amended. 2025 AB 1061 / SB 1045 (adult-use + medical) likewise would have addressed employment consequences — proposed only. A 2023 decriminalisation-era proposal (AB 861) would have created § 103.157 employer non-liability for not testing for THC/synthetic cannabinoids — also proposed only, and it protected employers who do not test, not employees who test positive. Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/proposaltext/2023/REG/AB861,11,2 — accessed 28 October 2026.

1.9 Pending legislation — final status confirmed on docs.legis.wisconsin.gov

Session framework (official): 2025 Senate Joint Resolution 1 establishes the 2025–26 biennial session period from Monday 6 January 2025 to noon Monday 4 January 2027, with floorperiods including 13–15 January, 20–22 January, 10–19 February and 17–19 March 2026 (last general-business floorperiod), a limited-business floorperiod 14–16 April 2026, a veto-review floorperiod 12–13 May 2026, and interim/committee work from 20 March 2026 to 4 January 2027. Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2025/related/proposals/sjr1 — accessed 28 October 2026. Bills not passed by the end of the general-business period are recorded as “Failed to pass pursuant to Senate Joint Resolution 1” on 23 March 2026 — that is the standard end-of-session disposition, not a floor vote against the bill.
