PART 2 — TAXES

2.1 Sales and use tax — general framework

Wisconsin imposes a 5% state sales and use tax. The DOR fiscal estimate for 2025 SB 681 states this expressly: “At a state sale and use tax rate of 5%, the department can estimate potential sales tax revenues of $6.7 million in the first full year after sales begin and $8.8 million in the second full year.” Source: DOR Fiscal Estimate, LRB 25-5185/1, SB 681, dated 4 Dec 2025 — docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2025/related/fe/sb681/sb681_dor.pdf — accessed 28 October 2026. The state tax is imposed under Wis. Stat. § 77.52 on retail sales of tangible personal property and specified taxable services — [Verify] current § 77.52 text was not opened in full in this pass; the 5% rate itself is confirmed by the DOR fiscal estimate above. There is no cannabis-specific or hemp-specific sales-tax exemption in force located in this pass, and in particular no in-force medical-cannabis sales-tax exemption, because Wisconsin has no state-licensed medical cannabis programme. The only medical-cannabis sales-tax exemption located is PROPOSED in 2025 AB 547 / SB 534 (2.7 below).

2.2 Sales-tax treatment of hemp and CBD products

Default treatment: taxable tangible personal property. Secondary tax guidance summarising Wisconsin law states: “Cannabidiol (CBD) and hemp products taxed as tangible personal property. Products may be exempt if they are food or food ingredients,” citing Wis. Stat. § 77.52(1)(a) and § 77.54(20n)(a). Source (secondary): Wolters Kluwer, “State by state sales tax on cannabis”, wolterskluwer.com — accessed 28 October 2026. [Secondary; DOR has no hemp-specific sales-tax publication located in this pass — gap.]

Food / food-ingredient distinction — product-by-product: food and food ingredients sold for home consumption are generally exempt in Wisconsin, but the exemption does not extend to candy, soft drinks, dietary supplements, or prepared food, and meals/food/beverages sold for direct consumption on the premises are taxable. Those exclusions are in Wis. Stat. § 77.54(20) / (20n) and related DOR guidance as summarised in secondary sources. Current § 77.54(20n) text not opened in full in this pass — [Verify] exact current paragraph lettering before publication, because older statute prints show the food exemption at § 77.54(20).

Practical consequence, stated as analysis of the above rules, not as a DOR hemp ruling: a hemp/CBD oil, capsule, tincture marketed as a supplement, or a topical/cosmetic, falls on the taxable tangible-personal-property side; a product that genuinely qualifies as food or a food ingredient and is not candy, a soft drink, a dietary supplement, or prepared/on-premises food falls on the exempt side. A gummy is the hard case: if it is candy/confection it is taxable even if it contains a food ingredient. No DOR ruling classifying a specific hemp gummy, beverage, or tincture was located — [Verify] for any product-specific classification. Dietary supplements are excluded from the food exemption under the rule summarised above; a CBD product sold as a dietary supplement should therefore be treated as taxable unless a DOR product ruling says otherwise — [Verify].

PROPOSED change that confirms the current ambiguity: 2025 AB 606 / SB 681 would have amended § 77.51(3t) so that “Food and food ingredient” “does not include alcoholic beverages or tobacco, hemp-derived cannabinoid products,” and would have added HDC products sold for consumption on the seller’s premises to the premises-consumption tax in § 77.98. That amendment was not enacted. Source: AB 606 proposal text excerpts — docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2025/related/proposals/ab606/49/_1 — accessed 28 October 2026.

No DOR hemp/CBD sales-tax revenue figure exists in this pass. DOR’s SB 681 fiscal estimate expressly states: “There is no state-specific data for Wisconsin to accurately construct a fiscal estimate” for HDC products, and it therefore modelled Wisconsin on Minnesota and Michigan data. Source: DOR Fiscal Estimate, SB 681 — accessed 28 October 2026. The same estimate’s modelled, not collected figures, for a hypothetical regulated market, were: Wisconsin retail HDC sales $134.4 million in year 1 and $175.9 million in year 2; state sales tax $6.7 million / $8.8 million; local sales tax $0.8 million / $1.1 million. These are PROPOSED-bill estimates only, not revenue collected, and must not be quoted as Wisconsin hemp tax revenue.

2.3 Local sales taxes

  • County tax: most Wisconsin counties impose a 0.5% county sales and use tax. Secondary summaries of DOR Publication 209 state 70 of 72 counties have adopted it. [Secondary; current DOR Publication 209 edition and the identity of the two non-adopting counties were not opened in this pass — Verify.]
  • Milwaukee County and City of Milwaukee — maximum combined rate: Milwaukee County 0.9% (increased from 0.5% effective 1 Jan 2024 under 2023 Wisconsin Act 12, as summarised in secondary sources); City of Milwaukee 2.0% city sales tax, effective 1 Jan 2024. Combined in the City of Milwaukee: 5% state + 0.9% county + 2% city = 7.9%. [Secondary; 2023 Act 12 text not opened in this pass — Verify before citing Act 12 as the authority.]
  • Premier resort area tax: secondary summaries of DOR guidance state most premier resort municipalities charge 0.5%, with the City of Wisconsin Dells and Village of Lake Delton at 1.25%. [Secondary; current premier-resort list, including the 1 July 2026 additions reported secondarily for Sturgeon Bay and the Town of Minocqua, not verified from DOR in this pass — Verify.]
  • General local-tax principle: local sales taxes apply to the same taxable base as the state tax — a sale exempt from the 5% state tax is not subject to the county/city add-ons. [Verify — stated in an older DOR Publication 209 excerpt found in search; current edition not opened.]
  • No local cannabis/hemp tax in force was located. A municipal cannabis excise tax appears only in PROPOSED bills (2.7).

2.4 Hemp-specific excise tax — none in force

No hemp-specific excise tax, HDC occupational tax, or cannabis excise tax is in force in Wisconsin as of 28 October 2026. Every hemp/cannabis excise located is in a failed or stripped proposal (2.7). The only hemp-product excise that does apply in force is the vapor products tax where the product meets the vapor-product definition (2.5). That tax is not hemp-specific; it applies regardless of nicotine content.

2.5 Vapor products tax — the one excise that reaches hemp vapes

“Wisconsin law imposes an excise tax of 5 cents per milliliter of liquid or other substance on vapor products sold, offered or exposed for sale, possessed with intent to sell or removal for consumption or other disposition for any purpose by any person engaged as a distributor of them in Wisconsin (sec. 139.76(1m), Wis. Stats). The tax is due at the time the vapor products are received in Wisconsin for sale or distribution.”
“‘Vapor product’ means a noncombustible product that produces vapor or aerosol for inhalation from the application of a heating element to a liquid or other substance that is depleted as the product is used, regardless of whether the liquid or substance contains nicotine (sec. 139.75(14), Wis. Stats).”

Source: DOR, “Vapor Products Tax” FAQ, laws stated as of 27 Oct 2026 — revenue.wi.gov/Pages/FAQS/ise-vape.aspx — accessed 28 October 2026. Consequence: a hemp-derived vape liquid sold with a vapor device as one packaged item is within this tax; equipment/devices not sold with a liquid/substance are not; liquids not packaged and sold with vapor equipment are not. Consumer use tax: a use tax at the same 5 cents/ml is imposed on untaxed vapor products used or stored in Wisconsin (§ 139.78(1m)), reported on Form TT-104S. Distributor tax is reported on Form TT-100. Permit link: see Part 4.2 — a tobacco and vapor products distributor permit (Form CTV-200) is required for manufacturers/distributors/subjobbers selling vapor products to Wisconsin customers and for Wisconsin retailers purchasing untaxed vapor products.

Tobacco products tax does not, on the text located, reach hemp flower: the tobacco products tax is imposed on “tobacco products” at 71% of manufacturer’s list price (with a 50-cent-per-cigar cap and 100% for moist snuff) under § 139.76(1). No DOR guidance was located treating hemp flower, hemp pre-rolls, or hemp edibles as “tobacco products.” [Verify — do not assume hemp smokables are tobacco-taxed; equally, do not assume a hemp pre-roll escapes all excise if it is sold as a vapor product.]

2.6 Controlled substances tax stamp law — Wis. Stat. §§ 139.87–139.96

The subchapter is still printed in the statutes. Subchapter IV of ch. 139 is headed “TAX ON CONTROLLED SUBSTANCES.” Source: statute text as indexed — docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2013/statutes/statutes/139/iv — accessed 28 October 2026. [The current-year statute page was not opened in full in this pass; the rates below are from the statute text reproduced in multiple official statute prints.]

§ 139.87(2) — “dealer”: “a person who in violation of ch. 961 possesses, manufactures, produces, ships, transports, delivers, distributes, imports, sells or transfers to another person more than 42.5 grams of material containing tetrahydrocannabinols, more than 5 plants containing tetrahydrocannabinols, more than 14 grams of mushrooms containing psilocin or psilocybin, more than 100 milligrams of any material containing lysergic acid diethylamide, more than 7 grams of any other schedule I or schedule II controlled substance or of a controlled substance analog of a schedule I or schedule II controlled substance or more than 7 grams of ketamine or flunitrazepam. ‘Dealer’ does not include a person who lawfully possesses a controlled substance or controlled substance analog.”
Item — § 139.88Rate
Per gram or part of a gram of material containing THC, whether pure or impure, measured when in the dealer’s possession — § 139.88(1)$3.50
Per plant containing THC, regardless of weight — § 139.88(1d)$1,000
Per gram of mushrooms/parts containing psilocin or psilocybin — § 139.88(1g)$10
Per 100 mg or part thereof of LSD material — § 139.88(1r)$100
Per gram of other Schedule I or II substances — § 139.88(2)$200
Per gram of ketamine or flunitrazepam — § 139.88(3)$200

Mechanics — §§ 139.89–139.90: tax is due immediately upon acquisition or possession; DOR has a lien on the taxpayer’s property at that time; late payments bear interest at 1% per month or part of a month; stamps/evidence of payment are sold at face value and are non-transferable; “No dealer may possess” the substance unless the tax has been paid as evidenced by a stamp; acquisition of stamps “does not create immunity for a dealer from criminal prosecution” (§ 139.90).

Constitutional history — must be stated alongside the rates, because the annotations conflict across vintages: State v. Hall, 207 Wis. 2d 54, 557 N.W.2d 778 (1997): “The drug tax stamp law, ss. 139.87 to 139.96, 1995 stats., is unconstitutional, as it compels self-incrimination without protecting against the derivative use of the information it compels in a criminal proceeding.” State v. Jones, 2002 WI App 196: “Sections 139.87 to 139.96, as amended by 1997 Wis. Act 27 are constitutional.”

Application to hemp — [Verify]: on the face of § 139.87(2), a “dealer” must act “in violation of ch. 961,” and a person who lawfully possesses is excluded. Lawful hemp is excluded from the ch. 961 THC/marijuana definitions (Part 1). No DOR guidance was located in this pass stating whether DOR currently sells tax stamps, assesses this tax on marijuana seizures, or treats any hemp product as within it. Do not state that the stamp tax is actively collected, or that it is dead, without that DOR check. Penalties under § 139.93 and the full §§ 139.91–139.96 text were also not opened in this pass — [Verify]. No stamp-tax revenue figure was located in this pass. Do not quote one.

2.7 IRS § 280E and Wisconsin conformity — NO decoupling statute located

Federal baseline (given, not re-researched): split scheduling — state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; § 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to § 280E.

Wisconsin consequence on the given baseline: Wisconsin has no state-licensed medical marijuana programme (Part 4.1). Marijuana in Wisconsin therefore remains Schedule I for Wisconsin purposes under the split scheduling, and § 280E remains fully applicable to any Wisconsin marijuana activity — which is in any event illegal under state law. This is the direct application of the given baseline, not a separate Wisconsin ruling.

No Wisconsin decoupling law located. A secondary 280E conformity survey lists Wisconsin as “Yes — conforms to IRC § 280E to the extent of conformity to the underlying federal deduction or credit,” citing Wis. Stat. § 71.22(4)(l) and § 71.26(3). Source: Thomson Reuters survey excerpt (2020 run) — accessed 28 October 2026. [Secondary and dated (2020); the § 71.22(4) paragraph letter has since moved. Treat as supporting, not conclusive.]

Wisconsin uses static, not rolling, IRC conformity — and the current conformity date was NOT verified as enacted in this pass: 2025 SB 859 / AB 870 proposal text would provide: for taxable years beginning after 31 Dec 2022 and before 1 Jan 2026, “Internal Revenue Code” means the IRC as amended to 31 Dec 2022; and a new § 71.22(4)(o) would provide, for taxable years beginning after 31 Dec 2025, the IRC as amended to 31 Dec 2025, with listed exclusions. Source: SB 859 proposal text — docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2025/related/proposals/sb859/6/_1 — accessed 28 October 2026. Whether SB 859 / AB 870 was enacted, and what conformity date is in force for 2026 taxable years, was not confirmed in this pass — [Verify]. Do not cite a 2025 conformity date as current law on the basis of a proposal. Interaction question left open [Verify]: because Wisconsin conformity is static, whether any federal Schedule III / § 280E relief for state-licensed medical activity would flow through to Wisconsin income tax — and for which taxable year — depends on (a) the enacted conformity date, (b) whether Wisconsin follows the federal schedule change for state income-tax purposes, and (c) the absence of any Wisconsin-licensed medical activity to which it could apply. No DOR guidance on Schedule III / § 280E was located as of 28 October 2026. A secondary cannabis-tax guide states the 2025 budget proposal “does not decouple” from § 280E, but that is secondary and proposal-specific.

Lawful hemp businesses: hemp that is lawfully produced is not a Schedule I/II controlled substance, so § 280E trafficking disallowance should not apply to a lawful hemp business on the face of § 280E. No DOR ruling or guidance confirming this for Wisconsin hemp retailers/manufacturers was located — [Verify] before advising a specific business.

PART 3 — LOCAL RULES

Critical framing — read first. No local ordinance legalises marijuana in Wisconsin. State law remains in force everywhere in the state. A local ordinance only changes which citation track — municipal/county forfeiture vs state criminal charge — a local officer may use for a first, small-amount possession case. Advisory referendums are not law. Every referendum result below is expressly non-binding / advisory. None changed Wis. Stat. ch. 961, created a licence, or authorised a sale. Same-name warning applied throughout: City of Madison (Dane County) ≠ Town of Madison (Dane County); City of Milwaukee (Milwaukee County) ≠ Milwaukee County; City of Racine (Racine County) ≠ Racine County; City of Kenosha (Kenosha County) ≠ Kenosha County; City of La Crosse (La Crosse County) ≠ La Crosse County; City of Superior (Douglas County) ≠ Superior, CO/NE. Municipality type + county are given for every entry.

3.1 Authority — who may do what, and under what state law

3.1.1 The two specific possession-ordinance grants

Cities, villages and towns — Wis. Stat. § 66.0107(1)(bm):

“Enact and enforce an ordinance to prohibit the possession of 25 grams or less of marijuana, as defined in s. 961.01 (14), subject to the exceptions in s. 961.41 (3g) (intro.), and provide a forfeiture for a violation of the ordinance; except that any person who is charged with possession of more than 25 grams of marijuana, or who is charged with possession of any amount of marijuana following a conviction for possession of marijuana, in this state shall not be prosecuted under this paragraph.”

Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/scroll/up/292/2003/statutes/statutes/66 — accessed 28 October 2026. The same paragraph is also the subject of later, unenacted amendment proposals (e.g. 2023 AB 861 / 2021 AB 812, which would have changed the 25-gram / 14-gram thresholds and added a $250 cap and community-service alternative). Those proposals are not current law.

Counties — Wis. Stat. § 59.54(25):

“The board may enact and enforce an ordinance to prohibit the possession of 25 grams or less of marijuana, as defined in s. 961.01 (14), subject to the exceptions in s. 961.41 (3g) (intro.), and provide a forfeiture for a violation of the ordinance; except that any person who is charged with possession of more than 25 grams of marijuana, or who is charged with possession of any amount of marijuana following a conviction for possession of marijuana, in this state shall not be prosecuted under this subsection. Any ordinance enacted under this subsection does not apply in any municipality that has enacted an ordinance prohibiting the possession of marijuana.”

Source (2001 statutes snapshot as returned by docs.legis.wisconsin.gov): docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/document/statutes/2001/59.55 — accessed 28 October 2026.

[Verify] — county-vs-municipality precedence conflict: 2013 Wisconsin Act 293, which amended both §§ 59.54(25) and 66.0107(1)(bm), is shown in its Act text as creating § 59.54(25)(a)1.–2. and (b) stating: “(b) Any ordinance enacted under this subsection par. (a) applies in every municipality within the county.” Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2013/related/acts/293/2 — accessed 28 October 2026. That wording conflicts on its face with the “does not apply in any municipality that has enacted an ordinance” sentence in the 2001 snapshot above. Do not publish a single precedence rule until the current, consolidated §§ 59.54(25) and 66.0107(1)(bm) text is opened on docs.legis.wisconsin.gov in the Run 1 consolidation pass. The practical consequence in the sources below is consistent regardless: county ordinances are in practice enforced mainly by the county sheriff / in unincorporated areas and in municipalities without their own ordinance, while city police enforce the city ordinance inside city limits.

Synthetic cannabinoids — parallel grants: § 66.0107(1)(bn) (cities/villages/towns) and § 59.54(25g) (counties) authorise ordinances prohibiting possession of a controlled substance specified in § 961.14(4)(tb) to (ty), with the same “decline/dismiss” gate after 2013 Act 293. Source same Act 293 link above. This is the statutory basis for local synthetic/hemp-derived ordinances such as Wood County Ord. 236 (3.4.3), not a general hemp-retail licensing power.

3.1.2 Home rule vs limited county/town power

  • Cities and villages only have constitutional home rule: Wis. Const. art. XI, § 3(1) — “Cities and villages organized pursuant to state law may determine their local affairs and government, subject only to this constitution and to such enactments of the legislature of statewide concern as with uniformity shall affect every city or every village.” Corroborated: “Only cities and villages are granted constitutional home rule authority. Other units of local government, such as counties, towns and school districts, have administrative home rule authority pursuant to statute. See, e.g., Wis. Stat. § 59.03. A county’s statutory home rule authority is limited.” — State ex rel. Teunas v. Kenosha County, 142 Wis. 2d 498 (1988), as quoted in a secondary case summary — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Counties therefore need an express grant — §§ 59.54(25), 59.54(25g), 59.69 (county zoning) — and cannot invent a marijuana or hemp licensing scheme on home-rule grounds alone.
  • Towns have no constitutional home rule. A town board may exercise village powers only if granted under Wis. Stat. § 60.10(2)(c), and then may zone under § 61.35 via § 60.62. A town can enact a § 66.0107(1)(bm) possession ordinance, because that grant expressly says “board or council of any town, village or city.”
  • General forfeiture / imprisonment limit: Wis. Stat. § 66.0109 — “If a statute requires that the penalty under any county or municipal ordinance conform to the penalty provided by statute the ordinance may impose only a forfeiture and may provide for imprisonment if the forfeiture is not paid.” [Verify] whether any current local possession ordinance still relies on imprisonment for non-payment: Milwaukee’s 2021 proposal expressly repealed it (3.2.2); other cities’ deposit schedules must be checked individually.
  • Ordinance actions are civil: Wis. Stat. § 66.0114(1)(a) — “An action for violation of an ordinance or bylaw enacted by a city, village, town sanitary district or public inland lake protection and rehabilitation district is a civil action.” This is why a municipal ticket is not a criminal conviction — but it does not prevent a separate state criminal charge for the same conduct if the district attorney elects to prosecute under ch. 961 (subject to the §§ 59.54(25)/66.0107(1)(bm) bars above).
LevelPossession ordinance?Hemp/CBD/smoke-vape shop regulation?Zoning?
StateCriminal law, ch. 961; §§ 59.54(25)/66.0107(1)(bm) grantsDATCP hemp programme (state pilot, now USDA — Part 7); no state hemp-retail licence foundState shoreland/floodplain/farmland floors
CountyYes, § 59.54(25), ≤25 g, first offence only; enforced principally by sheriff / in areas without a municipal ordinance — precedence [Verify]Yes, by ordinance where a specific grant exists (e.g. hemp-derived age-21 ordinances in Dane County, Wood County — 3.4). No county hemp licence scheme locatedYes, § 59.69, in unincorporated areas / towns that have not adopted their own zoning
City / VillageYes, § 66.0107(1)(bm), ≤25 g, first offence only; city police enforce city ordinance inside limitsYes, under home rule + police power: age-21 sales bans, display/signage, buffers, general business licensing, smoking bans (Madison § 23.202 — 3.4.2). No city may legalise marijuana sale/cultivationYes, § 62.23(7) for cities; § 61.35 (incorporating § 62.23) for villages
TownYes, § 66.0107(1)(bm)Only if village powers granted (§ 60.10(2)(c)); otherwise county rules / state law governOnly under § 60.61 (if no county zoning) or § 60.62 (if village powers granted), subject to county approval where county zoning exists

3.1.3 Interaction with state law, sheriff and State Patrol

  1. State law is not repealed by a local ordinance. Madison’s own 2020 drafter’s analysis states it expressly: “While it is currently a violation of state statute and federal law to possess or consume cannabis or cannabis derivatives in the City, at the direction of the Dane County District Attorney’s Office, the Madison Police Department would not refer charges for cases that only involve possession of less than 28 grams of cannabis.” Source (official City of Madison Legistar File 62417): madison.legistar.com — File 62417 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  2. Prosecutorial discretion is the real gate, not the ordinance text alone. Dane County DA non-prosecution of simple possession <25 g was announced in 2007 and is still reflected in the Madison Police SOP eff. 10 August 2026 (3.2.1). Milwaukee County DA and City of Milwaukee practice are separate — a city ticket does not bind the DA.
  3. Sheriff / State Patrol are not bound by a city ordinance. A county sheriff’s deputy enforcing a county ordinance, or a Wisconsin State Patrol trooper enforcing state law, may cite/arrest under state law even inside a “$1 city.” This is stated in a secondary advocacy summary and is consistent with §§ 59.54(25)/66.0107(1)(bm): the county change “will actually only lower the fines issued by Milwaukee County Sheriff’s Deputies. Most arrests in Milwaukee County are issued by local police.” Mark the sheriff/State Patrol sentence as practice, not as a statute — no statute was located in this pass that expressly exempts sheriff/State Patrol from a city ordinance; the exemption follows from the fact that they enforce state/county law, not city law.
  4. Second and subsequent possession, >25 g (city/county track) / >28 g (Madison track), intent to deliver, cultivation and sale are outside every local ordinance below and remain state felonies/misdemeanours under ch. 961. Every city entry below is first-offence, small-amount only unless it expressly says otherwise.
  5. Court costs / surcharges are additional to the forfeiture in every entry. E.g. Milwaukee County: $1 forfeiture → “about $140” total with state-required costs (2021 fiscal analysis, secondary reporting of a county document); Green Bay: $0 forfeiture + $61 court costs; Eau Claire: $1 → ~$140–$148 total in contemporaneous reports. Do not quote a $1 or $0 figure as the total amount a person pays.

3.2 Local possession ordinances — jurisdiction by jurisdiction

Reading note on “current” fines: where the Municode / Legistar / council-minutes text could be opened, the fine is labelled OFFICIAL. Where only a 2015 Gannett table, NORML list, or 2021 WISCOCAN compilation could be found, the fine is labelled SECONDARY — VERIFY CURRENT TEXT. The 2015 Gannett table is 11 years old; it must not be republished as current without a Municode check. Its figures are reproduced in 3.2.16 for audit trail only.

3.2.1 City of Madison, Dane County — OFFICIAL, most detailed in the state

Current ordinance: Madison General Ordinances (MGO) § 23.20, “Regulations Concerning Cannabis,” as repealed and recreated by ORD-20-00109, adopted 17 November 2020 (Common Council, File 62417). Legistar history: 30 Sept 2020 referred; 14 Oct 2020 Public Safety Review Committee recommend adopt; 17 Nov 2020 Common Council Adopt, Pass. Source: madison.legistar.com — File 62417 — accessed 28 October 2026. Municode Ch. 23 definitions (crawled 22 Sept 2026, showing ORD-20-00109): “Cannabis” and “Cannabis derivative” (>0.3% delta-9 THC) definitions, excluding hemp and synthetic chemical cannabinoids.

“(2) Possession. A person who is eighteen (18) years of age or older may possess cannabis or cannabis derivatives in an amount not to exceed twenty-eight (28) grams on public property with the permission of the property owner, landlord or tenant or on private property. Exceptions: (a) Cannabis or cannabis derivatives may not be possessed in an amount greater than twenty-eight (28) grams on public or private property. (b) Cannabis or cannabis derivatives may not be possessed within one thousand (1,000) feet of any private or public school premise. (c) Cannabis or cannabis derivatives may not be possessed on a school bus.”
“(3) Consumption. A person who is eighteen (18) years of age or older may consume cannabis or cannabis derivatives on private property with the permission of a person who is lawfully on the property or on public property with permission of the owner, landlord or tenant. No person may consume cannabis or cannabis derivatives in a motor vehicle which is in operation. No person may consume cannabis or cannabis derivatives in violation of MGO Sec. 23.05 or Wis. Stats. Sec. 346.63.”
“(4) Prescription. Any person who has obtained cannabis or cannabis derivatives directly from or pursuant to a valid prescription or order of a practitioner while acting in the course of the practitioner’s professional practice may possess cannabis or cannabis derivatives on public or private property and may consume cannabis or cannabis derivatives on private property with the permission of a person who is lawfully on the property or on public property with the permission of the owner, landlord or tenant.”
“(5) A violation of this Section shall be subject to a forfeiture of up to one hundred dollars ($100).”

How the $1 figure arises — do not conflate with the $100 statutory maximum: the $100 is the ordinance maximum. The bail/deposit schedule in MGO § 1.08(3)(a), as amended by the same File 62417, sets the deposit actually charged. Secondary reporting of the 2020 change consistently describes the practical result as a $1 deposit/fine for the listed violations (possession >28 g, school-zone / school-bus possession, consumption without permission, consumption in an operating vehicle, smoking where smoking is prohibited). The City’s own Police SOP (eff. 10 Aug 2026) lists the prohibited circumstances, not the dollar deposit:

“Madison General Ordinance 23.20 prohibits possession of marijuana under certain circumstances: • Possession of more than twenty-eight (28) grams of marijuana • Possession of marijuana (any amount) by someone under the age of eighteen • Possession of marijuana (any amount) within 1,000 feet of a school • Possession of marijuana (any amount) on a school bus • Possession of marijuana (any amount) on property open to the public without the permission of the property owner, landlord, or tenant • Smoking marijuana anywhere smoking is prohibited (bars, restaurants, etc.) • Consuming marijuana in a motor vehicle which is in operation While simple possession of marijuana remains illegal under state law, the Dane County District Attorney’s Office will not prosecute simple possession cases unless extenuating circumstances are present”

Source (official City of Madison Police SOP): cityofmadison.com — Enforcement of Marijuana Laws SOP — accessed 28 October 2026. SOP enforcement rule: “Officers may only issue City ordinance citations for marijuana-related offenses consistent with ordinance changes as described above. State criminal charges related to marijuana should only result in arrest or referral [for] Delivery or possession with intent to deliver [or] Simple possession when a significant quantity (50+ grams) is located, and when there is another criminal charge being referred or when the possession is directly related to behavior impacting public safety.” Marijuana-related paraphernalia is excluded from the city paraphernalia ordinance and the DA will not prosecute those cases, per the same SOP.

History: Madison first decriminalised in 1977 — casual possession in a private place (≤28 g cannabis / ≤112 g marijuana under the pre-2020 text) “is not a crime and is not subject to forfeiture,” public-place casual possession subject to “a forfeiture of up to one hundred dollars ($100)” (Ord. 9244, 14 Aug 1987, as reproduced in File 62417’s “currently reads” note). The 1977 ordinance is therefore superseded by the 2020 text for current purposes; do not quote the 112-gram figure as current. Town of Madison distinction: the Town of Madison is a separate town in Dane County. No Town of Madison possession ordinance was located in this pass — [Verify] before attributing any Madison figure to the Town.

3.2.2 City of Milwaukee, Milwaukee County AND Milwaukee County (separate entries — do not merge)

A. City of Milwaukee (1st-class city, Milwaukee County) — Milwaukee Code of Ordinances § 106-38. Pre-2021: possession ≤25 g: forfeiture $0–$50; smoking/use in public: $250–$500; imprisonment on default; community service / substance-abuse education in lieu. Source (official Legistar File 201518, analysis): milwaukee.legistar.com — File 201518 — accessed 28 October 2026. 2021 proposal (File 201518, Ald. Lewis, referred 23 Mar 2021): “This ordinance reduces the penalty for possession of up to 25 grams of marijuana from 0 - $50 to $1. In addition, the ordinance reduces the penalty for smoking marijuana in public from not less than $250 and not more than $500, to not more than $1. It further repeals imprisonment as a penalty for default of payment. The ordinance also repeals community service and substance abuse education as options in lieu of payment of forfeiture.” Verbatim proposed recreated penalty: “Part 1. Section 106-38-3 of the code is repealed and recreated to read: 106-38. Possession of Marijuana. 3. PENALTY. Any person violating this section shall upon conviction forfeit no more than $1.” [Verify] STATUS: the Legistar history shown for File 201518 ends “7/7/2021 COMMON COUNCIL PLACED ON FILE Pass 15:0” and “6/24/2021 PUBLIC SAFETY AND HEALTH COMMITTEE RECOMMENDED FOR PLACING ON FILE Pass 5:0.” “Placed on file” in Milwaukee Legistar is not the same as “adopted/enacted” in the Madison record. Do not state as fact that the City of Milwaukee $1 ordinance is in force until the current § 106-38 text is opened on Municode / Milwaukee Code. Secondary sources after 2021 variously describe the city fine as $50 (pre-2021) or $1 (proposal) — that conflict is the reason for the Verify flag. The county $1 change below is separate and is not evidence for the city. Earlier history (secondary): city ordinance since May 1997; 2015 reduction to $50 maximum for ≤25 g.

B. Milwaukee County — Milwaukee County Code Ch. 24, §§ 24.01–24.02 (as cited in secondary sources). March 2021 change, 16–1 vote, signed by County Executive David Crowley: county forfeiture for possession of 25 g or less reduced to “no more than $1,” down from a $275 forfeiture (total $484 with costs, per the resolution; fiscal analysis put the new total at about $140 with state-required costs). The change also covered possession/delivery of drug paraphernalia. Secondary reporting of the official vote — accessed 28 October 2026 — and Badger Institute footnote (secondary, citing the Code as of 2023): “MILWAUKEE COUNTY, WIS., Possession and Use of Marijuana, Ordinances # 24.01, 24.02 (2023) (reducing fine to no more than $1).” Source: badgerinstitute.org — accessed 28 October 2026. Official text NOT opened in this pass — the county Code page / board resolution PDF was not returned as an openable official text. [Verify] current §§ 24.01–24.02 text on the county’s code publisher before Run 1 publication. The $1 figure is nevertheless treated as established by two independent secondary reports of the official vote plus a 2023 Code citation, but it is not labelled OFFICIAL here. Interaction: county ordinance is enforced by the Milwaukee County Sheriff; Milwaukee city police enforce the city ordinance inside the City of Milwaukee. A person in the City of Milwaukee may therefore receive a city, county, or state citation depending on the officer.

3.2.3 Dane County (county, not the City of Madison) — VERIFY / NO ORDINANCE TEXT LOCATED

No Dane County Code possession-ordinance text was opened in this pass. Secondary claims exist that “In 2015, Dane County made a change from $1000 fine to $1 fine for possession of arbitrarily small amounts of cannabis for first time offenders” and that the Dane County DA declined simple-possession prosecutions <25 g from 2007 (county ordinance fine then described as “up to $310”). What is official and current: (i) the Madison Police SOP above stating the Dane County DA “will not prosecute simple possession cases unless extenuating circumstances are present”; (ii) Dane County’s 2025 hemp-derived cannabinoid age-21 ordinance amendment, 2025 OA-016, adopted by the County Board 20 Nov 2025 (3.4.2) — that is a sales ordinance, not a possession ordinance. Do not publish a Dane County possession fine ($1, $310, or any other figure) as current law on this pass alone.

3.2.4 City of Eau Claire, Eau Claire County — SECONDARY, strong corroboration

November 2018: City Council voted 8–2 to reduce the fine from $300 to $1 for first possession; final cost with court fees ~$140, vs. ~$500 previously; second possession remains a state felony (as described by the council president in contemporaneous reporting). Compilation citation: “Eau Claire: $1 (Eau Claire Ordinances § 1.24.030 Cn. 1.)” — secondary: wiscocan.org, Feb 2021 — accessed 28 October 2026. Threshold / repeat: 25 g in the 2015 table (3.2.16); the 2018 reports do not restate a gram threshold. [Verify] current § 1.24.030 text and threshold on Municode / City of Eau Claire code. Eau Claire County is separate: it had a 2018 advisory referendum (3.3) and a 2022 advisory referendum (69% yes, 3.3.3). No Eau Claire County possession-ordinance fine was located — [Verify].

3.2.5 City of Green Bay, Brown County — SECONDARY, with official-council corroboration via news reports

1–2 March 2022, unanimous Common Council vote (first reading 1 Mar, second reading 15 Mar, effective on publication “within a few days” of second reading): fine for possession of 28 g or less reduced to $0, plus $61 court costs; same $0 + costs for consumption in a private space; possession of marijuana paraphernalia aligned to the possession penalty (previously the same as other drug paraphernalia). Sources (secondary, TMJ4 reporting the council action) — accessed 28 October 2026. What did NOT change in 2022: consumption in public and possession of >28 g remain punishable by a maximum citation of $500 (TMJ4 summary) / $1–$500 for public consumption (other secondary summaries — range [Verify]). Under-21: maximum $100 citation ($187 with costs) in the TMJ4 summary; a later local summary gives $100–$200 first offence, rising on subsequent offences — under-21 range [Verify], do not publish a single figure. Prior figure: maximum $500 + costs (typically $691 total) before March 2022. Green Bay Code citation in the 2021 compilation: “Green Bay Ordinances § 27.902(2)” — secondary, WISCOCAN Feb 2021. [Verify] current § 27.902 text on Municode.

3.3 Advisory referendums — every county and municipal question located

Legal status of all of the following: advisory / non-binding only. The Wisconsin Elections Commission (WEC) does not certify local advisory questions as state law; county clerks canvass them as local questions. Where a WEC county-by-county canvass PDF was returned, it was for a statewide referendum, not a local cannabis question — it is not cited as a cannabis result. For local questions, the official source is the county/municipal clerk’s canvass; where that canvass was not opened in this pass, the result is labelled SECONDARY — clerk canvass [Verify], with the percentage and, where available, raw votes from contemporaneous reporting. Turnout is given only where a source states it; otherwise it is marked [Unavailable] — it is not estimated from population. No statewide citizen initiative exists in Wisconsin — this is Part 9 territory, but it explains why every question below is advisory: only the Legislature can place a binding statewide question (constitutional amendment) or enact a law; a county board / common council may place an advisory question on its own ballot.

3.3.1 Before 2018 — context

  • Dane County, Nov 2010: medical marijuana advisory referendum — 159,408 Yes, 75.49% Yes (3-to-1). Sister referendum in the City of River Falls, Pierce + St. Croix Counties: 68% Yes. Source (secondary, WI NORML contemporary report) — accessed 28 October 2026. Clerk canvass [Verify].
  • Dane County, 2014: adult-use advisory — 64.5% Yes (secondary summary of the Dane series 2010/2014/2018 ranging 64.5%–76.4%). Question text and raw votes [Verify].
  • Menominee Indian Reservation / Menominee County tribal vote, Aug 2015 is tribal, not a Wisconsin county/municipal advisory referendum — it belongs to Part 10 and is not counted here.

3.4 Local zoning, moratoriums, caps/bans, and licence/permit fees for hemp, CBD and smoke/vape shops

3.4.1 Zoning authority — the statutes (general — not cannabis-specific)

  • Cities: Wis. Stat. § 62.23(7) — city zoning (districts, uses, setbacks, conditional uses). Villages act under § 62.23 via § 61.35.
  • Villages: § 61.35 incorporates § 62.23.
  • Towns: § 60.61 (town zoning where no county zoning ordinance exists, subject to §§ 60.61(3), (3m)) and § 60.62 (town with village powers under § 60.10(2)(c) may zone under § 61.35; where county zoning exists, town zoning / amendments need town-meeting or referendum approval and county-board approval, subject to the § 60.225 revocation exceptions).
  • Counties: § 59.69 (county zoning in unincorporated areas).
  • Extraterritorial zoning: § 62.23(7a) — within 3 miles of a 1st/2nd/3rd-class city, 1½ miles of a 4th-class city or village, via a joint extraterritorial committee (3 city/village + 3 town members per town).

Source for the zoning map above (secondary, UW–Extension Land Use Training, summarising the statutes): fyi.extension.wisc.edu — Types of Zoning — accessed 28 October 2026. Practical consequence for hemp/CBD/smoke shops: in the absence of a state hemp-retail licence, a shop’s local status is determined by (a) the zoning district’s permitted / conditional-use list (usually “retail sales” — a smoke/vape shop is not a separately zoned use unless the local code creates one), (b) any specific hemp-derived ordinance (3.4.2–3.4.3), (c) general business licensing / occupancy, and (d) state tobacco/vape retailer licensing where the product is a tobacco/vape product (Part 4 — no local tobacco-licence fee schedule was verified in this pass). A city/village can create a specific “tobacco / vape / hemp retailer” zoning or licensing category by ordinance under home rule, and can impose distance buffers — Madison and Wood County have done so for hemp-derived products (below) — but no statewide local cap, moratorium list, or ban list was located.

3.4.2 Madison + Dane County — hemp-derived cannabinoid age-21, display and buffer rules (2025) — OFFICIAL

City of Madison, MGO § 23.202, “Age Restrictions on Hemp-Derived THC Products,” Legistar File 90169, adopted 25 Nov 2025 (substitute passed 13–5; mayoral alternate removing display + buffer provisions failed).

Drafter’s analysis (official Legistar attachment): “The State of Wisconsin adopted the hemp exemptions contained in the 2018 Federal Farm Bill, which contained loopholes allowing for an unregulated market in the sale of psychoactive substances. The state legislature has not taken action to close the loopholes related to the sale of psychoactive substances or to regulate their sale. The City of Madison has seen a proliferation of unregulated businesses selling a variety of these substances and finds it necessary to restrict their sales in order to safeguard those under the age of 21 from these unregulated intoxicating products.”

Source: legistar2.granicus.com — Madison File 90169 attachment — accessed 28 October 2026.

  • Definition: “‘hemp-derived cannabinoid’ means either of the following: (a) A cannabinoid other than delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) or an isomer derived from such cannabinoid (delta-8 THC, delta-10 THC, hexahydrocannabinol (HHC), HHC-O, THCA, THC-O, THCP, THCV). (b) A hemp-derived product containing delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol in a concentration of 0.3 percent or less.” — “shall not mean any non-intoxicating cannabinoids, including cannabidiol (CBD).”
  • Age ban: “No person and no hemp-derived cannabinoid establishment… may sell, deliver, or provide any hemp-derived cannabinoid product to any person under the age of twenty-one (21).” Age must be verified “with a valid federal, state, Tribal, or municipal issued photo identification,” “performed by a person and shall not be automated.” Sale must be refused if the seller believes / has reason to believe the ultimate recipient is under 21 or using a forged/fake ID.
  • Signage/display: retailer “shall conspicuously post a sign in each area within their premises where any hemp-derived cannabinoid products are sold to consumers stating that the sale of any such product to any person under the age of twenty-one (21) is unlawful.” Products “shall be displayed from behind a sales or service counter so that no person may access it without assistance by an establishment employee.” Beverages may be in a cooler not behind the counter, subject to the same age verification; single cans/bottles may not be stored/displayed outside an enclosed cooler except in sealed cases/cartons/multipacks. Vending-machine sale is prohibited.
  • Buffer (new retailers only): “Establishments selling hemp-derived cannabinoids shall not be located within ¼ mile or 1,320 feet of a youth-serving organization and location such as, but not limited to: childcare centers, preschools, public or parochial schools, tribal schools, playgrounds, city or county parks, sporting arenas, or organizations with specific interest to serve children (Boys & Girls Club, YMCA, Head Start, etc.). The distance shall be measured by the shortest route along a designated roadway or walking path from the main entrance of the youth-serving business/organization to the premises selling the hemp-derived cannabinoid products.” Grandfather clause in the opened draft: “does not apply to businesses selling hemp-derived cannabinoids prior to November 1, 2025 January 1, 2026” — the strike-through shows the date was moved during amendment; final grandfather date [Verify] on the enrolled ordinance.
  • Penalties: sale to under-21 — first violation in 12 months $100–$500, second+ $250–$750 (deposit schedule in the same file lists $250 / $500 deposits — forfeiture-vs-deposit distinction [Verify]); signage violation $200–$500; repeated violation is a public nuisance abatable by injunction in Dane County Circuit Court by the City Attorney. Enforcement: Director of Public Health Madison and Dane County or designee + Police Department.
  • This is not a licence: § 23.202 creates no retailer licence, fee, cap, or moratorium. It is an age/display/location prohibition enforced by forfeiture. Further licensing work was described as a possible next step, not enacted in File 90169.

Dane County, 2025 OA-016, “Amending Chapters 2 & 34 of the Dane County Code of Ordinances, Regarding the Prohibition on the Sale of Cannabinoid Products to Persons Under 21 Years of Age” — adopted by the County Board 20 Nov 2025. Source (official Dane County Board minutes, 20 Nov 2025): legistar2.granicus.com — Dane County Board Minutes 20 Nov 2025 — accessed 28 October 2026: “A motion was made by ROSE, seconded by FURMAN, that the Ordinance be adopted Sub.2 (Proposed ROSE). The motion carried by a voice vote.” Text NOT opened in this pass — chapter/section numbers, buffer, display, penalty and geographic scope (unincorporated areas vs. countywide) are [Verify]. Do not copy Madison’s buffer/penalty figures onto Dane County. A proposal to raise the age to 25 failed 1–5 in committee.

3.4.3 Wood County — hemp-derived cannabinoid ordinance (Ord. 236) — OFFICIAL COUNTY PDF

Wood County Ordinance #236, “Prohibition of Possession of Marijuana, Synthetic Cannabinoids and Drug Paraphernalia” (as amended for hemp-derived cannabinoids): definition mirrors Madison’s: “A cannabinoid other than delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), or an isomer derived from such cannabinoid (delta-8 THC, delta-10 THC, hexahydrocannabinol (HHC), HHC-O, THCA, THC-O, THCP, THCV); or A hemp-derived product containing delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol in a concentration of 0.3 percent or less. Does not include non-intoxicating cannabinoids, including cannabidiol (CBD).” “It shall be illegal for a person under the age of twenty-one (21) to possess or use any amount of a hemp-derived cannabinoid… except as specifically allowed by Wisconsin law.” “It shall be illegal to sell or deliver any hemp-derived cannabinoid product… to a person under the age of twenty-one years, except as specifically allowed by Wisconsin law,” and illegal to sell/deliver “without having first verified their age by having the purchaser present a valid photo identification.” 750-foot buffer: “Hemp-derived cannabinoids shall not be sold within 750 feet of a hospital, church, or youth-serving organization such as, but not limited to: childcare centers, preschools, public or parochial schools, tribal schools, playgrounds, city or county parks, sporting arenas, or organizations with specific interest to serve children (Boys & Girls Club, YMCA, Head Start, etc.) The distance shall be measured by the shortest route along a designated roadway or walking path from the main entrance of the youth-serving business/organization to the premises selling hemp-derived cannabinoid products. The prohibition in this section does not apply to businesses selling hemp-derived cannabinoids from a location used for said sales on December 1, 2022.” Penalty: “The penalty provision under s. 236.04 shall apply to any violation of this subsection.” Effective “upon passage and publication.” Source (official Wood County Clerk ordinance PDF): co.wood.wi.us — Ordinance 236 PDF — accessed 28 October 2026. Adoption date of the hemp-derived amendment: not stated in the opened PDF excerpt — [Verify]. The December 1, 2022 grandfather date suggests the hemp provisions date from late 2022, but that is inference, not a stated adoption date — flag it as such.

3.4.4 City of Mauston, Juneau County — synthetic + hemp-derived ordinance (2026 file) — OFFICIAL PDF, DATE VERIFY

City of Mauston Ordinance No. 2026-2084, adding § 26-8, “Prohibition of Possession of Synthetic Cannabinoids,” Ch. 26, Art. I: definitions incorporate Wis. Stat. § 961.14(4)(tb) synthetics/analogs, plus a “HEMP-DERIVED CANNABINOID REGULATIONS” subsection defining hemp-derived cannabinoid in the same delta-8/delta-10/HHC/HHC-O/THCA/THC-O/THCP terms, excluding CBD. Source (official City of Mauston meeting attachment PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026. Full operative text (age, buffer, penalty) was not in the opened excerpt — [Verify]. The “2026-” number and the file’s 2026 context are as labelled in the source; do not backdate it to 2022–2025 without the adopted ordinance.

3.4.5 Moratoriums, caps, bans and local licence/permit fees — what was NOT found

  • No local moratorium, cap (numerical limit on shops), or outright ban on hemp/CBD/smoke/vape shops was located in an official ordinance text in this pass for any of the 15 named jurisdictions, except the new-retailer buffers in Madison (§ 23.202, ¼ mile) and Wood County (750 ft) above, which are location restrictions, not caps or bans.
  • No local hemp-retailer licence or permit fee schedule was located for Madison, Dane County, Milwaukee, or any other named jurisdiction in this pass. Madison § 23.202 and Dane County OA-016 impose prohibitions enforced by forfeiture; they do not create a licence to apply for or a fee to pay. A Municode search for “tobacco retailer,” “vape,” “smoke shop,” “hemp retailer” licensing chapters in each named city is still required — general tobacco retailer licensing under state law (Part 4) must not be presented as a hemp licence.
  • State-level proposals that would have created local roles are NOT local law and are not counted here: e.g. 2025 SB 681 (hemp-derived cannabinoid licensing, municipal approval / hours provisions) — proposal text: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2025/related/proposals/sb681/212/_348 — accessed 28 October 2026 — is a pending bill at the time, now failed, not an ordinance. Do not attribute its municipal-approval or closing-hours rules to any city or county.
  • Smoking bans: Madison MGO § 23.05 (smoking prohibited in certain public areas) applies to cannabis smoking via § 23.20(3) — see 3.2.1. No separate local smoke/vape-shop smoking-lounge ordinance was verified in this pass.

3.5 Official compiled list of local ordinances — ANSWER: NONE FOUND

No official state-compiled list of local marijuana / hemp ordinances was located. The Wisconsin State Bar’s municipal-ordinance research guide (secondary, State Bar) states the practical position: ordinances are “Organized alphabetically by municipality” and “If you are looking for ordinances for a municipality that has not posted them online, the best starting point is to contact the local municipal clerk.” Source: wisbar.org — InsideTrack — accessed 28 October 2026. The League of Wisconsin Municipalities is a member organisation with sample ordinances and legal opinions, much of it members-only — it is not a public compiled list of adopted local cannabis ordinances on the evidence opened.

Jurisdictions checked in this pass (code / Legistar / clerk / council sources searched or opened): State (docs.legis.wisconsin.gov §§ 59.54, 66.0107, 66.0109, 66.0114); City of Madison + Madison Police (Municode Ch. 23, Legistar 62417, 90169, SOP); City of Milwaukee (Legistar 201518); Milwaukee County (secondary vote reports only — county code not opened); Dane County (Board minutes 2025 OA-016; possession code not opened); City of Eau Claire (secondary only); City of Green Bay (secondary only); City of Appleton (official resolution PDF + secondary amendment); City of La Crosse (secondary) + La Crosse County (official minutes PDF); City of Stevens Point (secondary); City of Superior (secondary list only); City of Racine (Legistar minutes + secondary compilation); City of Kenosha (Granicus official paraphernalia text + secondary possession); City of Wausau (Municode PDF excerpt + secondary); City of Oshkosh (official proceedings excerpt + secondary); City of Janesville (secondary only); Rock County, Manitowoc County, Oconto County, Wood County (official ordinance PDF), City of Monona (agenda packet), City of Fitchburg, City of Middleton, City of Waukesha, City of Marshfield, City of Sturgeon Bay, Village of Egg Harbor, City of Mauston (official PDF), Village of Shorewood, City of West Allis, City of Wauwatosa (secondary mentions only).

The best non-official compilations located — and their stated limits — are: (i) WISCOCAN, Feb 2021: “This list may not be exhaustive because there is no definitive source to search and compare municipal codes” — wiscocan.org, Feb 2021 — accessed 28 October 2026; (ii) NORML Wisconsin Local Decriminalization list — norml.org — accessed 28 October 2026; (iii) Wisconsin Policy Forum, “Marijuana in Milwaukee” (2019) and “A Clear-Headed Look at Marijuana Policy” — accessed 28 October 2026. All three are secondary and, for the Policy Forum, dated 2019 — they must not be cited as official compilations.

3.6 Business concentration — only what official sources say

No official source located in this pass states where hemp/CBD/smoke-vape businesses are concentrated in Wisconsin, how many there are by city/county, or any ranking/cluster. The only official-source statement on concentration located is Madison’s drafter’s analysis for File 90169: “The City of Madison has seen a proliferation of unregulated businesses selling a variety of these substances” — source: Madison File 90169 attachment, above — accessed 28 October 2026. That is a qualitative finding about Madison, with no count, no comparison to other cities, and no cluster ranking. It must not be expanded into “Madison has the most shops” or any league table. No DATCP, Department of Revenue, county, or city business-licence count by locality for hemp/CBD/smoke shops was opened in this pass. Licence-source inventory belongs to Part 5; concentration must not be inferred from referendum votes, ordinance counts, or news anecdotes. Therefore, per the task rule, this section makes no estimate and no ranking. If Part 6 needs a market figure, it must come from an official count or a dated secondary figure labelled as such — not from this Part 3 pass.

PART 4 — LICENCE TYPES

4.1 State cannabis licences — NONE ISSUED

Wisconsin issues no state cannabis licences — no medical licence, no adult-use licence, no cannabis dispensary, grower, processor, or testing-lab licence exists in current law. Basis: (a) marijuana remains illegal for medical and adult-use purposes under ch. 961 (Part 1); (b) every medical/adult-use licensing bill located in the 2025–26 session failed (AB 547/SB 534, AB 50/SB 45 budget provisions, SB 1045 — see 4.3); (c) DATCP’s only cannabis-related licensing was the hemp programme, discontinued 1 Jan 2022 (4.2). Do not confuse the following with a Wisconsin cannabis licence: a USDA hemp producer licence (federal, for hemp only); a DOR seller’s permit (general tax registration); a DATCP food licence (general food safety); a DOR tobacco/vapor permit (general vapor/tobacco); or a DOR electronic vaping device directory listing (device certification, not a cannabis licence).

4.2 Current licences / permits / registrations needed to make, distribute, or sell hemp-derived products

There is no hemp-specific state licence to make, distribute, or sell hemp-derived products in force. What applies is a patchwork of general licences that bite because of what the business does (sells taxable goods, makes food, sells vapor devices, sells alcohol), not because the product contains hemp. The historic DATCP hemp processor licence is gone.

(a) Hemp cultivation — federal only since 1 Jan 2022 (full detail belongs to Part 7)

“Beginning January 1, 2022, the DATCP hemp program is discontinued. … Beginning January 1, 2022, Wisconsin hemp growers are licensed by the U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA).” Source: DATCP, Wisconsin Hemp Resources — hemp.wi.gov — accessed 28 October 2026. “Starting January 1, 2022, hemp growers will transition to the federal-run program, but hemp processors will no longer need a DATCP license to process hemp. Hemp processors will remain under DATCP’s current authority for consumer and food products.” Source: DATCP press release, 2 Sept 2021 — datcp.wi.gov — 2 Sept 2021 Hemp Transition — accessed 28 October 2026. Federal benefits stated by DATCP at transition: “no licensing fees, the federal license is three years instead of annual.” Historic DATCP fees (for the record only — NOT current): under the former ATCP 22 programme, grower licence one-time $150 (0–30 acres), $5/acre (31–199 acres), $1,000 (200+ acres); processor licence one-time $150; grower annual registration $350; processor annual registration $100 (waived first year); sampling/testing $250/sample. These figures are from a secondary reproduction of the former DATCP application guidance and are superseded — do not quote them as current fees.

(b) Selling hemp products at retail — NO DATCP hemp retail licence; DOR seller’s permit is the general requirement

Former DATCP FAQ position (still the best statement located, but historic): “No license currently is needed from the industrial hemp program to sell products made from industrial hemp, including CBD oil. However, licenses from other programs or departments may be required. For example, all retailers will need a sellers permit from the Wisconsin Department of Revenue.” Source: DATCP hemp FAQ as reproduced in a municipal meeting packet — accessed 28 October 2026. [Secondary reproduction of a DATCP FAQ; the underlying DATCP FAQ page was not re-opened in this pass — Verify against current DATCP guidance, but the position is consistent with the 2021 transition release that processors no longer need a DATCP licence.]

  • DOR seller’s permit — current, general: “A seller’s permit is required for every individual, partnership, corporation, or other organization making retail sales, leases, or rentals of taxable products or providing taxable services in Wisconsin, unless all sales are exempt from sales or use tax.” “NOTE: A seller’s permit will not be issued to wholesalers, manufacturers and other businesses if they have no taxable sales.” “Before or after the Department of Revenue issues you a seller’s permit, you may be required to make a security deposit of up to $15,000. If you do not make a security deposit as requested, the department may refuse to issue you a seller’s permit or may revoke your permit.” Security is most commonly requested where there is a history of delinquent taxes; it is returned after 24 consecutive months of compliance (Wis. Admin. Code Tax 11.925). Application via the Wisconsin One Stop Business Portal (business tax registration) or directly with DOR; FEIN/SSN/ITIN required; reviewed the next business day in most cases; permit packet within 5–7 business days. A seller’s permit is non-transferable and must be displayed at the place of business (or carried if no fixed location). Source: Wisconsin One Stop Business Portal — onestop.wi.gov/OpenMyBusiness — accessed 28 October 2026. Fee for a seller’s permit itself: none stated on the One Stop page opened — [Verify]. Do not quote a seller’s-permit fee.
  • No statewide general business licence was located beyond entity registration (DFI) + tax registration (DOR) + any sector licence below. Local (city/village) licences vary — see Part 3.

(c) Making hemp food / beverages — DATCP food licences (general, not hemp-specific)

  • Food processing plant licence (wholesale) — Wis. Stat. § 97.29, Wis. Admin. Code ATCP 70. DATCP’s current Food, Dairy, and Retail Food Licenses page lists “Food Processing Plant License (Wholesale)” with a linked Fee Schedule, statute § 97.29, and rule ATCP 70. Source: DATCP Food Licenses page — datcp.wi.gov — Food Licenses — accessed 28 October 2026. Current fee: NOT extracted in this pass. The DATCP page links to a Fee Schedule but does not print the dollar amount in the text fetched; an older rule proposal showed $78–$685 annual (based on size/type) plus a $261 canning surcharge at $25,000+ production, with reinspection $49–$431. Do not quote the older range as current — [Verify] from the current DATCP Fee Schedule.
  • Retail food licence — Wis. Stat. § 97.30, ATCP 75 / Wisconsin Food Code. DATCP lists “Food Retail License” with a linked Fee Schedule, statute § 97.30, and rule ATCP 75. Retail food establishments may instead be licensed by an agent city or county under § 97.41, in which case the agent’s fees apply — DATCP’s licensed-facilities lists expressly exclude facilities licensed by Dane and Milwaukee counties. Retail food establishments serving meals / catering — current DATCP fees printed on a DATCP page: Pre-packaged $105; Simple $230; Moderate $330; Complex $540 per year; renewals before 30 June each year. Source: DATCP, Retail Food Establishments Serving Meals and Catering — datcp.wi.gov — Restaurants/Catering — accessed 28 October 2026 (via search result; page text confirmed in search). [The catering-page fee table was confirmed via search extract, not a full page open in this pass — Verify against the live DATCP page before publication.]
  • Food warehouse licence — Wis. Stat. § 97.27, ATCP 71. Listed on the DATCP Food Licenses page with a linked Fee Schedule. Current fee not extracted — [Verify].
  • Hemp-as-food-ingredient overlay — historic DATCP guidance, status after 2022 [Verify]: Wis. Stat. § 97.02 provides a food is not adulterated solely because it contains industrial hemp or an industrial hemp product (as defined in § 94.55(1)). Historic DATCP guidance (2020) required, for hemp CBD foods: a “fit for commerce” certificate (or equivalent) from DATCP or another state’s hemp programme; processing in Wisconsin by a licensed processor; and packaging identifying each hemp-derived ingredient. That guidance predates the end of the DATCP processor licence, so its “licensed processor” limb cannot be read literally today. Source: DATCP hemp-derived CBD FAQ/guidance PDF (2020) — docs.legis.wisconsin.gov — DATCP Hemp CBD Guidance 2020 — accessed 28 October 2026. Current DATCP position on hemp foods, THC beverages as food, and whether a food licence alone suffices for an intoxicating-hemp beverage: NOT located in this pass — [Verify]. Do not state that a DATCP food licence “authorises” THC beverages.
  • Bottled water / THC water: historic DATCP FAQ directed CBD-water bottlers to DATCP Division of Food Safety for bottled-water rules. No current THC-water licensing statement located — [Verify].

(d) Vapor devices — DOR tobacco/vapor permit + electronic vaping device directory

  • Tobacco and vapor products distributor permit — Wis. Stat. § 139.79, Form CTV-200. Required if you are (i) a manufacturer, distributor, or subjobber selling vapor products to Wisconsin customers, or (ii) a Wisconsin retailer purchasing untaxed vapor products. Source: DOR Vapor Products Tax FAQ — revenue.wi.gov — Vapor FAQ — accessed 28 October 2026. Permit fee: not stated on the FAQ page opened — [Verify]. DOR’s FAQ also points retailers to the “Wisconsin Tobacco/Vapor Products Permit Listing” to check whether their supplier holds a distributor permit (see Part 5).
  • Electronic vaping device directory — Wis. Stat. § 995.15 (created 2023; enforcement phased): general rule: manufacturers must annually certify to DOR that a device either has FDA marketing authorisation, was marketed in the U.S. as of 8 Aug 2016 with a PMTA submitted by 9 Sept 2020 still pending/no final decision, or contains hemp (as defined in § 94.55(1)) and does not contain nicotine. Hemp-device timing is the critical nuance and is conflicting in secondary sources — [Verify] from the official § 995.15 text before publication: one secondary court-document summary states the hemp-device certification requirements “first apply … on July 1, 2026” (§ 995.15(2m)) and enforcement for hemp devices begins 1 Sept 2026, while a retailer-facing summary states nicotine-free hemp devices had to be certified by 1 July 2026 and could not be offered from 2 July unless listed. The $500-per-device manufacturer payment and $1,000-per-day-per-device forfeiture for unlisted sales are reported secondarily. Sources (secondary for § 995.15 detail): Justia § 995.15 text — law.justia.com — § 995.15 — accessed 28 October 2026. [Official docs.legis § 995.15 page not opened in this pass — Verify.] DOR must maintain the directory publicly and update it at least monthly (secondary summary of § 995.15). See Part 5 for the directory as a data source.

(e) Alcohol beverages and THC beverages — no alcohol licence for a non-alcohol THC beverage on the law located

No statute or DOR guidance was located in this pass requiring a ch. 125 alcohol-beverage licence for a hemp THC beverage that contains no alcohol. A THC beverage sold in a licensed alcohol premises, or a beverage that is an alcohol beverage with added hemp, would engage ch. 125 licensing — but that is a different product. [Verify — no DOR statement on THC beverages and alcohol licensing was located. Do not advise a THC-beverage retailer that it needs, or does not need, an alcohol licence without that check.] PROPOSED only: SB 681 / AB 606 would have renamed DOR’s Division of Alcohol Beverages as the Division of Intoxicating Products and placed HDC licensing under it. That renaming did not happen (bill failed, see 2.8).

(f) Entity registration — DFI (general, required before tax registration via One Stop)

Domestic LLC online filing fee: $130.00 plus a $1.00 portal fee; business corporation and statutory close corporation: $100.00 plus $1.00 portal fee; optional expedited service +$100.00 (acted on by close of business on the first business day following receipt). Fees non-refundable. Source: Wisconsin One Stop Business Portal — onestop.wi.gov/OpenMyBusiness — accessed 28 October 2026. DFI also handles all other entity types directly (see Part 5, DFI search).

(g) Other general permits that may bite, by activity (not hemp-specific)

Local food-dealer / retail licences (e.g., City of Milwaukee food dealer licences, with their own fee tables) — jurisdiction-specific, see Part 3. No statewide schedule was located. Weights-and-measures / method-of-sale compliance for hemp flower and CBD products (DATCP Bureau of Weights and Measures guidance, ATCP 90/91) — a compliance obligation, not a licence, and the guidance pages are linked from hemp.wi.gov — accessed 28 October 2026. Seed labelling (DATCP Seed Labeling, linked from hemp.wi.gov) — applies to seed sellers generally.

4.3 PROPOSED licence types — all failed / stripped, none in force

(i) Medical cannabis — 2025 AB 547 / SB 534 (FAILED 23 Mar 2026)

  • Regulator (proposed): Office of Medical Cannabis Regulation (for dispensaries/registry) + DATCP (for growers/processors/labs, under proposed § 94.57).
  • Grower licence (proposed § 94.57): annual fee $10,000.
  • Processor licence (proposed § 94.57): annual fee $10,000.
  • Testing laboratory licence (proposed § 94.57): annual fee $5,000; labs to test for all cannabinoids, molds, pesticides, heavy metals, and other harmful contaminants; enclosed, locked facility; statewide seed-to-sale database entry.
  • Dispensary licence (proposed ch. 259, § 259.10): annual renewal fee $5,000; dispensary may obtain packaged/labelled products only from licensed processors; may dispense only to a patient/caregiver presenting photo ID and a valid registry card, after registry verification; pharmacist consultation for new patients (secondary summary).
  • Patient/caregiver registry (proposed § 259.04): patient fee $20 at application and annually thereafter; up to 3 caregivers per patient.
  • No social-equity provision was identified in the AB 547 excerpts searched — [Verify] by full-text check before stating AB 547 had or lacked one.

(ii) Hemp-derived cannabinoid products — 2025 SB 681 / AB 606 (FAILED 23 Mar 2026)

  • Three-tier system modelled on alcohol (proposed ch. 125): Manufacturer’s permit — proposed § 125.751. Distributor’s permit — proposed § 125.753, including cooperative distributors for small manufacturers (1-year small-manufacturer certification; distributor may distribute only its members’ products). Retail licences — Class “HDC-A” and Class “HDC-B” licences — proposed § 125.761, issued at municipal level (municipalities retain ability to limit/prohibit retail sales, per sponsor testimony). Product registration with DOR; independent lab testing with QR-coded certificates of analysis; 21+ sales; potency caps by ingestion form; child-resistant, non-child-appealing packaging; behind-counter/restricted placement.
  • Permit/licence fees: NOT extracted from the bill text in this pass — [Verify]. Do not quote a fee for these proposed permits.
  • Occupational tax: see 2.8 (beverage $0.03/mg THC; other non-flower $0.045/mg THC; flower/plant parts $50/oz).
  • No social-equity provision was identified in the SB 681 / AB 606 excerpts searched — [Verify] by full-text check.

(iii) Hemp regulation (narrower) — 2025 SB 682 / AB 747 (FAILED 23 Mar 2026)

Title: “An Act to amend 94.55 (1); to create 94.55 (5) of the statutes; Relating to: regulating hemp-derived cannabinoid products.” The docs.legis status page opened gives title and history only; the operative licence/permit provisions, if any, were not summarised from the bill text in this pass — [Verify]. Do not attribute SB 681’s three-tier permits or tax rates to SB 682. Source: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/2025/proposals/reg/sen/bill/SB682 — accessed 28 October 2026.

(iv) Governor’s budget — 2025 AB 50 / SB 45 (cannabis provisions STRIPPED 8 May 2025, not enacted)

Would have created a medical programme and an adult-use market regulated similarly to alcohol, with home cultivation (up to 6 plants per household in secondary summaries), 15% wholesale + 10% retail excise (see 2.8), and, in secondary summaries, expungement and “social equity measures” in licensing. Licence types, fees, and the specific social-equity mechanism were not verified from AB 50 / SB 45 text in this pass — [Verify]. Secondary source for the social-equity claim — accessed 28 October 2026. [Secondary only; do not quote as the bill’s operative text.]

(v) Comprehensive legalisation — 2026 SB 1045 (FAILED 23 Mar 2026)

Secondary summaries describe adult-use + medical legalisation, expungement, and intoxicating-hemp guardrails (21+, 1–10 mg per serving / 12 fl oz beverage definition in one summary; trigger repeal of the hemp provisions when the federal hemp ban takes effect, folding low-dose products into the adult-use regime). Licence types, fees, and tax rates were not verified from the SB 1045 text in this pass — [Verify].

(vi) Age-only bills — 2025 SB 644 / AB 680 (FAILED 23 Mar 2026)

Title in secondary records: prohibiting individuals under 21 from accessing intoxicating hemp products, with a penalty. No licence type is stated in the title; no licence detail was verified — [Verify].

4.4 Social equity

Current programme: NONE located. There is no Wisconsin social-equity cannabis/hemp licensing programme in force, because there is no Wisconsin cannabis licensing programme at all. A secondary 2026 policy brief states the same conclusion in general terms (“no social-equity or microbusiness licensing (no market to license)”). [Secondary; the underlying fact — no programme in statute — follows from 4.1.] Proposed: only the Governor’s budget bills (AB 50 / SB 45) are secondarily described as including social-equity measures (see 4.3(iv)). No operative social-equity text (e.g., equity applicant definition, fee waiver, licence set-aside, or fund) was verified from any bill text in this pass. Do not describe a Wisconsin social-equity programme, current or proposed, in detail without a full-text check.

4.5 Estimated startup costs for hemp retail / manufacturing in Wisconsin

No official Wisconsin startup-cost estimate was located. The following is therefore not a total estimate — it is only the sum of the verified official fee components that apply before rent, inventory, equipment, testing, insurance, legal, build-out, or labour, none of which is officially priced in this pass and none of which is estimated here.

Verified official fee components (only): DFI entity formation (One Stop): LLC $131 total ($130 + $1 portal); corporation/close corporation $101 total ($100 + $1); optional expedited +$100. Source: onestop.wi.gov/OpenMyBusiness — accessed 28 October 2026. DOR seller’s permit: fee not stated (see 4.2(b)); possible security deposit up to $15,000 if DOR requires it (most commonly on delinquent-tax history). DATCP retail food serving-meals licence (if serving meals): $105 / $230 / $330 / $540 by complexity. DATCP food processing plant / retail food store / warehouse fees: current amounts not extracted — [Verify] (see 4.2(c)). USDA hemp producer licence (grower only): no licensing fee stated by DATCP at transition; FBI criminal-history check cost not verified — [Verify]. DOR vapor distributor permit: fee not stated — [Verify]; electronic vaping device directory manufacturer payment $500 per device reported secondarily — [Verify]. Local licences/fees: jurisdiction-specific, not compiled — see Part 3.

Any “total startup cost” figure from a non-official source (consultancy, business-plan site, or vendor) was deliberately not used, because the task requires non-official estimates to be marked as such and no reliable, dated, Wisconsin-specific non-official estimate was verified in this pass. If a range is needed, it must be sourced separately, dated, and labelled non-official.

Total counts by type — what is official, what is not

  • Official count from an opened primary source in this pass: NONE for current Wisconsin hemp producers. USDA’s plan list (5.5) gives status, not a count. DATCP’s historic page (5.7) gives counts only as images that were not transcribed.
  • Secondary counts — CONFLICTING, do not present any one as the official figure: “About 470 federally licensed hemp growers” in Wisconsin, reported May 2026 (Wisconsin Watch via Herring Bank summary) — accessed 28 October 2026. “470 federally licensed hemp producers as of November” 2025, as stated in Governor Evers’s February 2026 letter to the congressional delegation (reported by Cannabis Business Times, July 2026) — accessed 28 October 2026. “298 active USDA licensed hemp producers in Wisconsin” as of June 2026, and “nearly 400” in 2024, from a non-official business-plan site citing USDA Public Search Data / National Hemp Report — accessed 28 October 2026. [Low-authority source; the 298 vs. 470 conflict is itself a gap — the difference may reflect active vs. ever-licensed, a search-date difference, or a counting-method difference. Verify by running the USDA Public Search Tool filtered to Wisconsin and stating the search date and filter.]
  • Counts that must NOT be quoted as current: any DATCP 2018–21 grower/processor count (historic programme, ended 2021) and any SB 681 modelled licence count (the DOR fiscal estimate’s illustrative “300 licenses” for a different, failed bill’s fee-revenue illustration was located in search for a different bill, AB 1061, whose identity was not confirmed — it is not used in this report).
  • Other counts: zero state cannabis licences (Part 4.1) is a count of a different kind — it is the absence of a programme, not a register total. Zero hemp-specific state retail/manufacturing licences likewise reflects the absence of such a licence, not a register total.

PART 6 — MARKET DATA

Scope rule applied: official sources first (DATCP, USDA NASS, Governor’s Office, Wisconsin Legislative Fiscal Bureau via secondary reporting of its memo). Secondary sources are used only for non-law market facts and are labelled as such with publication date. Nothing below is estimated by this research. Where a page could not be opened directly, that is stated and the figure is marked [Verify].

6.1 Hemp acreage and licence counts by year

6.1.1 The break in the series — read this before the table

Wisconsin’s state hemp programme ended 1 January 2022: DATCP’s 2021 Plant Industry Bureau Annual Report states the state-operated programme “was discontinued and regulatory authority for the production of hemp in Wisconsin was transferred to the USDA. Wisconsin hemp growers are now licensed by USDA and hemp processors no longer require a license.” Source: datcp.wi.gov — 2021 PIB Annual Report — accessed 28 October 2026.

Consequences: DATCP licence/processor counts stop after 2021. There is no DATCP grower or processor count for 2022 onward. USDA NASS acreage figures from 2021 onward are survey estimates, not programme registrations. NASS itself notes its hemp estimates started in 2021, and 2020 is shown as “N/A.” “Licensed,” “licensed and registered/active,” “applications received,” and “registered acres” are different things in DATCP material and must not be added together. The table below keeps them in separate columns and labels each one.

Source chosen where figures conflict: for 2018–2020 programme counts, the contemporaneous DATCP “Hemp Update” to the Board of Agriculture, Trade and Consumer Protection, 14 May 2020, is used as the headline series: datcp.wi.gov — Hemp Update, 14 May 2020 — accessed 28 October 2026. For 2021, the DATCP 2021 Annual Report (above) is used. For 2022–2025 acreage, USDA NASS National Hemp Report, released 16 April 2026, is used: nass.usda.gov — National Hemp Report, 16 April 2026 — accessed 28 October 2026. All conflicting figures are listed in 6.7, not silently merged.
