Sources for the table: 2018–2020 DATCP counts and 2020 “registered” label: DATCP Hemp Update, 14 May 2020, above — accessed 28 October 2026. The same update notes ~50% returning / ~50% new licensees in 2020 and anticipated hemp in all 72 Wisconsin counties in 2020. 2021 DATCP counts, 1,989 acres, 66 counties, and the 1 January 2022 transfer: DATCP Plant Industry Bureau Annual Report 2021, above — accessed 28 October 2026. The same report states 722 regulatory samples were taken in 2021, 89% passing at 0.3% total THC or lower and 11% failing. 2021–2025 NASS acreage: USDA NASS National Hemp Report, 16 April 2026, above — accessed 28 October 2026; and USDA NASS Wisconsin Annual Statistical Bulletin 2025 for the 2021–2024 series as summarised in search results: nass.usda.gov — Wisconsin Ag Stats 2025 — accessed 28 October 2026 (2021: 680 planted / 580 harvested; 2022: 870 / 740, 76,881 sq ft under protection; 2023: planted and harvested withheld, 59,815 sq ft; 2024: 460 / 320, 42,260 sq ft). 2022 NASS figures also confirmed in USDA NASS release via Morning Ag Clips, 2023 — accessed 28 October 2026 (“Wisconsin hemp growers planted 870 acres of hemp in open fields in 2022, up 190 acres from 2021… Harvested acres for 2022 were estimated at 740, compared with 580 acres the previous year. Hemp producers used 76,881 square feet under protection”). 470 producers, November 2025: Governor’s press release/letter: content.govdelivery.com — Governor’s bulletin — accessed 28 October 2026 (“as of November 2025, Wisconsin had 470 federally licensed hemp producers”).

6.1.2 What the decline actually looks like — and what it does not show

  • Pilot peak (programme-reported): 2019 is the peak year on every DATCP measure that is comparable: 1,251 growers, 560 processors, ~5,000 acres planted. DATCP’s October 2019 bulletin put it in the same terms: “1,247 growers and 556 processors who were licensed and registered to be active in 2019” and “about 850 growers reported actually planting a crop, totaling approximately 5,000 acres.” Source: datcp.wi.gov — 2020 Hemp Apps Open — accessed 28 October 2026. The small difference between 1,251/560 (May 2020 update) and 1,247/556 (October 2019 bulletin) is a timing/snapshot difference, not a separate year — see 6.7.
  • Registered vs planted gap, 2019: UW–Madison Division of Extension, Sheboygan County, 24 February 2020, reporting DATCP data as of 4 February 2020, gives the fuller picture: ~16,000 acres registered across 2,601 locations in 71 counties, but only ~5,000 acres planted, ~860 licensees with samples taken, ~650 licensees reporting a harvest, ~3,930 acres reported harvested, ~98% harvested for CBD/biomass, ~688 acres reported destroyed for exceeding THC, ~3,521,000 lb reported harvested, and only ~6% of the harvest reported as sold. Source: sheboygan.extension.wisc.edu — 24 Feb 2020 — accessed 28 October 2026. This is the strongest official-adjacent evidence of the 2019 CBD oversupply: most registered acreage was never planted, and most of what was harvested was not yet sold.
  • 2020: the May 2020 DATCP figure of 9,430 acres is explicitly labelled “(registered)” in the DATCP table. It must NOT be read as 9,430 acres planted. A later secondary report (WeedLife News, reporting Wisconsin Public Radio / DATCP data, 2021) states 5,444 acres were planted in 2020, with 1,537 grower and 761 processor applications received that year. That planted figure is secondary and not adopted as the headline because the DATCP final 2020 report was not located in this pass — see 6.7.
  • 2021 contraction: DATCP licensed growers fell from 1,128 (2020) to 679 (2021), and processors from 536 to 427, before the programme closed. Secondary reporting (Shepherd Express, October 2021) states applications fell to 1,339 in 2021, a 42% drop from “higher than 2,200 per year until then.” That application figure is secondary and is not in the headline table.
  • Post-transfer scale: on the NASS survey series, Wisconsin open-field hemp is now a few-hundred-acre crop: 870 acres planted (2022), (D) withheld (2023), 460 (2024), 550 (2025). Harvested acreage tracked planted acreage closely in 2024–2025 (320 of 460; 330 of 550), unlike 2019, when reported harvested acreage (~3,930) was well below planted acreage (~5,000) and far below registered acreage (~16,000).
  • Floral dominance in recent years (NASS): in 2024, floral hemp harvested in the open was 275 acres out of 320 total harvested acres (86%); in 2025, floral was 325 acres out of 330 total harvested acres (98.5%). Source: USDA NASS National Hemp Report, 16 April 2026, above — accessed 28 October 2026. This means recent Wisconsin hemp acreage is overwhelmingly floral (cannabinoid) hemp, not fibre or grain — but NASS withholds Wisconsin grain, fibre, and seed acreage/values as (D) or reports them only by region, so a fibre/grain split for Wisconsin cannot be stated from NASS.
  • County concentration: DATCP 2021 reports hemp planted in 66 counties; DATCP/Extension 2019 reports registered locations in 71 counties. No official county-by-county acreage ranking was located in this pass. Do not rank counties. Official county-level detail, if needed, is a follow-up pull from DATCP annual reports / USDA AMS licence records (see 6.7).

6.2 Hemp product market size and retailer counts

6.2.1 Official position: there is no official Wisconsin retail market size or retailer count

Wisconsin has no state licence, permit, or registration for retail sale of hemp-derived products (Part 4). Without a retail licence register, DATCP and the Department of Revenue publish no official statewide retailer count and no official retail sales total for hemp-derived THC, CBD, or smoke/vape shops. Any statewide retailer number presented without that caveat would be invented. The Governor’s “$700 million in economic production / nearly 3,500 jobs” figure (November 2025 producer count context, press release above) is the closest thing to an official sector-size statement, but it is an executive estimate of economic production and employment across farmers, processors, and retailers, not a measured retail sales figure, and its methodology was not published with the letter. It must be labelled as an estimate whenever reused. No official Wisconsin Department of Revenue hemp/CBD sales-tax total was located in this pass. That is a gap, not a zero — see 6.7.

6.2.2 The only counted retailer figure found: Wood County (county-level only — do not extrapolate)

Wood County THC Community Report 2025 (University of Wisconsin–Stevens Point / Wood County Public Health, surveillance May–September 2025): of 242 potential retail locations scanned (online, phone, and in-person), 76 retailers (31%) were confirmed to sell at least one intoxicating hemp product as defined in Wood County Ordinance 236.05 (delta-8, delta-10, HHC, HHC-O, THCA, THC-O, THCP, THCV, or hemp-derived delta-9 at 0.3% or less). That is a 245% increase from 22 retailers identified in 2023 in the same county. 25% of the identified THC retailers were bars and taverns. Source: Wood County THC Community Report 2025 PDF — accessed 28 October 2026; and UW–Stevens Point summary, 11 March 2026 — accessed 28 October 2026. Explicit limit: Wood County is one of 72 Wisconsin counties. This 76-retailer figure says nothing about Milwaukee County, Dane County, or the state total, and it counts bars/taverns and convenience stores as well as dedicated hemp shops. It must not be multiplied or used as a statewide proxy.

“Hundreds of shops” language: secondary reporting (e.g., Drug Policy Watch brief, undated, summarising Wisconsin Watch / AP coverage) states industry owners warn the federal change “would decimate hundreds of dispensaries and shops opened since 2018.” That is advocacy/industry language, not a count, and is not adopted as a retailer total. Franchise-only counts are NOT statewide counts: CBD American Shaman’s own store-locator pages state “approximately 6 licensed CBD retail outlets in Wisconsin” / “5” on different pages for that one franchise chain only. These pages contradict each other (5 vs 6), count one brand only, and use the word “licensed” for a state with no retail licence. Do not use them as a Wisconsin retailer total. They are listed here only to warn Run 1 against them.

6.2.3 Business concentration — official sources only

No official source in this pass ranks or clusters Wisconsin hemp businesses by city or county, and no such ranking is offered here. The only official-adjacent geographic facts found are: (a) DATCP 2021: hemp planted in 66 counties; (b) DATCP/Extension 2019: registered locations in 71 counties; (c) Wood County 2025: intoxicating-hemp retail is present across store types including bars/taverns, not only dedicated shops. That is the full extent of what can be said officially about concentration in this pass.

6.3 Wisconsin residents buying in Illinois, Michigan, and Minnesota

6.3.1 Illinois — the only verified Wisconsin-resident figure

Verified (official LFB memo, via contemporaneous reporting — the memo PDF itself was not opened directly in this pass, see 6.7): Illinois dispensaries made $1,552.3 million in cannabis sales (excluding tax) in calendar year 2022 (Illinois Department of Financial and Professional Regulation data, via LFB). Of that, $239.7 million (15.4%) came from dispensaries in counties bordering Wisconsin. Of the border-county sales, $121.2 million (50.6%) were to out-of-state residents. That is ~7.8% of total Illinois cannabis sales revenue in 2022. Illinois tax attributed to those sales: $36.1 million, on the LFB assumption that all out-of-state sales in border counties were to Wisconsin residents. LFB itself notes it is possible not all such sales were to Wisconsin residents. Border counties with dispensaries in the memo: Jo Daviess, Lake, McHenry, Winnebago, and Boone (Stephenson County had none at that time — the 2023 reporting varies between “five of six” and naming four counties; the county list is therefore flagged in 6.7).

Sources: LFB memo, 10 March 2023, as reported by Ganjapreneur, March 2023 — accessed 28 October 2026; and Milwaukee Journal Sentinel / Wisconsin Watch fact brief, 23 March 2026 (confirming the $36.1 million / ~half of border-county sales / Michigan and Minnesota do not track nonresident purchases) — accessed 28 October 2026. Important limit: this is a 2022 figure. It is still the most recent verified LFB figure in this pass. Do not present it as a 2024, 2025, or 2026 figure.

Statewide Illinois context (official IDFPR data, NOT Wisconsin-specific): Illinois adult-use sales to out-of-state customers statewide totalled $385 million in 2024, ~22% of all adult-use sales, down from $408 million (25%) in 2023 and $479 million (31%) in 2022, per IDFPR figures summarised by Cannabis Business Times, February 2025 — accessed 28 October 2026. IDFPR’s own monthly releases publish the in-state / out-of-state split by month. Out-of-state ≠ Wisconsin. Visitors from Indiana, Iowa, Missouri, Kentucky, and elsewhere are in that total. No IDFPR release breaks out-of-state sales by purchaser’s home state.

Proximity context (secondary, dated): Wisconsin Policy Forum, “Changing Midwest Marijuana Landscape Impacts Wisconsin,” February 2023, found half of Wisconsin residents aged 21+ (about 2.16 million people) lived within a 75-minute drive of an adult-use dispensary in Illinois or Michigan, and 30% within an hour’s drive. Source: Wisconsin Policy Forum summary, 4 January 2024 — accessed 28 October 2026. This is a drive-time estimate from before Minnesota retail launch; it is not a sales or purchaser count, and it predates Minnesota’s September 2025 retail opening.

6.3.2 Michigan — no Wisconsin-resident figure; only an assumption-based estimate [Verify — do not adopt as official]

Michigan does not track nonresident cannabis purchases (Milwaukee Journal Sentinel / Wisconsin Watch, 23 March 2026, above). A secondary, assumption-based estimate circulating in 2026 reporting claims: Michigan had 854 retailers/microbusinesses in 2024, 22 in counties bordering Wisconsin (Gogebic, Iron, Dickinson, and Menominee counties), those 22 produced $85.4 million on an equal-sales-per-retailer assumption, and applying Illinois’ 41.5% out-of-state share gives ~$5.8 million in Michigan tax from Wisconsin residents. Source for the existence of this estimate (a scraped/mixed-content page — low reliability, see 6.7) — accessed 28 October 2026. This Michigan figure is NOT adopted. It stacks two assumptions (equal sales per store; Illinois’ share applies to Michigan’s Upper Peninsula) on top of an unverified LFB update. The only Michigan facts that can be stated here are: Michigan does not track nonresident purchases, and Michigan adult-use retailers may sell to any adult 21+ with valid government ID regardless of home state (secondary guides; the underlying Michigan law is Part 10’s subject, not this pass).

6.3.3 Minnesota — no Wisconsin-resident figure [Unavailable]

Minnesota does not track nonresident cannabis purchases (same 23 March 2026 fact brief). Minnesota state-licensed adult-use retail sales began 16 September 2025 (Minnesota Office of Cannabis Management press release, 16 September 2025) — accessed 28 October 2026. Tribal retail preceded state-licensed retail from August 2023, but no tribal sales-by-residency data was located. Minnesota OCM reported over $250 million in combined adult-use (~$150 million) and medical (~$100 million) sales in the first year of statewide adult-use sales (press release, September 2026, one-year anniversary) — accessed 28 October 2026. No Wisconsin share is given or can be derived from that total. Conclusion for all three neighbours: Illinois 2022 is the only year with a Wisconsin-attributed tax figure. For Michigan and Minnesota, and for Illinois after 2022, the Wisconsin-resident amount is [Unavailable] from official regulator data in this pass.

6.4 Official price data

6.4.1 USDA NASS — Wisconsin floral hemp (official, farm-gate, open-grown)

202427560016515510$55.00$8,52568%
202532577025024010$21.90$5,25681%

Source: USDA NASS National Hemp Report, 16 April 2026: nass.usda.gov — National Hemp Report — accessed 28 October 2026. Notes and limits: the 2024 price ($55.00/lb) and value ($8.525 million) match the secondary summary that described Wisconsin floral value as “$8.5 million, 2.2% of the U.S. total” — the NASS figure is adopted, the secondary percentage is not (the U.S. floral open value in the same report is $385.870 million for 2024, so $8.525m is 2.2% — arithmetic checks, but the percentage itself is not printed as a Wisconsin line in NASS). The price fell 60% from 2024 to 2025 ($55.00 → $21.90/lb) while yield rose (600 → 770 lb/acre) and utilised production rose (155,000 → 240,000 lb). Value therefore fell despite higher volume. This is a blended NASS price (biomass + flower combined in the floral category), not a smokable-flower retail price — see the Whitney Economics critique below; do not quote $55.00 or $21.90 as a retail or wholesale flower price.

Under-protection floral hemp, Wisconsin (official, but tiny and volatile): 2024: 311 lb total, 130 lb utilised, $961.00/lb, $125,000 value. 2025: 3,261 lb total, 3,202 lb utilised, $366.00/lb, $1,172,000 value. Source: same NASS report — accessed 28 October 2026. The per-pound figures under protection are an order of magnitude higher than open-grown because they reflect hand-trimmed/extracted product mix, not the same product. Do not average them with open-grown prices. Floral-only total: open + under-protection utilised value = $8.650 million (2024) and $6.428 million (2025). This is a constructed sum of two published NASS lines, labelled as floral-only. It is NOT total Wisconsin hemp production value: NASS withholds Wisconsin grain, fibre, and seed values as (D) or publishes them only by multi-state region, so no official total Wisconsin hemp value can be stated from NASS 2026. 2023 Wisconsin floral price/value: (D) withheld in NASS 2026. 2021–2022 Wisconsin floral price/value: not published as state lines in the NASS 2026 report (2021–2022 state floral tables are in earlier NASS reports, not pulled in this pass — see 6.7). U.S. context (for scale only, not Wisconsin prices): U.S. floral hemp grown in the open, 2025: 33.2 million lb produced, $574 million value, $19.30/lb average (NASS 2026, above). U.S. total hemp value 2025: $739 million, up 64% from 2024 ($449.573 million). These national figures must not be presented as Wisconsin figures.

6.4.2 Critique of NASS floral valuation (secondary, dated 2025–2026 — include as a caveat, not as a replacement price)

Whitney Economics (Beau Whitney) argues NASS’s blended biomass/flower average understates floral value: it cites a 2024 flower price “in excess of $330 per pound” vs biomass ~$2.00/lb, against NASS’s $18.56–$19.10/lb U.S. average, and a $386 million U.S. floral total that it recalculates as ~$6.86 billion at flower prices. Source: Cannabis Law Report press release summarising Whitney Economics — accessed 28 October 2026; and Hemp Gazette, 2025 — accessed 28 October 2026. Treatment: this critique is noted because Run 1 will otherwise misread NASS prices as market prices. The Whitney $330/lb figure is not adopted as a Wisconsin price — it is a national claim by an industry economist, not a Wisconsin transaction dataset.

6.4.3 Historic CBD price collapse (secondary, 2019–2020 — context for the acreage crash)

UW Extension, February 2020 (above): “CBD prices have collapsed… Prices down ~70% from spring… Price crashed as harvest started,” citing The Globe and Mail, 1 February 2020. WisBusiness / WisPolitics, May 2020, reporting Hemp Benchmarks and PanXchange: hemp flower at $145/lb in April 2020, down 36% from $226/lb in January 2020; crude oil down 21–46%; hemp seed down from $1,435/lb to $578/lb (−60%). These are private price-benchmark figures, not official Wisconsin prices, and are included only to explain why planted acreage and licence counts fell after 2019. No official Wisconsin retail price series (for CBD, delta-8, THCA flower, edibles, or beverages) was located. No official Illinois/Michigan/Minnesota retail price is used as a Wisconsin price here. Illinois’ 2024 average adult-use flower price ($9/gram, per Illinois Cannabis Regulation Oversight Officer via Cannabis Business Times) is an Illinois figure and must not be presented as a Wisconsin price.

6.5 Cannabis/hemp-friendly banks and credit unions operating in Wisconsin

Bottom line for this pass: no Wisconsin-chartered bank or credit union was confirmed, from a public source opened in this pass, as operating a dedicated cannabis or hemp banking programme in Wisconsin. What follows is everything that could be verified, and what could not.

6.5.1 Named institutions with a public source — with strict limits

  1. Herring Bank — hemp/CBD banking marketed to Wisconsin (NOT confirmed as Wisconsin-chartered or Wisconsin-branched). Herring Bank’s Wisconsin hemp-banking page states it “works with Wisconsin hemp growers, processors, manufacturers, wholesalers, retailers and testing labs to open compliant business checking and savings accounts,” lists Milwaukee, Madison, Green Bay, Kenosha, Racine, Appleton, Waukesha, Eau Claire, Oshkosh, and Janesville as cities served, and states Wisconsin has “about 470 federally licensed hemp growers” (citing Wisconsin Watch, May 2026). Source: herringbank.com — Hemp Banking Wisconsin — accessed 28 October 2026. Limits: this is a vendor marketing page, not a regulator list, not evidence of a Wisconsin branch, and not evidence of marijuana (as opposed to hemp) banking. Herring Bank’s home state / charter and any Wisconsin physical presence are [Verify] — they are not stated on the page excerpt and are not asserted here. Do not list Herring as a “Wisconsin bank.”
  2. Safe Harbor Financial (SHF Holdings) — national fintech, NO Wisconsin partner named. Safe Harbor describes itself as a financial technology company (not a bank) providing cannabis/hemp banking through FDIC/NCUA-insured partner banks and credit unions, serving dispensaries, cultivators, manufacturers, distributors, testing labs, and hemp/CBD businesses nationally. Source: shfinancial.org — FAQ — accessed 28 October 2026. Limit: no Wisconsin partner bank or credit union is named on that page, and no Wisconsin client is evidenced. Safe Harbor must NOT be listed as “operating in Wisconsin” on this evidence alone. Its February 2026 extension with “PCCU” (GlobeNewswire, 9 February 2026) does not identify PCCU as a Wisconsin institution in the excerpt, and that identification is [Verify] — do not expand the acronym.
  3. Community Choice Credit Union — DO NOT LIST AS WISCONSIN. Its cannabis-banking page describes a “Choice Cash” cannabis programme with 24 brick-and-mortar locations, but nothing on the page excerpt ties it to Wisconsin, and it is a Michigan credit union by name recognition that was NOT verified in this pass. It is mentioned here only to prevent a Run 1 error: a web search for “Wisconsin credit union cannabis” returns it, but that does not make it a Wisconsin institution.

6.5.2 What Wisconsin regulators and trade groups have actually said (secondary/legal commentary, dated)

  • Boardman Clark (Madison law firm), “Should I Bank Hemp and CBD Customers?” (undated in excerpt; discussing the then-current Wisconsin Pilot Program, so pre-2022 in substance): “the Wisconsin Department of Financial Institutions (DFI) does not currently examine banks for CBD and hemp-related business and will not write-up a bank for banking these businesses,” and FDIC treated lawful hemp as a safety-and-soundness / know-your-customer issue, with the 2014 FinCEN marijuana guidance not applying to lawful hemp (but potentially applying to CBD oil depending on legality). Source: boardmanclark.com — accessed 28 October 2026. This is law-firm commentary reporting regulator conversations, not a DFI or FDIC publication, and it predates the USDA transfer and the 2026 federal hemp redefinition. Do not present it as current DFI/FDIC policy.
  • Wisconsin Bankers Association / Wisconsin Credit Union League / DFI testimony (Assembly Committee on Financial Institutions hearing, reported second-hand, ~2020): WBA officers described banking a marijuana business in Wisconsin as “highly complex, highly risky,” DFI’s deputy secretary said banks/credit unions cannot bank marijuana customers/businesses while marijuana is not legal in Wisconsin, and a Wisconsin marijuana-banking statute would give institutions “a bit of comfort.” Source (advocacy blog reporting a hearing — low reliability for exact quotations, usable only for the general position that no Wisconsin marijuana-banking framework existed) — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Federal baseline (given, not re-researched): there is no federal cannabis banking law. That baseline, plus Wisconsin having no legal marijuana market, means there is no Wisconsin marijuana-banking market to list — only hemp banking, which operates under general BSA/AML due diligence, not a cannabis-specific safe harbour.

6.5.3 Gap

No DFI list, FDIC list, or Wisconsin Bankers Association / Credit Union League directory of hemp-friendly institutions was located. A systematic check would require: (a) DFI’s bank/credit union directories, (b) direct confirmation from any institution claiming a hemp programme, and (c) separation of “hemp/CBD accounts offered nationally” from “Wisconsin branch or Wisconsin-chartered.” See 6.7.

6.7 Tribal hemp/market developments — only as they affect market numbers

No official tribal hemp acreage, producer count, retail sales, or tribal tax figure for Wisconsin tribes was located in this pass. Tribal operations, ordinances, and regulators are Part 10’s subject; they are not estimated here. The only tribal market-adjacent facts verified in this pass are event-related: the Great Lakes Indigenous Cannabis and Hemp Summit (above) was hosted in Lac du Flambeau, Vilas County, with tribal leadership participation, and its founder is described as founder of “Wisconsin’s first independent Indigenous owned hemp farm and cannabis retailer Canndigenous” and a member of the Bad River Band of the Lake Superior Tribe of Chippewa Indians (Indigenous Cannabis Industry Association press release, 21 January 2026). That “first” claim is secondary/organiser language, [Verify] against tribal and business sources, and it is not a market-size figure. Effect on market numbers: because tribal hemp production, if any, would be licensed under tribal/USDA frameworks rather than DATCP, it would NOT appear in DATCP pilot counts and may or may not appear in NASS survey estimates (NASS does not publish a tribal breakout for Wisconsin). Any tribal acreage is therefore a potential undercount in both series, unquantified here. Flag for Part 10 to resolve with tribal sources.

6.8 Gaps, conflicts, and recommended follow-ups (Part 6)

Conflicts — source chosen is stated; the other figure is preserved here, not deleted:

  1. 2021 planted acreage — DATCP 1,989 acres vs NASS 680 acres. DATCP Annual Report 2021 says 1,989 acres planted in 66 counties; NASS says 680 planted / 580 harvested for 2021. Chosen: both are reported side-by-side in 6.1 with the conflict flagged, because they measure different things (programme reports vs survey estimates) and neither source explains the gap. Follow-up: DATCP 2021 final production reports and NASS 2021 methodology note; do not average them.
  2. 2018 grower/processor counts — DATCP Hemp Update (185/82) vs DATCP Biennial Report 2017–2019 (242 growers / 99 processors licensed in 2018; 1,322 grower / 628 processor licences issued in 2019) vs DATCP October 2019 bulletin (1,247/556 active in 2019). These differ by snapshot date and by “licensed” vs “licensed and registered/active” vs “licences issued.” Chosen: Hemp Update May 2020 for the headline table. Follow-up: DATCP biennial report PDF — accessed 28 October 2026 via search results; the PDF was not opened directly in this pass — re-open to confirm the 242/99 and 1,322/628 lines before Run 1 cites them.
  3. 2019 acreage — ~5,000 planted (DATCP, consistent across sources) vs ~16,000 registered (Extension/DATCP, Feb 2020) vs 14,200 → 5,300 acres claim (Shepherd Express, Oct 2021). The Shepherd figures do not match DATCP’s 2019 registered (~16,000) or 2021 planted (1,989) figures and appear to conflate registered and planted acres across years. Chosen: DATCP/Extension registered-vs-planted distinction. The Shepherd 14,200/5,300 pair is not adopted.
  4. 2020 planted acreage — 9,430 registered (DATCP, May 2020) vs 5,444 planted (secondary, WPR/WeedLife) vs 5,444 planted / 587 harvested (low-reliability business-plan site). The 5,444 planted figure is plausible as a final-season figure but is secondary; the 587 harvested figure appears only on the business-plan site and conflicts with the scale of 2020 sampling (“similar to 2019” per DATCP). Chosen: DATCP registered figure labelled as registered; 5,444 noted as secondary; 587 marked [Verify] and not adopted.
  5. 2023–2024 USDA producer counts — “nearly 400” (business-plan site, citing USDA Public Search Data, for 2024) vs 470 (Governor, November 2025). These are different dates and the “nearly 400” figure is from a non-official site that also contains the unverified 587-acre claim. Chosen: 470 (November 2025, Governor citing USDA) as the only official-adjacent count. Follow-up: count the USDA AMS Hemp Licensees FOIA PDF directly and date-stamp the count.
  6. PART 7 — HEMP LICENCES

7.1 The transition: Wisconsin ended its state pilot on 1 January 2022

DATCP's transition page states: "Beginning January 1, 2022, the DATCP hemp program is discontinued" and "Beginning January 1, 2022, Wisconsin hemp growers are licensed by the U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA)." Source: hemp.wi.gov — accessed 28 October 2026. DATCP's historical page adds that from 1 January 2022 hemp processors no longer require a DATCP licence, and that 2018–21 annual registrants must keep specified records for 3 years under Wis. Admin. Code ch. ATCP 22, with DATCP retaining authority to request them. Source: datcp.wi.gov/Pages/Programs_Services/HempData.aspx — accessed 28 October 2026.

Consequence: there is no current Wisconsin state hemp grower, processor, or handler licence to apply for. The only current production licence for a Wisconsin grower outside tribal jurisdiction is the USDA producer licence. The DATCP licences in §7.7 are historic only.

7.2 Current licence: USDA hemp producer licence (7 CFR part 990, subpart B)

What it allows: production of hemp (planting, growing, harvesting) in a location covered by the USDA plan — which includes Wisconsin, since Wisconsin has no USDA-approved state plan. It does not by itself authorise manufacture, retail sale, or any activity separately regulated under general Wisconsin law (food, retail, tobacco/vapor, tax — see Part 11 and Run 1 Part 4).

  • How to apply: applications are accepted at any time through the Hemp eManagement Platform (HeMP). Sources: 7 CFR 990.21, ecfr.gov — 7 CFR part 990; USDA AMS hemp programme, ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Fee: no licence fee is stated in 7 CFR 990.21 or in the DATCP transition statement. Do not publish a USDA producer licence fee — none was located [Unavailable]. Testing, sampling-agent, and laboratory costs are charged by the sampler/lab, not as a USDA licence fee, and no Wisconsin-specific figure was located [Verify].
  • Term: valid until 31 December of the year three years after the year issued. Source: 7 CFR 990.22 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Renewal: not automatic; same terms, information, and approval criteria as an initial application unless law or regulation has changed. Source: 7 CFR 990.22 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Modification and transfer — expressly prohibited in substance: a modification is required for any change to application information, including sale of a business, production in a new location, or change in key participants. Licences "may not be sold, assigned, transferred, pledged, or otherwise disposed of, alienated or encumbered." Source: 7 CFR 990.21–990.22 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Multi-state rule: an applicant producing in more than one state/tribal territory may hold more than one licence, but an operation in a location covered by a state or tribal plan must be licensed under that plan, not the USDA plan. Source: 7 CFR 990.21 — accessed 28 October 2026.

7.3 Current grower requirement: criminal history report

The application is incomplete without a current criminal history report — for an individual applicant, or for all key participants of a business entity — dated within 60 days of submission. No licence issues unless the report confirms no state or federal felony conviction relating to a controlled substance within the past 10 years, except a person who lawfully grew hemp under section 7606 of the Agricultural Act of 2014 before 20 December 2018 whose conviction also occurred before that date. Material falsification makes the person ineligible. Source: 7 CFR 990.20–990.22 — accessed 28 October 2026.

Terminology note: the operative federal rule requires a criminal history report; whether a given report is FBI-sourced depends on the channel used. Say "criminal history report (see 7 CFR 990.20)" rather than asserting an FBI-only channel, unless USDA's current application instructions confirm it — [Verify] the exact FBI-channel instruction on the current HeMP application page.

Contrast with historic DATCP rule (do not conflate): the early DATCP rule text stated no grower or processor licence would issue to an applicant who has ever been convicted of a criminal violation of the federal Controlled Substances Act, Wis. Stat. ch. 961, or any other state's controlled-substances law, as shown by the criminal history search. That lifetime bar is broader than USDA's 10-year bar and is historic only. Source: historic ATCP 22 rule text via EmR2111 / DATCP hemp programme materials — accessed 28 October 2026.

7.4 Current grower requirement: lot / GPS / FSA registration

A USDA licensee must report hemp crop acreage to the USDA Farm Service Agency (FSA) within 30 days of planting, giving at minimum: street address and, to the extent practicable, geospatial location of the lot, greenhouse, building, or site; acreage or greenhouse/indoor square footage; and the hemp licence number. All production locations must be reported. Source: 7 CFR 990.23 — accessed 28 October 2026. There is no current Wisconsin state lot/GPS registration form — the historic DATCP planting-report regime ended with the programme on 1 January 2022 (see §7.7).

7.5 Tribal-plan licensing — only as it touches Wisconsin growers

USDA's List of Approved Hemp Plans (updated 17 February 2026 per Run 1 Part 5) lists three Wisconsin tribes as "Approved Plan (pdf)": the Lac Courte Oreilles Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians, the Lac Du Flambeau Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians, and the Sokaogon Chippewa Community. Wisconsin itself is listed as "USDA Hemp Producer License." Source: USDA AMS List of Approved Hemp Plans, ams.usda.gov — state and tribal plans — accessed 28 October 2026. Practical effect: a grower producing on land within a tribe's approved-plan jurisdiction must be licensed under that tribe's plan, not the USDA plan. Lac Courte Oreilles' Industrial Hemp Code, chapter CSV.8, establishes its Agricultural Division as regulator for hemp on lots within tribal jurisdiction and authorises submission to USDA for approval as its hemp regulatory plan (see Part 10). Other Wisconsin tribes' hemp-plan status beyond the three listed above, and any tribal licence fees, application forms, or tribal producer lists, were not verified in Run 2 — [Verify/Unavailable]; see Part 10.

7.6 Current licences for hemp product businesses: there is no hemp-specific one

No Wisconsin hemp-specific retail, manufacturing, distribution, or handler licence is in force. A hemp product business uses the general licences that apply to any comparable business, as inventoried in Run 1 Part 4: DOR seller's permit; DATCP food processing plant licence under Wis. Stat. § 97.29 / ATCP 70 (for hemp foods); DATCP retail food licence under § 97.30 / ATCP 75; DATCP food warehouse licence under § 97.27 / ATCP 71; DOR tobacco/vapor distributor permit, Form CTV-200 (for vapor products); Wis. Stat. § 995.15 electronic vaping device directory (for vaping devices). Sources: Run 1 Part 4; docs.legis.wisconsin.gov, Wis. Stat. §§ 97.27, 97.29, 97.30, 995.15 — accessed 28 October 2026. Fees, terms, and renewal periods for those general licences are as stated in Run 1 Part 4 and were not re-priced in Run 2; do not carry a hemp-specific fee into this file.

"Handler" licence: no current Wisconsin licence type called a "hemp handler licence" was located. Under the historic DATCP regime, handling was folded into grower/processor licensing; under USDA, handling by the producer is within the producer licence and post-harvest handling of compliant lots enters the stream of commerce (7 CFR 990.26). If this file uses the word "handler," it must say no such standalone current licence exists — [Verify] once more against the current DATCP hemp page before publication, since absence-of-licence findings are the easiest to get wrong.

7.7 Historic DATCP licences (2018–2021) — HISTORIC ONLY, ended 1 January 2022

A DATCP licence was a one-time licence for the duration of the research programme, plus an annual registration expiring 31 December each year. A licence holder needed both a licence and a current annual registration to grow or process in a given year. A grower with a current annual registration could process hemp they grew without an additional processor licence and registration. Source: EmR2111 ATCP 22 / DATCP hemp programme materials — accessed 28 October 2026.

Historic itemFee located
Grower licence, one-time$150 for 0–30 acres; $5 per acre for 31 or more acres, up to $1,000
Grower annual registration$350
Processor annual registration$100; $0 in the year the processor first obtains a licence
Sampling/testing$250 per sample
Sampling/testing of a remediated lot, or resampling/retesting a remediated lot (added by EmR2111)$300
Retesting where resampling is not required (EmR2111)$200
Licence amendment$50 per form, plus $5 per additional acre up to $850 in the amendment rule text located

Historic processor licence fee conflict — DO NOT publish a single figure: EmR2111 fiscal material and secondary reproductions state a $150 processor licence fee, while a 2018 DATCP news release states processors needed a one-time licence "at no cost." This conflict is unresolved; carry both figures with a [Verify] flag or omit the historic processor licence fee. Historic DATCP information links (archival): hemp.wi.gov and datcp.wi.gov — Hemp Data — accessed 28 October 2026. The live DATCP hemp page is now a transition/archival page, not an application portal.

7.8 Sampling-agent rules as they apply to growers (current USDA regime)

To sample USDA-licensed producers, a person must: take USDA Sampling Agent Training; upload the certificate of completion into HeMP; and receive USDA verification. The official certification then contains a unique sampling-agent ID number. USDA-licensed producers can view contact information for certified sampling agents on the AMS website. Producers may not sample their own growing facilities and may not act as sampling agents. Source: 7 CFR 990.24; USDA AMS sampling-agent guidance, ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp — accessed 28 October 2026. [Verify]: the direct URL of the public sampling-agent list was not captured verbatim in Run 2; link to the AMS hemp hub rather than a guessed sub-URL.

7.9 Registries — cross-reference to Part 5 (not duplicated)

  • Current USDA producer licences: USDA AMS / HeMP (no public full Wisconsin producer list was verified in Run 2 — [Verify] whether AMS publishes a searchable Wisconsin producer list; do not claim one exists).
  • USDA approved state/tribal plans: ams.usda.gov — state and tribal plans — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • USDA DEA-registered / hemp analytical testing laboratories directory: ams.usda.gov — DEA laboratories — accessed 28 October 2026 (see Part 11 §11.1.4).
  • Historic DATCP registrant data: datcp.wi.gov — Hemp Data — accessed 28 October 2026 (2018–21 data; records-retention duty of 3 years noted in §7.1).
  • Full registry inventory (licence counts, local lists, conflicts such as USDA producers 470 per the Governor, November 2025, vs 298 per a secondary source): Run 1 Part 5 — cross-referenced, not repeated here.

PART 8 — CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES IN WISCONSIN

8.0 Headline finding — read this first

No verified, currently listed, fee-simple Wisconsin property expressly zoned or marketed for hemp cultivation, hemp processing, or hemp-product retail — with address, zoning type, and price all confirmed from the listing itself — was found in Run 2. That is not a claim that no such property exists anywhere in Wisconsin. It is the result of the searches listed in §8.4. The structural reason the count is thin is stated in Run 1 Part 4.1 and confirmed by the 420Property Wisconsin page text itself: Wisconsin has no state medical or adult-use cannabis programme and therefore no state cannabis licence to attach to a property. The 420Property Wisconsin page text returned in search results says exactly that: "Wisconsin has no medical or adult-use cannabis program — a persistent legislative stalemate despite legal markets in Illinois, Michigan, and Minnesota drawing Wisconsin dollars across every border… Hemp is what is legal and listed here." Source: 420property.com/wisconsin — search-snippet only, page not directly opened, accessed 28 October 2026.

Consequence: Wisconsin hemp land and commercial space normally trades as ordinary farmland or ordinary commercial/industrial space, not as "cannabis-zoned" listings. A general warehouse or farm for sale in Wisconsin is not counted here unless the listing itself markets it for hemp/cannabis use or states cannabis-relevant zoning. That rule is why the long exclusion list in §8.3 exists.

No other Wisconsin-address 420Property listing was surfaced in Run 2. The other 420Property listings returned in the same searches — Brooklyn, NY dispensary; Los Angeles, CA micro-business space; Central NJ cultivation; Forest Grove, OR grow facilities; Dimondale/Lansing, MI industrial lease; Warren, MI municipal-approved retailer facility ($1,500,000, C-2 zoning, 4,425 sq ft, 1.41 acres, listed/updated 22 October 2026, source 420property.com — Warren listing) — are out of state and excluded. A 420Property "Class C recreational license" turnkey cultivation listing (12,500 acres, industrial zoning, 800 amps, source 420property.com — turnkey cultivation listing) states no Wisconsin location in the snippet and is excluded; "Class C" is a Michigan licence class, not a Wisconsin one, and must not be assigned to Wisconsin.

8.3 Excluded results — searched, found, and deliberately NOT counted

  • BizTrader $300,000 retail listing — NOT attributed to Wisconsin. BizTrader page biztrader.com — retail store for sale shows: business-for-sale type "Cannabis, CBD, & Hemp Retail Stores & Dispensaries", price $300,000, listed/updated 27 October 2026, status ACTIVE, year established 2020, 6 employees, inventory not included, FF&E included, leased location at $7,800 rent plus $500 NNN, "One of 2 licenses in town" and "80k monthly." No Wisconsin location is shown in the snippet. Wisconsin has no town cannabis licence of that kind, which further argues against attributing it to Wisconsin. It is a business sale in any case, not a real-estate sale. Accessed 28 October 2026.
  • 420Property locality pages with no listing found (search snippets, pages not directly opened): Sparta, WI — 420property.com — Sparta — "We couldn't find a listing for your search criteria." Chilton, WI — 420property.com — Chilton — same no-listing result. Oconto, WI — 420property.com — Oconto — same no-listing result. Accessed 28 October 2026. These are negative results, not listings.
  • Crexi general commercial pages — NOT cannabis-zoned: 5373 US-10 E, Stevens Point, WI 54482, and 1735 Merrill Ave, Wausau, WI 54401-2475, appeared as general commercial/ownership pages with no evidence in the snippets that either is zoned or marketed for hemp cultivation, processing, or hemp retail. Not counted.
  • LoopNet general Wisconsin warehouses — NOT cannabis-zoned: LoopNet's Wisconsin warehouses-for-sale page reports 196 warehouse listings for sale in Wisconsin (source loopnet.com — Wisconsin warehouses for sale — accessed 28 October 2026). Those are general warehouses, not hemp/cannabis-marketed properties, and none is individually identified here as cannabis-zoned. An earlier LoopNet result in Run 2 showing "0 Hemp Farms For Sale" used a Canada search URL (loopnet.com — Canada hemp farms) and proves nothing about Wisconsin; it is not quoted as a Wisconsin result.
  • General Wisconsin commercial/land results — NOT cannabis-zoned: e.g. 4800 W Mitchell St, West Milwaukee, WI (Village of West Milwaukee, Milwaukee County), M-2 General Manufacturing, 372,070 sq ft — a general industrial listing with no hemp/cannabis marketing in the snippet; 726 S High Point Rd, Madison, WI 53719 (City of Madison, Dane County), 10-acre agricultural vacant land — no hemp marketing in the snippet. Neither is counted.
  • Operating hemp shops are not for-sale listings: e.g. Ignite Dispensary, 1550 N Water St, Milwaukee, WI 53202 (City of Milwaukee, Milwaukee County), and other shop-directory entries prove shops exist; they do not prove a property is listed for sale or lease and are not converted into Part 8 listings.

  1. "420Property Wisconsin hemp cultivation property for sale" — one Wisconsin-address entry (A-1) + out-of-state listings + Sparta/Chilton/Oconto no-listing pages.
  2. "Wisconsin hemp farm property for sale LoopNet Crexi cannabis" — LoopNet Canada-path "0" result (discounted, see §8.3); Crexi general commercial pages (discounted).
  3. "2903 West Clybourn Milwaukee hemp distribution 420 Property price" — A-1 only; no price, zoning, size, or tenure found.
  4. "Wisconsin hemp business for sale Milwaukee Madison Green Bay dispensary shop" — BizBuySell Wausau asset sale (B-1); BizTrader $300,000 unlocated listing (excluded); shop directories (not listings).
  5. "420Property Wisconsin Milwaukee Madison Brillion hemp land for sale lease listings" — no additional Wisconsin 420Property listing; returned only the Warren, MI listing, general Brillion/Sauk City residential pages, and general commercial pages (all excluded).
  6. "LoopNet Wisconsin cannabis warehouse industrial for sale Milwaukee hemp" — 420Property Wisconsin page text (A-1 confirmed as the only Wisconsin entry on that page in the snippet) + LoopNet 196 general Wisconsin warehouses (not cannabis-marketed) + general West Milwaukee industrial listing (excluded).
  7. "Wisconsin hemp business for sale CBD store business sale Milwaukee Madison price" — B-1 price conflict identified; no additional verified Wisconsin hemp business sale with a confirmed price.
  8. Prior-slice searches (carried forward): general Realtor/land and commercial searches returned no Wisconsin property expressly zoned or marketed for hemp cultivation, processing, or retail with address + zoning + price confirmed. No Wisconsin-filtered Crexi hemp-specific count was established; that remains a gap.

Part 8 conclusion, stated explicitly: on the searches above, Wisconsin has one surfaced 420Property entry with a Wisconsin address (2903 West Clybourn Street, City of Milwaukee, Milwaukee County), with price, zoning, size, tenure, and listing date all Verify; one Wisconsin hemp-retail business-asset sale (Wausau) with a price conflict; one historic 2019 Monroe County purchase agreement; and zero verified, currently listed, fee-simple properties expressly zoned or marketed for hemp cultivation, processing, or hemp retail with all of address, zoning, and price confirmed from the listing itself.

PART 9 — REGULATORS AND HISTORY

9.1 State agencies with a role in cannabis and hemp

No single Wisconsin agency licenses medical or adult-use cannabis, because no such programme exists (Run 1 Part 4.1). Roles below are therefore: (a) historic hemp licensing, (b) tax, (c) criminal-law enforcement and scheduling, (d) health, (e) general business/financial oversight, and (f) election administration. Agency public contacts only; no personal phone or email of any individual is reproduced. Where a page was reached only via search snippet, that is noted.

Local administrators also matter for §9.3: municipal clerks run local elections and hold local canvasses; county clerks hold county canvasses. No official compiled list of local cannabis ordinances was found in Run 1 or in Run 2.

Bills that passed one chamber — direct answer. In the 2025–26 session, no cannabis or hemp-regulation bill passed either the Assembly or the Senate. The three bills checked reached only these stages, verified as above: SB 534 — committee passage recommended 4–1 on 5 February 2026, no floor vote; AB 606 — committee vote 12 November 2025 (7–3, report 20 November 2025), referred to Rules, no floor passage; SB 682 — reported without recommendation 4–4 on 3 February 2026, no Senate passage. A pre-2025 chamber-passage check (e.g. whether any earlier medical bill ever passed one chamber) was not completed in Run 2 and is Unavailable — do not assert that no bill ever passed a chamber in Wisconsin history.

Governor's budget cannabis proposals — summary. Evers included cannabis provisions in the 2019–21, 2021–23, 2023–25, and 2025–27 executive budgets; each time the Legislature/Joint Finance Committee removed them (2023: reported 12–4 JFC vote, 2 May 2023; 2025: 8 May 2025 removal among ~600 provisions). All four cycles are secondary reporting in Run 2 except where an official bill number is given (AB 50 / SB 45 for 2025–27). Revenue figures ($44.4m + $10.2m for 2023; $58.1m for 2025–27) are executive estimates, not collected revenue.

9.3 Ballot history

9.3.1 Wisconsin has no statewide citizen initiative and no statewide veto referendum — confirmed.

  • Only the Wisconsin Legislature can place a ballot measure to amend the Wisconsin Constitution on statewide ballots, by joint resolution approved by a simple majority of each chamber during two consecutive legislative sessions, followed by voter ratification by simple majority. Source: Ballotpedia News, 12 February 2025, "Wisconsin voters have decided on 257 ballot measures since statehood," news.ballotpedia.org — Wisconsin ballot measures — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Governor's press release (same position): "Unlike in other states, Wisconsinites do not have the power to propose new laws or constitutional amendments through a binding ballot initiative process" and "only the Legislature can approve and place ballot questions on the ballot." Source: content.govdelivery.com — Governor's bulletin — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Historical rejection: in 1914 Wisconsin voters defeated an amendment that would have established the initiative and referendum process, with 63.62% opposing. A separate initiative-institute history states the amendment passed both houses in the 1911–1912 and 1913–1914 legislatures and was placed on the November 1914 ballot. Source: initiativeandreferenduminstitute.org — Wisconsin — accessed 28 October 2026. The recall process was later adopted in 1926 (Ballotpedia, same source) — recall is not initiative and does not create a cannabis ballot route.
  • Consequence: no statewide cannabis ballot measure has ever been placed by citizens in Wisconsin, because no citizen-initiative route exists. No binding statewide cannabis question appears in Run 1 or in Run 2 searches.
  • What citizens can do instead: local advisory referendum petitions at municipal and county level, which are non-binding. A WEC-related explainer quoted in search results calls them advisory questions that "give elected officials a sense of where the citizens are" but "the result of the vote does not bind the officials," and notes WEC receives inquiries about petitions for local advisory referenda. That explainer is secondary for WEC practice; the official WEC position itself was not opened in Run 2 — Verify.

9.3.2 Full advisory referendum record — cross-reference to Run 1 Part 3.3, percentages unchanged.

All percentages below are secondary until county/municipal clerk canvasses are opened. Raw votes for several counties are in Run 1 Part 3.3.2. Turnout for the 2018 local questions is Unavailable in Run 1. Never present any advisory result below as law.

  • Pre-2018 context (secondary, question text and raw votes Verify unless noted): Dane County, November 2010, medical, 159,408 Yes, 75.49% Yes. City of River Falls (Pierce + St. Croix Counties), 68% Yes. Dane County, 2014, adult-use, 64.5% Yes.
  • 6 November 2018 — 16 counties + 2 cities, 18 questions, all passed (secondary): Adult-use: Dane County 76.4%; Milwaukee County 70%; Racine County 59% (60.2% in a range summary — conflict, Verify); Rock County 69%; La Crosse County 63%; Eau Claire County three-option 54% recreational / 31% prescription-only / 15% illegal; City of Racine (Racine County) recreational 66%, medical 88%, tax 83%, decriminalisation 72%. Medical-only: Brown County 75% (76% in one report — conflict, Verify); Clark County 67.1%; Forest County 78% (79% in one report — conflict, Verify); Kenosha County 88.5%; Langlade County 77%; Lincoln County 81%; Marathon County 82%; Marquette County 78%; Portage County 83%; Sauk County 80%; Racine County medical 85%; City of Waukesha (Waukesha County) 77%. Dane and Milwaukee raw-vote totals conflict between secondary sources — Verify. Wikipedia's summary range for 2018 (medical 67.1% Clark to 88.5% Kenosha; recreational 60.2% Racine to 76.4% Dane; 16 counties accounting for over half the state's population) is consistent with the above except for the Racine 59% vs 60.2% point. Source: en.wikipedia.org — Cannabis law in Wisconsin — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Spring 2019 (April 2019 election) — 1 county + 2 municipalities, 6 questions, 5 passed / 1 failed (secondary, clerk canvasses Verify): Wood County medical approved 71–29%; Wood County adult-use failed 40–60% (the only failed cannabis advisory question in the record); Village of Egg Harbor (Door County) medical approved 82–18%, personal use approved 55–45%; City of Sturgeon Bay (Door County) medical approved 74–26%, personal use approved 51–49%.
  • 8 November 2022 — 3 counties + 5 municipalities, all adult-use, all passed (secondary, raw votes, turnout, and clerk canvasses Verify): Dane County Q1 82%; Dane County Q2 (expungement) approved, percentage Verify; Eau Claire County 69%; Milwaukee County 74%; City of Appleton (Outagamie County) approved, percentage Verify; City of Kenosha (Kenosha County) 72%; City of Racine (Racine County) 76%; City of Stevens Point (Portage County) approved, percentage Verify; City of Superior (Douglas County) approved, percentage Verify. Wikipedia's range summary (69% Eau Claire to 82% Dane) is consistent. Source: en.wikipedia.org — Cannabis law in Wisconsin — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • 2020, 2024, and later: no county or municipal cannabis advisory referendum was located in Run 1 searches. Absence is not proven — a WEC / county-clerk search is still required before stating "none" as fact.
  • Excluded from this count: the August 2015 Menominee tribal vote (§9.2) belongs to Part 10, not to the Wisconsin advisory record.

9.4 Public opinion polling — Marquette Law School Poll, most recent first

All polling below is secondary unless the official Marquette topline is opened; no official Marquette topline was directly opened in Run 2. Poll support is not law and is not a referendum result. Question wording, sample, and mode are flagged Verify wherever they were not verified from the official release. No Marquette poll after June 2025 was located in Run 2; no Marquette poll on hemp-derived THC, delta-8, or THC beverages specifically was located; no non-Marquette statewide poll with a Wisconsin sample and published methodology was verified in Run 2 — all Unavailable/Verify, not filled with national polls.

Most recent — June 2025 (released 25 June 2025).

  • Pollster: Marquette Law School Poll. Field dates: 13–19 June 2025. Population: Wisconsin registered voters. Sample: n=873. Margin of error: ±4.7 percentage points (as reported).
  • Adult-use question wording (from the June 2025 press-release copy): "Do you think the use of marijuana should be made legal, or not?"
  • Result: 67% favour legalising marijuana, 33% opposed (0% don't know in the trend table).
  • Party breakdown: Democrats 88% support; independents 79% support; Republicans 44% support / 56% oppose.
  • Trend claim in the same reporting: support has generally increased since Marquette first tracked the question in 2013, up 17 percentage points from the 2013 result, and has remained above 60% since 2022.
  • Same June 2025 poll, separate question — citizen-led ballot initiative process: 77% support in a secondary summary, with party splits Republicans 68%, independents 79%, Democrats 85% (from press-release copy). This is a separate question from marijuana legalisation and must not be conflated with it. Overall 77% figure is secondary — Verify against the official topline.
  • Mode (phone/online/mixed) for June 2025 was not verified in Run 2 — Verify.
  • Sources: Marijuana Moment, 25 June 2025, marijuanamoment.net — June 2025 poll; MPP poll table, mpp.org — polls; June 2025 press-release trend table (mirror copy — official Marquette topline Verify), MLSP85 press release (mirror) — all accessed 28 October 2026.

Full Marquette recreational-legalisation trend (June 2025 press-release Table 9, "Among registered voters," Yes / No / Don't Know): 10/21–24/13: 50 / 45 / 5. 3/20–23/14: 42 / 52 / 6. 9/11–14/14: 46 / 51 / 2. 1/16–20/19: 59 / 35 / 7. 4/3–7/19: 59 / 36 / 4. 2/22–27/22: 61 / 31 / 7. 8/10–15/22: 69 / 23 / 8. 10/3–9/22: 64 / 30 / 6. 1/24–31/24: 63 / 29 / 7. 6/13–19/25: 67 / 33 / 0. Do not smooth these into a single trend line without noting different poll dates, samples, and possible question-wording differences, none of which have been verified from official toplines for the earlier dates in Run 2. Note the table lists no February 2025 row for the recreational question — see the conflict below.

February 2025 — CONFLICT PRESERVED, not resolved by picking one figure silently.

  • One secondary source (Wikipedia summary) states: "In February 2025, a Marquette Law School Poll showed that 88% of Wisconsinites supported legalizing medical marijuana" and "63% … supported legalizing recreational marijuana." Source: en.wikipedia.org — Cannabis law in Wisconsin — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Two other secondary sources state 86% for medical: MVS Legal, "a February 2025 survey conducted by Marquette University Law School … 63% … supported adult recreational use, and 86% supported medical cannabis legalization" (milwaukee-criminal-lawyer.com); and Marijuana Moment reporting a Marquette poll released "this month" with 86% medical (Democrats 95%, independents 84%, Republicans 78%) and 63% complete legalisation (marijuanamoment.net — February 2025 poll) — both accessed 28 October 2026.
  • The recreational figure, 63%, is consistent across those sources. The medical figure is 88% vs 86% — unresolved. Sample, field dates, and exact question wording for any February 2025 poll were not verified from the official Marquette release in Run 2 — Verify. The absence of a February 2025 row in the June 2025 Table 9 recreational trend adds to the uncertainty: the February 2025 reporting may conflate the January 2024 poll (below) with a February 2025 release. Do not publish a February 2025 topline until the official Marquette February 2025 release is opened.

January 2024 (field dates 24–31 January 2024, per Table 9).

Recreational: 63% support (29% no, 7% don't know, per Table 9). Medical: 86% said medical marijuana should be legal in Wisconsin with a doctor's prescription — secondary reporting (Gannett, January 2025, and Volume One, 21 January 2025, describing a poll "conducted last January"). That reporting states the medical question had been asked only twice in the poll's history to that point, and that 2019 medical support was 83%. Sample, margin of error, and exact medical question wording are Verify against the official topline. Sources: volumeone.org — marijuana movement and Gannett mirror Gannett — Wisconsin marijuana legalization — accessed 28 October 2026.

2022 (three polls in Table 9 — do not collapse into one number).

February 22–27, 2022: 61% Yes. August 10–15, 2022: 69% Yes — this is the figure cited by ACLU of Wisconsin as a 2022 Marquette Law School study ("69% of Wisconsin registered voters supported legalising recreational weed, including 51% of Wisconsin Republicans, 53% of Wisconsinites over age 60, and majorities in every racial, regional, income, and educational demographic in the survey," as reported by ACLU-WI — secondary, sample and question wording Verify). October 3–9, 2022: 64% Yes. The 69% August 2022 figure is higher than the 63% figures in January 2024 and (reported) February 2025 and the 67% figure in June 2025. Different dates/samples explain the movement; do not present 69% as the current figure.

2019, 2014, 2013 — baseline points, all secondary and Verify.

2019: recreational 59% (January 16–20, 2019) and 59% (April 3–7, 2019), per Table 9; medical 83%, per January 2024 secondary reporting. 2014: recreational as low as 42% (March 20–23, 2014, per Table 9: 42 / 52 / 6) and 46% (September 11–14, 2014). The Gannett "as low as 42% in a 2014 poll" line matches the March 2014 row. 2013: first tracking year, 50% Yes (October 21–24, 2013, per Table 9: 50 / 45 / 5). The June 2025 "17-point rise" claim (67 − 50 = 17) is arithmetically consistent with this row. The 2013 figure, wording, and sample remain Verify against the official 2013 topline before being asserted independently of Table 9.

PART 10 — TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10.0 Headline answers (read first)

  1. No Wisconsin tribe has a verified state-style medical-marijuana or adult-use marijuana sales programme. The verified tribal activity is (a) hemp regulation and hemp/CBD retail under tribal hemp plans/ordinances, (b) one tribal decriminalisation (Ho-Chunk, tribal-police non-enforcement only), and (c) one historic, litigated hemp crop (Menominee, 2015, destroyed, lawsuit dismissed).
  2. No verified Wisconsin tribal medical programme exists to which the federal "state-licensed" question could attach. That question is therefore Verify — and, on current evidence, moot in Wisconsin (see §10.5). Do not state that any tribal programme IS or IS NOT "state-licensed" under the split-scheduling baseline without a federal source; none was located.
  3. No Wisconsin–Illinois, Wisconsin–Michigan, Wisconsin–Minnesota, or Wisconsin–tribal cannabis/hemp compact or MOU was located. Gaming compacts with all eleven tribes are NOT cannabis/hemp compacts (see §10.7).
  4. Nothing a Wisconsin resident lawfully buys as marijuana in Illinois, Michigan or Minnesota becomes lawful by bringing it into Wisconsin. Marijuana remains illegal to possess in Wisconsin regardless of lawful purchase next door, and interstate transport of marijuana is a federal offence (see §10.6).
  5. Two direct-text contradictions the reader must resolve before publishing Part 10 (do not publish a single rule on either): Lac du Flambeau buyer age: the ordinance text as opened says under 18; Little Pines reporting says the store forbids sale under 21. Lac du Flambeau product cap: §95.308(7) says a licensee "may not sell Industrial Hemp Products that contain 0.3% or greater THC content," yet secondary reporting describes Little Pines selling hemp-derived THC products. Whether compliance rests on a dry-weight calculation, a later Authority regulation, or a different product definition is Verify.

10.1 The eleven federally recognised tribes (official list)

Wisconsin DOA, Tribal Compacts page, opened directly: "The State of Wisconsin has entered into gaming compact agreements with all eleven (11) federally recognized Tribes of Indians in Wisconsin, as permitted by the Indian Gaming Regulatory Act of 1988, 25 U.S.C. sec. 2701 (Act)." The eleven names, exactly as on that DOA page (accessed 28 October 2026): (1) Bad River Band of the Lake Superior Tribe of Chippewa Indians; (2) Forest County Potawatomi Community of Wisconsin; (3) Ho-Chunk Nation; (4) Lac Courte Oreilles Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians; (5) Lac du Flambeau Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians; (6) Menominee Indian Tribe of Wisconsin; (7) Oneida Nation; (8) Red Cliff Band of Lake Superior Chippewas; (9) Sokaogon Chippewa Community; (10) St. Croix Chippewa Indians of Wisconsin; (11) Stockbridge-Munsee Community. Source: doa.wi.gov — Tribal Compacts — accessed 28 October 2026.

Spelling warning: other sources use variants ("Red Cliff Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians," "Stockbridge-Munsee Band of Mohican Indians," "St. Croix Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians," "Oneida Tribe of Indians of Wisconsin" as a former name). Reconcile to the DOA list above; do not silently merge variants.
