10.2 USDA approved tribal hemp plans — current status (Run 1 §5.5 check)

Run 1 position (preserved): Lac Courte Oreilles, Lac du Flambeau and Sokaogon each listed as "Approved Plan (pdf)*" (* = approved under the final rule effective 22 March 2021), USDA list page stated "Updated February 17, 2026." New check: search-index text for the USDA list page still shows all three as "Approved Plan (pdf)*" — consistent with Run 1. Source: ams.usda.gov — hemp state and tribal plan review — accessed 28 October 2026 (via search index; see method note below).

Method note / honesty flag: a direct open of that USDA list URL in Run 2 failed with a network-grant error and, per instruction, that exact request was NOT repeated. The plan status above therefore rests on search-index text plus Run 1, not on a fresh direct open. Mark it accordingly.

Conflict to flag, not resolve: a CSBS (Conference of State Bank Supervisors) reproduction of USDA plan data dated May 2026 showed Lac du Flambeau as "Under Review" while showing other tribes as approved. That conflicts with both Run 1 and the current USDA search-index text for Lac du Flambeau. Possible explanations (stale reproduction, plan amendment under review, or index lag) cannot be distinguished without the live USDA page. Do not publish Lac du Flambeau's plan status as unqualifiedly settled until the live USDA list is opened by an eligible route.

What a plan does and does not prove: a USDA-approved tribal hemp plan proves the tribe may act as the primary regulator of hemp production in its territory under the 2018 Farm Bill framework. It does NOT by itself prove a retail operation, a marijuana programme, or any particular buyer rule. No tribe in this section is credited with an operation on plan status alone.

10.3 Tribe-by-tribe detail

10.3.1 Bad River Band of the Lake Superior Tribe of Chippewa Indians

No tribal-government cannabis/hemp ordinance, regulator, sales operation or buyer rule was verified. Do not attribute Canndigenous / Ripley Green to the tribal government. Rob Pero, a member of the Bad River Band (and of the Oneida Nation, per ICIA reporting), founded Canndigenous, described in secondary reporting as Wisconsin's first independent Indigenous-owned hemp company, and Ripley Green apothecary in Cambridge, Wisconsin — not on Bad River tribal land as described. Canndigenous scaled a 10-acre hemp grow in 2020 and sells CBD/hemp goods sourced from roughly 10 Wisconsin farms (secondary, Cannabis Law Report / Madison.com summary). In 2022 Canndigenous became the first Indigenous-owned entity to receive a USDA Climate-Smart Commodities grant, worth $15 million (secondary, Money Times / ICIA announcement summary). These are private-business facts, not Bad River tribal law. Advice-seeking only: Milwaukee Journal Sentinel, 15 December 2025, reported the Bad River Ojibwe Tribe "recently sought legal advice for possibly pursuing a cannabis business on its reservation in far northern Wisconsin." That is advice-seeking, NOT an operation, ordinance or regulator. Source: jsonline.com — tribal nations and federal THC hemp ban — accessed 28 October 2026. Bad River is listed among ICIA member tribes in secondary reporting. ICIA membership alone is not an operation.

10.3.2 Forest County Potawatomi Community of Wisconsin

No cannabis/hemp ordinance, regulator, sales operation or buyer rule was verified from the tribe's code (NARF Tribal Law Gateway index for Forest County Potawatomi was located, but no hemp/cannabis title was identified in it in Run 2). Source: narf.org — Forest County Potawatomi codes — accessed 28 October 2026. Unverified new claim — do not upgrade Run 1: a Buffalo's Fire report of the 25 February 2026 Great Lakes tribal summit at Lake of the Torches states: "Currently, many operations in Wisconsin, including the Lac du Flambeau operation, grow, package, and sell cannabidiol products derived from hemp. The Menominee Nation of Wisconsin, the Oneida Nation of Wisconsin and the Forest County Potawatomi also have hemp growth operations." Source: buffalosfire.com — Great Lakes tribal summit — accessed 28 October 2026. Treatment: this is a single secondary summit report with no ordinance, licence, acreage or operator detail for Forest County Potawatomi (or for Menominee/Oneida in the same sentence). It is recorded here as an unverified assertion, and does NOT change the "no verified operation" finding for any of the three tribes. The parent should not publish it as a verified operation without a tribal or USDA source. Same-name warning: do NOT confuse Forest County Potawatomi Community of Wisconsin with the Prairie Band Potawatomi Nation (Kansas), whose Title 32 Hemp Legalization and Control Ordinance appears in search results. That is a different tribe in a different state.

10.3.3 Ho-Chunk Nation — decriminalisation only, NOT legalisation or sales

What happened (secondary, multiple outlets, consistent): on 30 April 2024 the Ho-Chunk tribal legislature voted to decriminalise cannabis on tribal lands; Ho-Chunk police will not issue citations for possession. Tribal Business News (2024) and Wisconsin Public Radio (7 May 2024, via Central Wisconsin Broadcasting) agree on this core fact. Sources: tribalbusinessnews.com — Ho-Chunk decriminalizes and cwbradio.com — Ho-Chunk news — accessed 28 October 2026. What it is not: it is NOT full legalisation and NOT a medical or adult-use sales programme. No Ho-Chunk cannabis/hemp sales operation, sales regulator or buyer rule was verified. Tribal statement (secondary quotation): "The Ho-Chunk Nation recognizes that marijuana and its derivatives are natural growth plants with medicinal and industrial applications… Indigenous people have used marijuana and hemp for hundreds of years for a variety of purposes and the Ho-Chunk Nation acknowledges its functional purpose." (Ho-Chunk statement via Cannabis Law Report, May 2024.) Source: cannabislaw.report — Ho-Chunk decriminalizes — accessed 28 October 2026. Limits (secondary legal analysis, labelled as such): cannabis "remains illegal under state law and federal law" (Marquette professor Scott Idleman, quoted May 2024); an FAQ distributed within the Ho-Chunk Nation indicates county or state police could still issue citations (Rob Pero, quoted). One secondary summary adds that cannabis "remains illegal under tribal law" notwithstanding tribal-police non-enforcement — i.e. decriminalisation here means non-enforcement by tribal police, not a tribal right to possess/sell. The Husch Blackwell analysis (JD Supra, 2024) frames the same point as a direct conflict between tribal decriminalisation and Wisconsin's categorical ban, under Public Law 280 (see §10.4). Source: jdsupra.com — Ho-Chunk Nation — accessed 28 October 2026. Verbatim ordinance text: NOT obtained. A Red Cliff tribal background document states: "Ho-Chunk Nation voted in favor to reverse marijuana prohibition in 2015 but legalization was not enacted. On April 30, 2024, they removed cannabis from their Criminal Code." That is a third party's summary, not Ho-Chunk code text. Verify the Ho-Chunk Criminal Code section and the 2024 amending resolution before quoting any removal as verbatim law. Do not quote the Red Cliff sentence as Ho-Chunk law. Forward-looking statements only: Legislator Kristin White Eagle (District 2) was quoted on job creation, revenue for tribal programmes, medicinal benefits including opioid addiction, and hempcrete/housing possibilities; legalisation for medicinal use was described as "a longer way off"; ICIA advice quoted was to start with industrial hemp. The tribe began considering a cannabis industry in 2015 (White Eagle, via Tribal Business News). None of this is an enacted programme.

10.3.4 Lac Courte Oreilles Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians — hemp code verified; retail/medical NOT verified

Ordinance (tribal law library, opened directly): Chapter CSV.8, "Industrial Hemp Code of the Lac Courte Oreilles Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians," as amended by Resolution No. 2024-07 to comply with USDA final review. Source: law.lco-nsn.gov — Resolution 2024-07 — accessed 28 October 2026. NARF copy: narf.org — LCO Resolution 2024-07 (PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026.

Verbatim — authority, CSV.8.1.020: "This Ordinance is enacted pursuant to the inherent sovereign authority of the Lac Courte Oreilles Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians. In the implementation of this inherent sovereign authority, Article V, § 1 (h) and (n) of the Amended Constitution and Bylaws of the Lac Courte Oreilles Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians, empowers the Tribal Governing Board to: 'To engage in any business that will further the social or economic wellbeing of members of the Band or undertake any programs or projects designed for the economic advancement of the people,' Article V, § 1 (h); and 'To regulate, by enactment of ordinances, the conduct of business within the territory of the Band, including the power to impose taxes or license fees upon members and nonmembers doing business within the reservation' Article V, § 1 (n)."
Verbatim — policy, CSV.8.1.030(a): the policy is to "(1) Establish and grant regulatory authority to the Lac Courte Oreilles Agricultural Division to serve as a governmental instrumentality and arm of the Tribe for the regulation of Hemp on lots within the jurisdiction of the Tribe; (2) Allow persons to plant, produce, harvest, sample, test, process, transport, transfer, take possession of, sell, import, and export Hemp on lots within the jurisdiction of the Tribe to the greatest extent allowed under federal law and to comply with the 2018 Farm Bill; (3) Treat the production and processing of Hemp on lots within the jurisdiction of the Tribe as an agricultural commodity under the laws of the Tribe; and, (4) Provide an additional source of revenue and an economic opportunity for Tribe and its members."
Verbatim — hemp definition, CSV.8.2.010(r) (opened directly): "'Hemp' means the plant species Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a total Delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis." Source: law.lco-nsn.gov — CSV.8.2.010 — accessed 28 October 2026.
Verbatim — regulator, CSV.8.2.010(b): "'Agricultural Division' or 'Division' means the Agricultural Division of the Lac Courte Oreilles Conservation Department as established by the Tribal Governing Board through this ordinance which shall serve as a governmental instrumentality and arm of the Tribe."
Verbatim — licensing, CSV.8.5.010 (search-index text of tribal law library): "(a) Any person or Hemp Business, including a Tribally Owned Corporation, who wishes to produce or process Hemp at any lot within the Tribe's jurisdiction, shall submit to the Director of the Agricultural Division a completed application for a Production Licensing Agreement and/or Processor Licensing Agreement. (b) Tribal preference in licensing shall be given to Tribally Owned Corporation(s) and to Lac Courte Oreilles citizens (members). … (e) No person may cultivate, dry, harvest or store Hemp at any lot within the jurisdiction of the Tribe without a Production Licensing Agreement."
Verbatim — prohibited acts, CSV.8.8.010: "(a) A person who does not hold a Production Licensing Agreement from the Agricultural Division shall not process Hemp at any lot within the jurisdiction of the Tribe. … (d) No person may produce, possess, process, transport, or assist in the cultivation, processing or transport of any Cannabis sativa L. with a total Delta-9-THC concentration in excess of three-tenths of one percent (0.3 %) on a dry weight basis." Source: narf.org — LCO CSV.8.8.010 — accessed 28 October 2026.

Operation: no LCO retail dispensary, medical programme or adult-use sales channel was verified. The only operation-level material located is secondary: Lac Courte Oreilles Ojibwe University farm has grown hemp for research in recent years, with a natural-science course on medicinal/research use planned, farm focus described as research application only under current permitting/USDA grant parameters, with possible expansion into personal-use hemp plants only with additional permitting via the LCOU Conservation Department; CBD oil extraction with no THC psychoactive effects is described as the eventual aim, not a current medical-marijuana programme. Treat as secondary/unverified for operation status; do not present the university farm as a commercial or medical programme. Who may buy: no retail buyer rule (member vs nonmember, age, on/off-reservation) was located in the code excerpts opened. Verify before stating any buyer rule. Note the code's "tribal preference in licensing" is a producer/processor preference, not a buyer rule.

10.3.5 Lac du Flambeau Band of Lake Superior Chippewa Indians — hemp ordinance + Little Pines verified in outline; two tensions flagged

Ordinance (official tribal PDF, opened directly): Chapter 95, Industrial Hemp Control Ordinance. History note in the document: "Current Ordinance: Adopted June 25, 2019, Resolution No. 231(19). Enacted by referendum July 2, 2019." Revisions approved via Tribal Referendum Election 27 August 2024, results accepted by Tribal Council via Resolution No. 272(24). File as opened is the "2024.09.06 … Comments clean" version. Source: ldftribe.com — Chapter 95 (PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026.

Verbatim — purpose, §95.103: "The purpose of this Ordinance is to regulate and control the Cultivation of Industrial Hemp and the sale of Industrial Hemp Products on the Lac du Flambeau Indian Reservation. Given the State of Wisconsin's decision to regulate Industrial Hemp, the descheduling of Hemp under the Federal Controlled Substance Act, and the enactment of the 2018 Farm Bill, the Tribal Council finds that it is in the Tribe's best interests to enact this Ordinance governing Industrial Hemp Cultivation and the sale of Industrial Hemp Products on the Reservation."
Verbatim — hemp definition, §95.201(9): "'Industrial Hemp' or 'Hemp' means the plant species Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3% on a dry weight basis."
Verbatim — regulator, §§95.201(3), 95.401: "'Authority' means the Tribal Licensing and Regulatory Authority." "The Tribal Licensing and Regulatory Authority ('Authority'), created under Chapter 94 of the Tribal Code, shall be responsible for all licensing and regulatory duties and obligations provided for in this Ordinance." Note the regulator-name conflict: a 2024 secondary summary described licensing "through the Tribal Compliance and Regulatory Commission," while the ordinance text as opened names the Tribal Licensing and Regulatory Authority (Chapter 94). Use the ordinance name; the "Commission" wording is Verify (possible paraphrase or former name).
Verbatim — sales licence, §95.308(1): "Any Person desiring to process, and/or sell Industrial Hemp Products within the exterior boundaries of the Lac du Flambeau Reservation is required to obtain an Industrial Hemp Products Processing and Sales License from the Authority established pursuant to this Ordinance. Any person desiring to import Industrial Hemp Products within the exterior boundaries of the Lac du Flambeau Reservation is required to confirm with all applicable Tribal, state, and federal laws."
Verbatim — product cap, §95.308(7): "The Licensee may not sell Industrial Hemp Products that contain 0.3% or greater THC content." (§95.308(6) requires licensees to keep receipts/certificates certifying the seller is licensed and products "do not contain more than 0.3% or greater THC content" — the ordinance's own wording is awkward; quote §95.308(7) as the operative cap.)
Verbatim — sale restrictions, §95.501: "(1) No retailer, direct marketer, manufacturer, distributor, agent, employee, or independent contractor of a retailer, direct marketer, manufacturer, or distributor may sell or provide for nominal or no consideration Industrial Hemp Products to any person under 18 years of age. (2) [No such person] may sell or provide … Industrial Hemp Products by way of vending machine anywhere on the Lac du Flambeau Reservation. (4) No retailer may sell Industrial Hemp Products on the Lac du Flambeau Reservation in a form other than a package or container on which a stamp or seal approved by the Authority is affixed."
Verbatim — penalties, §95.502(2): "A Person who commits a violation is subject to a forfeiture of: (a) Not more than $500 if the Person has not committed a previous violation within 12 months of the violation; or (b) Not less than $200 nor more than $500 if the Person has committed a previous violation within 12 months of the violation." §95.502(4): "The possession, use, and consumption of Industrial Hemp Products, on the Lac du Flambeau Indian Reservation, that have been licensed and approved by the Tribe, are permitted to the fullest extent of Tribal law."

Cultivation-licence scope nuance: §95.201(4) defines "Business" as "a business incorporated under Tribal law or Section 17 of the Indian Reorganization Act which is wholly-owned by the Tribe," and §95.301(1) requires "Any Business desiring to engage in Industrial Hemp Cultivation and Distribution" to obtain a cultivation licence. The sales licence (§95.308) by contrast applies to "Any Person." Whether non-tribal-owned cultivation is therefore excluded, or is licensed under a different provision, is Verify from the full Chapter 95 text — do not state that any person may cultivate under Chapter 95.

Operation — Little Pines Dispensary (secondary + tribal listing, not ordinance text): tribal enterprises listing describes "Little Pines Dispensary" as "a tribally owned and operated CBD retail store." Address shown: 418 Little Pines Rd, Lac du Flambeau, WI 54538. (Phone number shown in search results is omitted here per the commission rule.) Secondary reporting (Marijuana Moment / ICT, 2024) says Little Pines opened in early July 2024 in a strip mall on tribal lands, opened by the Band's business development corporation, described as the first free-standing tribal business of its kind in Wisconsin. Job postings for Little Pines LLC state: "Little Pines and its vendors have obtained the appropriate sales license from the Tribal Licensing and Regulatory Authority, as required by the tribal ordinance Chapter 95 – Industrial Hemp Control," and that the Band "is approved by the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA) to cultivate and distribute CBD (<0.3% THC), which allows the tribe to self-regulate." Sources: ldftribe.com — tribal enterprises; marijuanamoment.net — Indian tribes and hemp THC — accessed 28 October 2026.

Buyer age — CONFLICT (see §10.0): Director of retail and cultivation Candace Thompson (citizen of Lac du Flambeau Band) is quoted in secondary reporting that "tribal regulations forbid sale of Little Pines products to persons under 21." The ordinance text as opened (§95.501(1)) forbids sale to persons under 18. Possible reconciliations — a later Authority regulation raising the age to 21, a store policy stricter than the ordinance, or a paraphrase error — were NOT verified. Do not publish a single Little Pines age rule until the Authority's current regulations are opened. The same reporting quotes Thompson: "If a state or tribal nation submits a plan to the [USDA] to grow or sell hemp then they are allowed to regulate themselves," and "The state of Wisconsin hasn't done that but the tribe has; we have tribal licensing and regulatory authority," and, on THC enhancement after harvest, "That's the legal loophole."

Who may buy / off-reservation transport: whether non-tribal members may buy at Little Pines, and whether products may lawfully be taken off tribal land, was NOT verified in the ordinance excerpts opened or in the reporting located. The tribal listing phrase "serving our community and beyond" does NOT prove nonmember sales or off-reservation legality. Verify; do not infer. Note §95.502(4) permits possession/use/consumption only "on the Lac du Flambeau Indian Reservation" for tribe-licensed/approved products — that territorial wording cuts against assuming an off-reservation right.

Product-cap tension (see §10.0): the §95.308(7) <0.3% cap sits uneasily with reporting of hemp-derived THC products for sale. How Little Pines products comply (dry-weight percentage vs per-serving milligrams, or a later amendment/regulation) is Verify. Do not describe Chapter 95 as authorising intoxicating-THC sales beyond its text.

Decriminalisation consideration (secondary): the Band considered decriminalising marijuana on the reservation about two years before the 2024 article but decided to wait for the state to determine marijuana legality because Wisconsin is governed by Public Law 280 (Thompson/secondary reporting). No Lac du Flambeau marijuana decriminalisation ordinance was verified.

10.3.6 Menominee Indian Tribe of Wisconsin — advisory votes, 2015 hemp crop, federal raid, lawsuit dismissed

Same-name discipline: the Menominee Indian Tribe of Wisconsin and its reservation are conterminous with Menominee County, Wisconsin. That county/tribe is distinct from Menominee, Michigan, and from Menominee County, Michigan. Every "Menominee" below means the Wisconsin tribe/reservation unless stated otherwise.

  • August 2015 advisory referendums (secondary; certified tribal canvass not located — Verify): recreational legalisation approved 677 to 499 (about 58% / "nearly 58 percent" / "almost 60 per cent" in different secondary reports — preserve as a range, do not pick one percentage without the certified canvass); medical legalisation approved 899 to 275 (about 76.5% / 77% in different reports). Turnout reported as about 17% (Wisconsin Radio Network, 21 August 2015) vs about 13% of about 9,000 members (Aboriginal Multi-Media Society / Ganjapreneur) — conflict, Verify from tribal certified results. Both questions were advisory only. Sources: wrn.com — Menominee referendum; ammsa.com — Menominee voters — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Contemporaneous tribal position (secondary quotation): Tribal Chair Gary Besaw was quoted that tribal legislators then had direction to proceed, that "It's still illegal. Nothing has changed" at that time, that if tribal lawmakers approved growing marijuana it would only be available to tribal members and only allowed on tribal lands, and that the tribe would defer to the US Attorney's Office for interpretations of the DOJ 2014 memorandum. No enacted Menominee medical or adult-use sales ordinance following the advisory vote was verified. Do not present the members-only/on-lands-only proposal as current law.
  • 2015 hemp ordinance and crop (court complaint, filed 18 November 2015): the Tribe's 2015 ordinance defined industrial hemp as all parts of Cannabis genera with THC concentration 0.3% or less by weight, created a licensing procedure, and the Tribe entered an agreement with the College of the Menominee Nation to research hemp viability, issued a licence to the College, which planted a crop on tribal lands for research. The complaint alleges the Tribe cooperated with DOJ/DEA on testing, agreed to destroy any hemp testing above the limit, and that no known test exceeded 0.3%. Source (complaint, Case No. 1:15-cv-01378-WCG): archive.org — Menominee hemp case complaint — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Federal enforcement — date and outcome (court/government-adjacent sources, not blogs alone): on Friday, 23 October 2015, federal agents entered the Menominee Reservation and seized and destroyed the Tribe's industrial hemp crop (complaint ¶75: "On Friday, October 23, 2015, federal agents entered the Menominee Reservation and seized and destroyed the Tribe's industrial hemp crop. There was no known THC test exceeding 0.3%."). Agency attribution — Verify: the complaint says "federal agents" and "federal agents and their state counterparts." Secondary reporting attributes the seizure to DEA (DEA reported it confiscated 30,000 high-grade marijuana plants; the Tribe said they were industrial hemp) and a tribal-council-member account says agents had visited in July 2015, the US Attorney tested samples days before the raid and they came back negative, then agents said samples were "hot" but never showed test results to tribal leaders. Do not attribute the raid solely to DEA in the compiled page without a government source confirming DEA as lead agency; the court record as located says "federal agents."
  • Lawsuit: Menominee Indian Tribe of Wisconsin v. Drug Enforcement Administration and US Department of Justice, US District Court, Eastern District of Wisconsin, Green Bay Division, Case No. 1:15-cv-01378-WCG, complaint filed 18 November 2015. The Tribe sought a declaration that cultivation for agricultural/academic research with the College of the Menominee Nation was lawful under 7 U.S.C. §5940 (2014 Farm Bill), on three grounds: (1) the Tribe acted as a "State" under §5940; (2) Wisconsin cannabis laws have no application on the Reservation, so cultivation was "allowed" under Wisconsin law; (3) the College is an "institution of higher education" under §5940.
  • Outcome (court order, opened via court text): Order 23 May 2016, Chief Judge William C. Griesbach, granted the Government's motion to dismiss, denied the Tribe's motion for summary judgment, and directed the Clerk to enter judgment dismissing the action. Verbatim reasoning: "Wisconsin law does not allow the growing of hemp. While Wisconsin law is not enforceable on the Menominee Reservation, that does not change the fact that the growing or cultivating of industrial hemp is not allowed under the laws of the State of Wisconsin. Because the Tribe is located in the State of Wisconsin, the hemp exception to the Controlled Substances Act does not apply to the Tribe." The court did not reach whether the College is an institution of higher education. Sources: justia.com — Menominee order (PDF); narf.org — Menominee v. DEA/DOJ — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Appeal — Verify, with a trap avoided: no Seventh Circuit merits decision on the Menominee hemp case was located. A Seventh Circuit document found in searching (No. 15-3850, 22 September 2016) is USA v. Derrick Corn, an unrelated assault case — do NOT cite it as the hemp appeal. Whether the Tribe appealed the 23 May 2016 dismissal, and any appellate disposition, is Verify.
  • Later tribal position (secondary): Tribal Chairwoman Joan Delabreau (May 2016, via Oneida Nation site republishing AP) said the Tribe would not plant a new hemp crop until a federal judge resolved the lawsuit, and that the raid cost millions and unfairly suggested "we were growing high-grade marijuana." No current Menominee hemp/cannabis operation was verified (the Buffalo's Fire 2026 summit assertion in §10.3.2 is unverified and does not change this).
  • Public Law 280 position: Menominee is the Wisconsin exception case — see §10.4 timeline. Do not state a single current PL 280 rule for Menominee without the retrocession verification in §10.4.

10.3.7 Oneida Nation — 2019 pilot only

Official tribal site, 5 March 2019: Oneida Nation filed for a permit with DATCP to begin hemp production; for the 2019 growing season the Tribe would participate through the DATCP pilot programme, not growing/selling at commercial scale; plan was approximately 32 acres on the Oneida Reservation, one acre earmarked for CBD; the Tribe was in process of creating rules for hemp production on the reservation but had not yet done so. Source: oneida-nsn.gov — Oneida hemp pilot — accessed 28 October 2026. Secondary reporting (2019) adds: legal hiccups prevented growing 5 acres the prior year under Wisconsin's pilot programme; after the 2018 Farm Bill the Tribe planned to plant 32 acres in June 2019 for fall harvest; it had not pursued a processor's licence at that time. No current Oneida hemp/cannabis operation, regulator or buyer rule was verified. Do not infer a current operation from the 2019 pilot plan (and see the unverified 2026 summit assertion in §10.3.2).

10.3.8 Red Cliff Band of Lake Superior Chippewas — vending machine announced, not verified as operating

Tribal newsletter (official tribal communication channel), December 1–7, 2025: "Coming Soon: CBD & Hemp Vending Machine. Red Cliff will soon have a CBD and Hemp vending machine available at Legendary Waters Resort & Casino. 7 Gen Vending is supported by the Red Cliff Business Development Corporation. The vending machine will consist of CBD / Hemp-based products, which are known for their medicinal purposes in helping with PTSD, sleep problems, anxiety, focus, and more. Stay tuned for more details along with a grand opening date and information session." Source: constantcontact.com — Red Cliff newsletter Dec 2025 — accessed 28 October 2026. A November 3–9, 2025 tribal newsletter likewise said to "watch for the green ribbon cutting of the new CBD vending machine that is being installed." Milwaukee Journal Sentinel, 15 December 2025, reported Red Cliff "announced this month that it will soon have CBD and hemp vending machines available at its Legendary Water Resort and Casino." Sources: constantcontact.com — Red Cliff newsletter Nov 2025; jsonline.com — tribal nations and federal THC hemp ban — accessed 28 October 2026. Treatment: announced/installing as of December 2025; grand opening, operating status, product list (THC vs CBD-only), age rule and regulator were NOT verified. Do not list Red Cliff as an operating cannabis/hemp programme. Note the contrast with Lac du Flambeau Chapter 95 §95.501(2), which bans vending-machine sale of hemp products on that reservation — Red Cliff has no such verified ban located, but absence of a located ban is not proof of a Red Cliff vending ordinance. Advocacy, not operation: Red Cliff Chairwoman Nicole Boyd, State of the Tribes Address (reported February–March 2026 newsletter), expressed Tribes' desire for improved access to medical cannabis and hemp-derived THC and advocacy for bipartisan state action, citing Lac Vieux Desert Band's Wild Glow Cannabis Company in Michigan and its state compact for revenue sharing as an example. That is a Michigan tribe's arrangement, cited as advocacy — not a Red Cliff operation and not a Wisconsin compact. Background page, not law: a Red Cliff tribal referendum page contains a cannabis history summary (Flandreau Santee Sioux 2015, Menominee 2015, Ho-Chunk 2015/2024, Eastern Band of Cherokee medical 2021/adult-use 2023, Michigan, Illinois, Minnesota tribal compacts). It is background/history, NOT a Red Cliff ordinance — do not cite it as Red Cliff law. Source: redcliff-nsn.gov — referendum — accessed 28 October 2026.

10.3.9 Sokaogon Chippewa Community (Mole Lake) — hemp plan + DRAFT ordinance; do not present as enacted

USDA-hosted document: "Sokaogon Chippewa Community Tribal Industrial Hemp Ordinance," file SCC_HempOrdinance-USDA-Revision_2025-Final, marked "DRAFT: 2025-10-01" in its text. Source: ams.usda.gov — Sokaogon draft ordinance (PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026. Draft preamble (as written, not as enacted law): the Tribe is federally recognised, organised under 25 U.S.C. §476, meets the definition of an Indian Tribe eligible to participate in hemp production under the 2018 Farm Bill (cited in the document as 17 U.S.C. §1639o(2) — note: the operative hemp definition is at 7 U.S.C. §1639o; the "17 U.S.C." citation in the draft appears to be a scrivener error, Verify against the final text), desires primary regulatory authority over hemp in its sovereign territory, administrative offices on the Sokaogon Chippewa Community Mole Lake Reservation, Crandon, Wisconsin. Draft policy, §12-201 (as written): hemp is a valuable agricultural crop/commodity; purposes include establishing/granting regulatory authority to the Hemp Commissioner as a governmental instrumentality/arm of the Tribe; allowing persons to plant, produce, harvest, sample, test, process, transport, transfer, take possession of, sell, import and export hemp on lots within tribal jurisdiction to the greatest extent allowed under federal law and to comply with the 2018 Farm Bill; treating hemp production/processing as an agricultural commodity under tribal law; and providing revenue/economic opportunity. Draft transport rule, §E (as written): the licensee/transporter must carry: copy of the hemp cultivator's licence; copy of lab test results showing acceptable hemp THC levels for the harvest lot in transit; copy of invoice/bill of lading with buyer/seller information; transportation logs; and any other documentation required by tribal regulation. Seed-certification and pesticide-destruction provisions also appear in the draft text. Draft eligibility (search-index text): eligible cultivator/processor applicants include natural persons aged 18 and over and business entities; key participants may not have felony convictions relating to controlled substances within the past 10 years; separate application per noncontiguous location; tribe retains application information for no less than 3 years. Why enactment is NOT assumed: the document is marked DRAFT 2025-10-01 despite its "2025-Final" filename, and its legislative history line reads "Passed by Tribal Council Resolution 03 - ____ - 2024 on March ____, 2024" — with blanks. A 2024-passed version may exist and the 2025 document may be a USDA revision draft, but the two cannot be reconciled from the document alone. Treatment: quote the draft ONLY as draft text; state adoption/enactment and the final USDA-approved version as Verify; do not list Sokaogon as having a verified enacted retail ordinance or a verified retail operation. Sokaogon is listed among ICIA member tribes in secondary reporting; that is not an operation. Regulator: "Hemp Commissioner" appears only in this draft text. Current regulator, licence fees and buyer rules are Verify.

10.3.10 St. Croix Chippewa Indians of Wisconsin — historic CBD programme; current status NOT verified

2017 ordinance (press-release PDF, 27 September 2017): "St. Croix Medical Cannabidiol Control Ordinance" — intent to provide an additional source of revenue for tribal operations; the Title governs cultivation of industrial hemp and processing/distribution of CBD on the reservation for medical purposes, and enables the Tribe to control possession/consumption of CBD on the reservation; tribal regulation is stated as necessary to protect health, security and general welfare. Two express disclaimers in the search text: "Nothing in this Title shall be deemed to be in positive conflict with the Controlled Substance Act, 21 U.S.C. §801 et seq." and "Nothing in this Title shall be deemed to create a right to grow and/or distribute marijuana as defined by the federal CSA." Sovereign immunity is stated not to be limited/waived by the Title. Section 29-1-3, Control of Industrial Hemp Cultivation and CBD Processing and Distribution, is quoted in search text. Full verbatim text, adoption date, regulator and buyer rules still to be verified from the full tribal code, not the press-release PDF. Source: turtletalk.blog — St. Croix press release (PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026. 2017 plan (secondary): the Tribe planned to produce/distribute CBD on tribal lands in northwestern Wisconsin, in a decommissioned fish hatchery (reported as 200,000 sq ft in one source, 149,000 sq ft in another — conflict, Verify; location Danbury in one source); planned six acres of hemp; initial employment 15 workers (secondary, 2017); products would be from hemp with no psychoactive effect, not for recreational use / to get high; tribal attorney Jeff Cormell quoted on strict regulatory programmes similar to other cannabis-legal states and DOJ policy compliance. 2018 litigation/settlement (secondary, settlement text NOT verified): the Tribe filed federal suit in February 2018 in Madison against Wisconsin Attorney General Brad Schimel, seeking a declaration that Wisconsin's CBD/hemp law had no applicability on St. Croix tribal lands and that delays would have substantial detrimental impact; Schimel had replied he was "not in a position to make any promises" the Tribe could proceed. Secondary reporting (Vote Hemp / Wisconsin Public Radio summary) says the parties reached an agreement that the Tribe will oversee hemp production to produce CBD oil on reservation lands and will notify the state of any changes to its ordinance. Settlement document, court disposition and current operation status were NOT verified — Verify; do not present the 2017 plan as a current operation and do not cite the reported agreement as a verified compact/MOU (see §10.7). St. Croix is listed among ICIA member tribes in secondary reporting. No St. Croix medical-marijuana or adult-use sales operation was verified.

10.3.11 Stockbridge-Munsee Community — no operation verified

No cannabis/hemp ordinance, regulator, sales operation or buyer rule was verified. The tribe appears in the eleven-tribe lists and, for Stockbridge-Munsee, in a 2026 sports-betting lawsuit party list (eight tribes: Bad River, Forest County Potawatomi, Ho-Chunk, Menominee, Oneida, Red Cliff, Mole Lake, Stockbridge-Munsee Mohican) — that lawsuit is not a cannabis programme.
