10.4 Legal framework with the State, including Public Law 280

10.4.1 What Public Law 280 is — and is not

Public Law 83-280, enacted 15 August 1953, 67 Stat. 558, codified at 18 U.S.C. §1162 (criminal) and 28 U.S.C. §1360 (civil), is a mandatory-state law for California, Minnesota, Nebraska, Oregon and Wisconsin (Alaska added later). It grants state criminal jurisdiction over offences by/against Indians in Indian country to the same extent as elsewhere in the state, and civil adjudicatory jurisdiction over private civil causes of action between Indians or to which Indians are parties. It does NOT grant state civil regulatory jurisdiction over tribes/trust land: Bryan v. Itasca County, 426 U.S. 373 (1976), held PL 280 authorises jurisdiction only over private civil litigation, not state regulation/taxation of tribes. Secondary summaries also state PL 280 did not grant regulatory power over environmental control, land use, gambling, licences on reservations, nor power to tax tribal lands or to exercise jurisdiction over Indian water rights, nor over basic tribal governmental functions such as enrollment and domestic relations. Verify each limit against Bryan and the statute text before quoting the list as law; the Bryan holding (no regulatory jurisdiction) is the settled point, the longer list is secondary summary. Practical consequence for cannabis (secondary legal analysis, labelled as such — Husch Blackwell / JD Supra 2024; Wisconsin Public Radio May 2024): because Wisconsin is a mandatory PL 280 state, a tribal decriminalisation/legalisation regime conflicts with Wisconsin's categorical marijuana ban; tribal-police non-enforcement does not bind county/state police, who could still cite under state law; tribes seeking commercial cannabis would need a strictly regulated, licensed, overseen tribal regime and/or a compact with Wisconsin mirroring Washington/New Mexico models, while noting some tribes may oppose compacts given historically antagonistic state relations. That analysis is secondary, not law. Source: jdsupra.com — Ho-Chunk Nation — accessed 28 October 2026.

10.4.2 The Menominee exception — timeline, NOT a single current rule

Present this as a timeline. Do NOT publish either "Wisconsin has PL 280 jurisdiction over all eleven tribes" or "Menominee is excepted" as a settled current fact on search text alone.

  1. 1953 — original PL 280 excluded the Menominee Reservation from the grant to Wisconsin (Wisconsin AG opinion summary, 66 Op. Att'y Gen. 290 (1977), as quoted in search text; Menominee complaint ¶43).
  2. 24 August 1954 — Congress amended 18 U.S.C. §1162 to strike the Menominee exception, subjecting the Menominee Reservation to state criminal jurisdiction, alongside the Menominee Termination Act of 17 June 1954 (68 Stat. 250); the Reservation area became Menominee County, Wisconsin's 72nd county (government brief summary in search text).
  3. 1976 — retrocession: the Menominee Restoration Act (87 Stat. 770, 25 U.S.C. §§903–903f) repealed the Termination Act; Menominee were removed from PL 280 coverage again effective 1 March 1976 (66 Op. Att'y Gen. 290 (1977) summary); county and reservation boundaries once again coterminous for jurisdictional purposes (66 Op. Att'y Gen. 115 (1977) summary).
  4. Current text — unresolved conflict in sources located: the current 18 U.S.C. §1162(a) table as reproduced in search text lists Wisconsin as "All Indian country within the State" with NO Menominee exception printed in that table, while a Wisconsin Supreme Court case footnote states: "The Menominee Tribe of Indians is excepted from this grant of jurisdiction because of retrocession of jurisdiction by the State of Wisconsin" (citing Cal. v. Cabazon Band, 480 U.S. 202 (1987) discussion). A secondary tribal-courts summary states: "Under PL-280, the State of Wisconsin has criminal jurisdiction on Indian Reservations, over Indians (except on the Menominee Reservation)." Sources: case.law — Wisconsin case PDF; corpuslegalis.com — 18 U.S.C. §1162; fox11digital.com — Menominee PDF — accessed 28 October 2026. Resolution required: verify current operative PL 280 status for Menominee from the current 18 U.S.C. §1162 text PLUS the retrocession statute/order (25 U.S.C. §§1321–1326, Wisconsin retrocession) before stating whether Wisconsin currently has PL 280 criminal jurisdiction on the Menominee Reservation. The practical consequence already documented is consistent — Menominee is uniquely positioned in Wisconsin for the 2015 vote analysis (secondary: "the only reservation falling solely under federal law") while all other Wisconsin tribes are PL 280 tribes — but that "solely federal" phrasing itself needs the retrocession verification.

All eleven tribes have some form of judicial system (secondary tribal-courts summary); Ho-Chunk also operates a Traditional Court; other tribes have peacemaking available; tribal law is written down for many tribes and available online via tribal courts / Judicare links. Verify per tribe before citing a specific court as a cannabis regulator. No tribal court was verified as a cannabis regulator in Run 2 except Lac du Flambeau's Tribal Court in its Chapter 95 penalty provisions (§95.502(3) licence suspensions) and LCO's Tribal Court as defined in CSV.8.2.010(nn).

10.4.3 Federal DOJ tribal marijuana policy — Wilkinson Memo rescinded; no replacement verified

Wilkinson Memorandum, 28 October 2014 (DOJ Director Monty Wilkinson, to all US Attorneys): extended the 2013 Cole Memorandum's eight federal enforcement priorities to Indian Country, treating tribal governments like state governments for marijuana-enforcement discretion where tribes established robust regulatory systems. The eight priorities (from the Cole/Wilkinson framework, via secondary summaries and a compact redline quoting them): (1) preventing distribution to minors; (2) preventing revenue going to criminal enterprises, gangs and cartels; (3) preventing diversion from legal states to other states; (4) preventing state/tribal-authorised activity being used as cover for trafficking other illegal drugs or other illegal activity; (5) preventing violence and use of firearms in cultivation/distribution; (6) preventing drugged driving and exacerbation of adverse public health consequences; (7) preventing growing on public lands and attendant public-safety/environmental dangers; (8) preventing marijuana possession/use on federal property. Sources: jdsupra.com — Wilkinson memo summary; hobbsstraus.com — General Memorandum 18-001 — accessed 28 October 2026. Rescission — verified in secondary summaries, memo text not opened: on 4 January 2018, Attorney General Jeff Sessions sent a memorandum to all US Attorneys terminating the Obama-era marijuana policies, stating the Cole and Wilkinson Memoranda "are no longer in effect" and that US Attorneys are free to prosecute marijuana activities under existing federal laws and the US Attorney's Manual. The Sessions memorandum "does not replace the rescinded memoranda with any new guidance" (Hobbs Straus General Memorandum 18-001, summarising the Sessions memo). Sources: hobbsstraus.com — General Memorandum 18-001; coquilletribe.org — litigation update (PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026. Current status — do NOT cite Wilkinson as current policy. Secondary summaries agree enforcement has not returned to pre-2013 levels and US Attorneys have largely continued to exercise discretion de-prioritising state/tribal-compliant activity, and a 2025 Senate letter urged reinstatement of tribal prosecutorial discretion — but no post-2018 DOJ tribal marijuana guidance replacing Wilkinson was located in Run 2. Verify any current DOJ/DEA tribal enforcement guidance before stating a current federal enforcement policy. The Menominee 2015 reliance on the 2014 memorandum (Besaw deferring to the US Attorney's Office for interpretations) is historical, not current policy. Federal-law overlay that survives any memo: marijuana remains federally controlled; the given baseline (split scheduling, Schedule III only for state-licensed medical and FDA-approved products effective 28 April 2026, 91 FR 22714) is federal law, not prosecutorial discretion. A rescinded memo does not legalise anything.

10.5 The "state-licensed" question — VERIFY, and on current evidence moot in Wisconsin

Finding: no Wisconsin tribal medical-marijuana programme (card, registry, dispensary selling marijuana) was verified for any of the eleven tribes. Little Pines sells hemp-derived THC/CBD products under a tribal hemp plan/licence framework, not marijuana; its own governing ordinance (LDF Ch. 95) defines its products as "Industrial Hemp" (≤0.3% total delta-9 THC, dry weight) and its CBD definition states CBD "possesses no psychoactive effect" (§95.201(5)) — which sits in tension with intoxicating-THC retail reporting (see §10.3.5). St. Croix's programme was CBD from hemp, expressly disclaiming a right to grow/distribute marijuana under the federal CSA. LCO's programme is hemp production/processing (plus university research); Ho-Chunk is decriminalisation only; Menominee's 2015 advisory votes produced no verified enacted medical sales programme.

Conclusion for this file: state the question as Verify in this form: "Whether any tribal medical programme counts as 'state-licensed' for Schedule III purposes under 91 FR 22714 could not be determined, because no verified Wisconsin tribal medical-marijuana programme was located to which the question could attach, and no DEA/DOJ guidance on tribal licences under the split-scheduling baseline was located." Do NOT state that a tribal programme IS state-licensed, and do NOT state that it is definitively NOT state-licensed as a matter of federal law, without the 91 FR 22714 text and any DEA/DOJ tribal-licence guidance. Neither was located in Run 2. The safe published sentence is the Verify formulation above, plus the Run 1 baseline: Wisconsin has no state-licensed medical programme, so marijuana remains Schedule I in Wisconsin under split scheduling, subject only to the statutory exclusions in Run 1 Part 1.

10.6 Interstate

10.6.1 Hemp shipments — federal interstate protection and its limits

Statute (2018 Farm Bill §10114, Pub. L. No. 115-334): "Nothing in this title or an amendment made by this title prohibits the interstate commerce of hemp or hemp products." And §10114(b): "No State or Indian Tribe shall prohibit the transportation or shipment of hemp or hemp products produced in accordance with subtitle G of the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 (as added by section 10113) through the State or the territory of the Indian Tribe, as applicable." USDA guidance (May 2019 memo, via secondary summaries — Arnold & Porter; Duane Morris): states/tribes may not prevent movement of hemp through their states/territories even if they prohibit its production, and §10114(b) effectively preempts state law interfering with interstate hemp commerce (Supremacy Clause / conflict preemption). Sources: arnoldporter.com — USDA hemp guidance; duanemorris.com — USDA hemp memo; cannabusiness.law — USDA hemp regulations — accessed 28 October 2026. Limit (critical): protection applies ONLY to hemp/hemp products produced in accordance with subtitle G (or, in the USDA memo discussion, the 2014 Farm Bill as "other Federal laws"). Hemp produced outside that authority is still subject to seizure. Practical burden at roadside stops (secondary legal commentary, NOT law): manifest, producer licence, certificate of analysis showing <0.3% THC, invoice/bill of lading — undocumented cannabis-family material may be temporarily seized and, if testing shows THC above legal limits, the transporter could face controlled-substance possession charges. Tribal transport rules that mirror this burden (draft/ordinance text, not Wisconsin state law): Sokaogon draft §E (licence + lab results + invoice/bill of lading + logs, see §10.3.9); LCO code requires Agricultural Division reporting and HeMP-platform test reporting (CSV.8.5.030, CSV.8.2.010(ll)). These are tribal-programme rules, not a Wisconsin state transport-permit regime. Wisconsin-specific interaction — Verify: Wisconsin law defines hemp by delta-9 only (§94.55(1), Run 1), while federal hemp is total-THC based after the 2026 redefinition. How interstate shipments into/through Wisconsin are treated after 11 December 2026 (PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019, per Run 1 §1.5.7) — including whether a shipment lawful federally but over Wisconsin's delta-9 definition, or vice versa, is protected — is Verify and must be flagged alongside Run 1 §1.5.7. No Wisconsin statute, rule or DATCP/DOR/DOJ guidance amending §94.55(1) to a total-THC definition or creating a state transition period was located (Run 1 finding, unchanged). "Fit-for-commerce certificate" caution: one secondary page states any hemp transported within/through Wisconsin must carry a DATCP fit-for-commerce certificate (THC results, amount, chain of custody), with seizure/destruction and licence suspension/revocation for hemp found without it. That description matches the former DATCP pilot regime, not the current USDA-direct licensing regime (DATCP programme ended 1 January 2022, Run 1). Do NOT publish a current Wisconsin fit-for-commerce certificate requirement without an official current source — Verify. The practical point that documentation reduces seizure risk is supported by the Kovacic case below, not by that secondary page alone.

10.6.2 Wisconsin residents buying in Illinois, Michigan and Minnesota — cross-reference, figures UNCHANGED

Run 1 Part 6.3 figures are NOT changed by Run 2. No new official source was located that would justify changing them. The only new material is a secondary corroboration (see below), which does not count as a new official source.

  • Illinois 2022 (Run 1, preserved verbatim in substance): calendar 2022, Illinois dispensaries $1,552.3 million total (excluding tax); $239.7 million (15.4%) from border counties; $121.2 million (50.6%) of border-county sales to out-of-state residents (~7.8% of total Illinois sales); $36.1 million Illinois tax attributed to Wisconsin residents on the Legislative Fiscal Bureau (LFB) assumption that ALL out-of-state border-county sales were to Wisconsin residents. LFB memo 10 March 2023, reported second-hand — memo PDF itself not opened in Run 1; county list flagged (Jo Daviess, Lake, McHenry, Winnebago, Boone; Stephenson had none at that time; 2023 reporting varies). Do NOT present as a 2024/2025/2026 figure. Unverified 2024-claimed figures ($319.4m border sales; $132.4m / 41.5% out-of-state; "more than $36 million" tax; 36 dispensaries in five border counties; LFB April 2026 memo) were NOT adopted in Run 1 and are NOT adopted here — Verify by opening the memo PDF itself.
  • Secondary corroboration (does not change the figure): WRCE Richland Center fact brief, 23 March 2026, states Illinois "collected $36.1 million in tax revenue in 2022 from out-of-state residents who purchased cannabis in counties bordering Wisconsin" and "About half of cannabis sales in 2022 at dispensaries in Illinois counties that border Wisconsin were to out-of-state residents," citing the LFB analysis; and confirms "Michigan and Minnesota do not track nonresident cannabis purchases." Source: wrce.fm — Wisconsin losing millions to legal states — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Michigan and Minnesota amounts: Unavailable from regulator data in Run 1; unchanged here. Michigan does not track nonresident purchases; Minnesota does not track nonresident purchases (WRCE brief, citing Michigan Cannabis Regulatory Agency and Minnesota Office of Cannabis Management contacts).
  • Neighbour-state tribal retail drawing Wisconsin residents (secondary, Michigan tribe — NOT a Wisconsin tribe): Sokaogon (Mole Lake) Chairman Robert Van Zile, 2023 State of the Tribes Address reporting, said the Hannahville Potawatomi Tribe (Michigan's Upper Peninsula, about 100 miles east of Mole Lake) earns about 90% of its $5 million annual cannabis business from Wisconsin residents (via Green Bay Press-Gazette, quoted in Journal Sentinel 15 December 2025). Secondary, single-source, Michigan tribe — do not present as a measured Wisconsin outflow figure and do not attribute to any Wisconsin tribe. Source: jsonline.com — tribal nations and federal THC hemp ban — accessed 28 October 2026.

10.6.3 What a Wisconsin resident can buy next door — purchase rules only insofar as they affect bring-back

All neighbour-state purchase limits below are secondary summaries except where a statute/rule is cited; the full neighbour-state law is NOT re-researched here. Verify each limit against the neighbour regulator before publishing it as law.

  • Illinois — nonresidents may buy adult-use, at half limits (current, post-doubling): since 12 June 2026 (SB 3222 / Public Act 104-0463, per the Illinois Run 1 dossier — resident possession doubled to 60 g flower / 1,000 mg infused THC / 10 g concentrate; nonresidents half), a nonresident aged 21+ may possess up to 30 g flower, 5 g concentrate, 500 mg THC in cannabis-infused products (cumulative possession limits, not per-transaction allowances; valid government ID required). Illinois does NOT offer medical reciprocity and will not accept out-of-state medical cards; out-of-state visitors 21+ may only buy as adult-use customers (secondary, MMJ.com summary of IDPH / Cannabis Regulation and Tax Act position — Verify against IDFPR/IDPH). Sources: themarijuanaherald.com — possession limits by state; mmj.com — Illinois medical card; herb.co — city guides — accessed 28 October 2026. Bring-back effect: nil. A lawful Illinois purchase at or below the nonresident limit is still marijuana in Wisconsin (see §10.6.4).
  • Michigan — visitors buy on the same terms as residents: adults 21+ may buy up to 2.5 ounces of cannabis flower per transaction at a licensed retailer, with no more than 15 g of that amount in concentrate form (Michigan Admin Code R 420.506, via secondary summary); possession generally up to 2.5 ounces outside the home, up to 10 ounces at a residence (amounts over 2.5 ounces secured). Limits "apply identically to Michigan residents and out-of-state visitors" with valid government photo ID showing 21+ (secondary summaries of MCL 333.27954 / MRTMA). Out-of-state visitors cannot participate in Michigan's medical programme (secondary). Sources: legalclarity.org — Michigan purchase limits; marijuanapackaging.com — Michigan purchase limits — accessed 28 October 2026. Bring-back effect: nil (see §10.6.4). Michigan's Upper Peninsula locations (Iron Mountain, Ironwood, Menominee, Michigan — note: Menominee, Michigan is NOT the Wisconsin tribe) are the geographically closest Michigan retail to northern Wisconsin, but proximity does not create a Wisconsin right.
  • Minnesota — visitors may buy; no additional nonresident restriction: state-licensed adult-use retail sales began 16 September 2025 (Minnesota Office of Cannabis Management press release, per Run 1); tribal retail preceded state-licensed retail from August 2023 (Red Lake Nation, White Earth Nation — Minnesota tribes, not Wisconsin tribes). Minnesota "has no additional restrictions for out-of-state customers, and people with a valid ID showing they are 21 or older will be allowed to purchase cannabis products at licensed retailers" (Minneapolis.org summary of OCM position). Possession for adults 21+: 2 ounces flower in public, 2 pounds at a private residence, 8 grams concentrate, 800 mg THC in edibles (Minnesota Statutes ch. 342 / §342.09, via secondary summaries and University of Minnesota summary). Sources: minneapolis.org — cannabis; umn.edu — Minnesota Adult Use Cannabis Act (PDF); legalclarity.org — buying marijuana in Minnesota — accessed 28 October 2026. Bring-back effect: nil (see §10.6.4). Minnesota's own guidance states: "If someone were to cross a border into a neighboring state with legally purchased cannabis, the person could be subject to prosecution in that state. It is also a federal crime to transport marijuana across state lines."
  • Medical cards — no cross-border recognition in Wisconsin: no out-of-state medical card is recognised as a Wisconsin defence (Run 1 §1.4, Verify for absolute completeness). A Wisconsin resident's Illinois/Michigan/Minnesota medical purchase, even if lawful there, confers no Wisconsin right. Conversely, Wisconsin has no medical card to present in Illinois (no Illinois reciprocity, above), Michigan (visitors excluded from medical programme, above) or Minnesota (Minnesota medical residency required, per secondary OCM summary — Verify).

10.6.4 What can legally be brought into Wisconsin

  • Marijuana: nothing. On Run 1 law, marijuana (non-hemp THC) from Illinois, Michigan or Minnesota remains illegal to possess in Wisconsin under §961.41(3g)(e) (first conviction fine ≤$1,000 or imprisonment ≤6 months or both; second or subsequent offence Class I felony), regardless of lawful purchase in the neighbouring state. Transporting marijuana across state lines is a federal offence (secondary legal summary quoting Marquette professor Idleman, May 2024: "It is a federal offense to transport marijuana from a state where it is legal, like Illinois, Michigan or Minnesota, but prosecutions have not been a priority for the government"). An Illinois/Michigan/Minnesota receipt, bag or licence does NOT protect the holder in Wisconsin (secondary defence-practice summary — do not overstate as a court holding, but the statutory point stands on §961.41(3g)(e) itself).
  • Hemp/hemp products produced in accordance with subtitle G: federally protected in transit (§10114(b), §10.6.1), subject to the Wisconsin total-THC/delta-9 interaction flagged as Verify. Hemp meeting Wisconsin's §94.55(1) definition is not "marijuana" under §961.01(14) and its THC is excluded from §961.14(4)(t)3 (Run 1).
  • Certified CBD: narrow carve-out only. A cannabidiol product may be possessed under §961.32(2m) with a physician certification (certification ≤1 year old, not expired); hemp needs no certification (2019 Act 68 sentence) (Run 1). This does NOT authorise bringing back intoxicating hemp-THC products as "CBD," and does NOT authorise bringing back marijuana.
  • Tribal hemp products bought on a Wisconsin reservation: on-reservation permission ≠ off-reservation right. LDF §95.502(4) permits possession/use/consumption of tribe-licensed/approved hemp products "on the Lac du Flambeau Indian Reservation." No verified rule was located authorising transport of tribal hemp-THC products off any Wisconsin reservation into Wisconsin's general jurisdiction, or into another state. Verify before stating any off-reservation transport right for tribal retail products. The same caution applies to any future Red Cliff vending product.
  • Exact "bring-back" rule — residual Verify: no Wisconsin DOJ / State Patrol guidance stating the bring-back rule in those terms was located in Run 2. The conclusion above rests on §961.41(3g)(e) + §961.01(14) + §94.55(1) + §10114, not on a bring-back guidance document. Flag that provenance in this file.

10.6.5 Enforcement near the borders — what was actually found (and what was not)

No Wisconsin-specific border-enforcement policy, State Patrol hemp-seizure guidance, or border-county enforcement dataset was located. Do not invent one. The material below is the closest verified enforcement evidence; none of it is a border checkpoint or border-county crackdown programme.

Kovacic v. Oconto County et al., E.D. Wis. Case No. 23-C-642 (Decision and Order 23 September 2025, Judge Griesbach) — the strongest Wisconsin hemp-transport enforcement evidence located: plaintiffs Kevin and Tammy Kovacic, lawfully transporting legal hemp to a customer in northeast Wisconsin in the course of Ms. Kovacic's business (Wautoma Cannabis Queen, LLC), were stopped; a drug-detection dog alerted on the car and the hemp tested positive for the presence of THC; law enforcement seized the hemp, arrested Mr. Kovacic and booked him into Oconto County Jail; by the time the hemp was tested and found lawful, it had lost all value. The court noted plaintiffs' argument that marijuana and legal hemp have an indistinguishable odour, a dog trained to alert on marijuana will also alert on legal hemp, and a Nark II test cannot differentiate legal hemp from marijuana (it detects THC presence only); plaintiffs stated they repeatedly explained they were hemp farmers with a USDA hemp producer licence, and that USDA's public search tool lets law enforcement verify licences. The 23 September 2025 order granted summary judgment in part and denied it in part (granted as to all defendants except Oconto County, except Monell claims against Oconto County — full disposition requires reading the whole order, not just the summary). Sources: justia.com — Kovacic order; justia.com — Kovacic order (PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026. Use in Part 10: as evidence that (a) lawful hemp transport in Wisconsin carries a real seizure/arrest risk at a traffic stop despite documentation, and (b) field tests and canine alerts do not distinguish hemp from marijuana. Do NOT present it as a border-enforcement case — Oconto/Forest Counties and Crandon are northeast Wisconsin, not a state-line operation — and do NOT present the 2025 order as a final merits holding that the stop was unlawful (it is a summary-judgment order, granted in part / denied in part). Tribal-land enforcement overlay: Ho-Chunk FAQ that county/state police could still cite despite tribal-police non-enforcement (§10.3.3); PL 280 criminal jurisdiction for ten tribes, Menominee status unresolved (§10.4.2); Menominee 2015 federal raid (§10.3.6). No Illinois/Michigan/Minnesota border-county enforcement cooperation agreement with Wisconsin was located. Driving: Wisconsin's restricted-controlled-substance driving law (§346.63(1)(am); delta-9 THC threshold 1 ng/ml under §340.01(50m)(e)) means a Wisconsin resident who lawfully consumes in a neighbouring state and drives in Wisconsin faces a separate Wisconsin OWI exposure even apart from possession — secondary defence-practice summaries confirm the threshold; the statute text itself is the authority. Roadside saliva screening under Act 99 is a screen, not a hemp/marijuana distinguisher. (Flag: this paragraph is enforcement-adjacent, not border enforcement; keep it brief in this file or cross-reference Part 1/Part 2.)

10.6.6 Interstate laws or agreements (compacts, MOUs) — none located for Wisconsin

No Wisconsin–Illinois, Wisconsin–Michigan, Wisconsin–Minnesota, or Wisconsin–tribal cannabis/hemp compact or MOU was located in searches of Wisconsin DOJ, DATCP, Governor's office and tribal government sources in Run 2. State "none located" — not "none exist" in absolute terms — unless a dedicated DOJ/DATCP records check is run.

What must NOT be presented as Wisconsin agreements:

  • Gaming compacts with all eleven tribes (DOA page, §10.1) — Class III gaming under IGRA, not cannabis/hemp.
  • Minnesota tribal cannabis compacts (e.g. White Earth Band / Red Lake Nation compacts with Minnesota, cited in secondary summaries and in the Red Cliff Chairwoman's advocacy) — Minnesota arrangements, not Wisconsin agreements.
  • Michigan tribal-state cannabis compacts (e.g. Lac Vieux Desert Band Wild Glow Cannabis Company compact, cited by Red Cliff Chairwoman Boyd) — Michigan arrangement, not a Wisconsin agreement.
  • St. Croix's reported 2018 agreement with the Wisconsin Attorney General (Tribe oversees CBD production, notifies state of ordinance changes) — reported settlement/agreement, settlement text NOT verified (§10.3.10). Do not cite as a compact until the document is opened.
  • The 25–26 February 2026 Great Lakes Indigenous Cannabis and Hemp Summit (Lake of the Torches Resort & Casino, Town of Lac du Flambeau, Vilas County; ICIA host, Lac du Flambeau Tribe co-host per listing) — a summit discussion of PL 280 and Minnesota's adult-use law (ICIA executive director Jane Oatman quoted on early collaborative engagement to avoid regulatory uncertainty), NOT an agreement.

Proposed tribal-agreement language (NOT law): bill drafts located in Run 2 contain a proposed §139.980, "Agreement with tribes" — "The department may enter into an agreement with a federally recognized American Indian tribe in this state for the administration and enforcement of this subchapter and to provide refunds of the tax imposed under s. 139.971 on marijuana sold on tribal land by or to enrolled members of the tribe residing on the tribal land" (2023 SB 486, 2023 AB 506, 2021 AB 846 texts). These are proposal texts for a marijuana tax subchapter that is NOT current law. Do not present §139.980 as enacted, and verify the final disposition of each bill before mentioning them beyond "proposed language was located in bill drafts." Sources: docs.legis.wisconsin.gov — SB 486; docs.legis.wisconsin.gov — AB 506; docs.legis.wisconsin.gov — AB 846 — accessed 28 October 2026.

Historic state-tribal hemp friction (not a compact): the former DATCP pilot era included a provision that a tribe or tribal member could not plant/grow/cultivate/process industrial hemp without a DATCP licence, with a tribal pilot-programme workaround (2017 SB 119 draft text, §(6)). That is superseded history (DATCP programme ended 1 January 2022; tribes now under USDA/tribal plans). Do not present it as current tribal-state hemp law; it is useful only as background for why the Menominee/St. Croix disputes arose.

PART 11 — OPERATIONS RULES

Part 11 is split throughout into (A) hemp growers and (B) hemp product businesses. Where Wisconsin has no rule, that is stated expressly — it is a finding, not an omission.

11.1 Testing, sampling, failed tests, recalls

11.1.A — Hemp growers (current USDA regime)

Required test: pre-harvest THC compliance testing of every lot. Testing must use post-decarboxylation or other similarly reliable methods approved by the Secretary, must consider potential conversion of THCA into THC, and must report total available THC derived from the sum of THC and THCA content on a dry-weight basis. Gas or liquid chromatography with detection are listed as meeting methods. Measurement of uncertainty (MU) must be estimated and reported. Laboratory quality assurance, method validation, and an effective disposal procedure for non-compliant samples are required. Any sample result exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level is conclusive evidence the lot is not in compliance. Source: 7 CFR 990.25 — accessed 28 October 2026.

Sampling rules (verbatim operative substance): a sampling agent must collect samples no more than 30 days before anticipated harvest. Producers may not sample their own growing facilities and may not act as sampling agents. Samples are from flowering tops, cutting the top 5 to 8 inches from the main stem, terminal bud, or central cola. The method must be sufficient at 95% confidence that no more than 1% of plants in the lot would exceed the acceptable hemp THC level, and must produce a representative, homogeneous sample. An authorised representative of the licensee should be present if possible. Sampling agents must be given complete and unrestricted access during business hours to all hemp/cannabis plants, production and storage areas, land, buildings and structures, and all licence locations. The crop must not be harvested before samples are taken. Post-harvest samples are only for remediated biomass. Source: 7 CFR 990.24 — accessed 28 October 2026.

Harvest window and commingling: harvest no later than 30 days after sample collection, otherwise a second pre-harvest sample is required. Harvested lots must not be commingled with other lots or material. Compliant lots may enter the stream of commerce; non-compliant lots are subject to § 990.27. Source: 7 CFR 990.26–990.27 — accessed 28 October 2026.

Failed tests — grower consequences: plants exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level constitute marijuana, a Schedule I controlled substance. The producer must either use a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement to dispose of them, or ensure disposal on site at the farm or hemp production facility. The producer must notify USDA of intent to dispose of or remediate and verify disposal/remediation with required documentation. Remediation requires additional sampling and testing of the post-remediated crop. A producer may request additional pre-harvest testing if the original result is believed to be in error. Source: 7 CFR 990.26–990.27 — accessed 28 October 2026.

Negligent vs culpable violations (testing-related): producing cannabis exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level is one of the three listed negligent violations (with failing to provide an accurate legal description of land and producing hemp without a licence). No negligent violation arises if the producer makes reasonable efforts to grow hemp and total THC is not more than 1.0% on a dry-weight basis. A violation with a culpable mental state greater than negligence must be immediately reported by USDA to the U.S. Attorney General and to the chief law enforcement officer of the state or Indian territory, and the negligent-violation protections do not apply. Source: 7 CFR 990.29 — accessed 28 October 2026.

11.1.B — Hemp product businesses (current Wisconsin law)

Wisconsin has no hemp-specific product testing rule. No current Wisconsin statute or rule was located requiring batch testing, potency labelling verification, contaminant testing (pesticides, heavy metals, solvents, microbials), or a hemp-product certificate of analysis for retail hemp products. This is consistent with Run 1's finding that no statewide testing, labelling, or seed-to-sale rule for hemp THC was located.

The only detailed product-testing rules located are PROPOSED AND FAILED: 2025 AB 606 / SB 681 would have created Wis. Stat. § 125.745, requiring a manufacturer to submit a representative sample of each batch to an independent, accredited laboratory for testing and certification before sale. That bill failed on 23 March 2026 and is not law. Do not present it as a requirement. Source: 2025 SB 681 proposal text, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov — SB 681 text — accessed 28 October 2026; failure date per Run 1.

General food-law rules that do reach hemp food processors (not hemp-specific):

  • Recall plan required: "An operator shall have a written plan for identifying and recalling food produced at that plant, should a food recall become necessary. The operator shall update the plan as necessary, and shall make it available to the department for inspection and copying upon request." Plan contents include key recall personnel, contacts, lot identification/dating/tracking, shipment tracking cross-referenced to production lots, scope determination, communication procedures and audiences, and key recall information. Source: Wis. Admin. Code § ATCP 70.32, docs.legis.wisconsin.gov — ATCP 70.30–70.32 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Pathogen/toxin reporting: a food processing plant operator must report to DATCP within 24 hours any microbiological test or laboratory analysis indicating ready-to-eat food contains pathogenic organisms, toxins, or any other adulterant capable of causing disease or injury if ingested, unless the food is lot-identified and still entirely under the operator's direct control without having been sold or distributed. Source: Wis. Admin. Code § ATCP 70.30 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Scope limit: ATCP 70 applies to food processing plants. It does not create a recall rule for non-food hemp products (topicals, inhalables, non-food oils, flower sold as a non-food product). For those, Wisconsin has no hemp-specific recall rule located — [Verify] whether DATCP's general consumer-protection or DHS authority has ever been used for a non-food hemp recall; none was located in Run 2.

Failed-test / recall rule for non-food hemp products: none located in current Wisconsin law — state this expressly in any republication.

11.1.4 — Accredited labs serving Wisconsin, including DEA-registered labs for hemp

USDA directory (verified in part): the USDA AMS "Hemp Analytical Testing Laboratories" directory, crawled 12 February 2026, lists Assurance Laboratories, LLC, West Milwaukee, Wisconsin, on its first page. Source: ams.usda.gov — DEA laboratories (page 0) — accessed 28 October 2026. Limit of that verification: the directory is paginated and only the first page was opened in Run 2. Other Wisconsin laboratories are not verified and must not be claimed. Say "at least one Wisconsin laboratory was verified on the directory's first page" and direct readers to the full directory — [Verify] the full directory (all pages) before publishing any count of Wisconsin labs.

DEA-registration deadline conflict — flag, do not resolve silently: 7 CFR 990.25(g)(3) as printed states that after 31 December 2022 USDA licensees may only use DEA-registered laboratories. The USDA AMS "Information for Hemp Testing Laboratories" page states USDA is delaying enforcement of the DEA-registered laboratory requirement until 31 December 2026 — which matches the Run 1 federal baseline. An older USDA FAQ extract stated 31 December 2025. Sources: 7 CFR 990.25; USDA AMS hemp laboratory pages, ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp — accessed 28 October 2026. Operative position: treat the current AMS enforcement-discretion page as the operative enforcement position, with the text-vs-enforcement distinction flagged, and note the 31 December 2026 date is the enforcement-discretion date, not a change to the printed rule text. [Verify] the AMS page again immediately before publication, because an enforcement-discretion date can move.

Wisconsin state lab accreditation for hemp: no Wisconsin state hemp-lab accreditation programme was located. Wisconsin does not operate its own hemp testing-lab licence; growers use USDA-recognised/DEA-registered labs under the federal regime above. [Verify] whether any Wisconsin lab holds a separate state accreditation relevant to hemp (e.g. DATCP lab certification for other matrices) — none was located in Run 2.

11.2 Licence rules: transfers, ownership changes, renewals, relocation, revocation, insurance/bonding

11.2.A — Hemp growers (current USDA regime)

  • Transfers: prohibited — licences "may not be sold, assigned, transferred, pledged, or otherwise disposed of, alienated or encumbered." Source: 7 CFR 990.22 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Ownership changes: a modification is required for any change to application information, including sale of a business or change in key participants. A new criminal history report is implicated by a key-participant change (see §7.3). Source: 7 CFR 990.21–990.22 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Renewals: not automatic; same terms, information, and approval criteria as an initial application unless law or regulation changed. Source: 7 CFR 990.22 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Relocation / new production location: a modification is required for production in a new location; all production locations must be reported (FSA, §7.4). Source: 7 CFR 990.21, 990.23 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Suspension: USDA may suspend on credible evidence of a part violation or failure to comply with a written AMS Administrator order relating to negligence. A suspended producer must not handle or remove hemp/cannabis from the location at suspension without prior written USDA authorisation and must not produce hemp during suspension. Appeal is under subpart D. Restoration is possible after a 1-year waiting period; if the licence was issued more than 3 years before restoration, a new application and criminal history report are required; a corrective action plan may be required for full restoration. Source: 7 CFR 990.30 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Revocation: immediate if the licensee pleads guilty to or is convicted of any felony related to a controlled substance, made any materially false statement with a culpable mental state greater than negligence, or is found growing cannabis exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level with a culpable mental state greater than negligence, or negligently violated the part 3 times in 5 years. Source: 7 CFR 990.31 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Negligent-violation ladder: negligent violations are limited to one per calendar year. Each draws a Notice of Violation and a corrective action plan in place for a minimum of 2 years, including correction date, steps, and compliance-demonstration procedures; a subsequent negligent violation while a plan is in place requires a new plan with heightened quality control, staff training, and quantifiable measures. Three negligent violations in 5 years result in revocation and ineligibility for 5 years from the third violation. Negligent violations are not subject to criminal enforcement by any federal, state, tribal, or local government. Source: 7 CFR 990.29 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Audits and records (grower): audits may occur once every 3 years unless USDA determines otherwise, may cover the current and previous crop years, and may be remote or in person; audit reports issue no later than 60 days after the audit concludes; a corrective action plan must include a reasonable correction date, USDA approves or denies it within 60 days of receipt, and the producer must periodically report for not less than 2 calendar years following the violation. Records of all hemp plants acquired, produced, handled, disposed of, or remediated must be kept at least 3 years and made available during reasonable business hours, with USDA access to any premises where hemp plants may be held. Source: 7 CFR 990.28 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Insurance/bonding minimum for growers: Wisconsin has no hemp-grower insurance or bonding minimum, and no USDA insurance/bonding minimum was located in 7 CFR part 990. State this expressly; do not import another state's bonding rule.

11.2.B — Hemp product businesses

  • Transfers, ownership changes, renewals, relocation, revocation: no hemp-specific rules. The general food/retail/vapor licences in §7.6 carry their own transfer/renewal/revocation terms under their own statutes and rules (e.g. ATCP 70/75/71, DOR permit rules) — those general terms were inventoried in Run 1 Part 4 and are not re-stated as hemp rules here. [Verify] any licence-specific transfer term before applying it to a named hemp business.
  • Insurance/bonding minimum for product businesses: none located for hemp product businesses in current Wisconsin law. State this expressly. (General commercial insurance is a business decision, not a hemp licence condition, on the evidence located.)
  • Historic DATCP revocation grounds (historic only): EmR2111 revocation grounds included pleading guilty to or conviction of any controlled-substance felony, false statements, producing cannabis over 0.3% THC with a culpable mental state greater than negligence, or negligently violating the chapter 3 times in 5 years; DATCP could also revoke for prohibited activities, failure to comply with ch. 94.55 or ATCP 22, or failure to comply with a department or law-enforcement instruction or order, except for negligent violations as defined in § 94.55(2g). Source: EmR2111 ATCP 22 — accessed 28 October 2026. Historic only — do not present as a current enforcement route.

11.3 Cultivation rules: licensing, plot registration, pre-harvest sampling, indoor/outdoor

11.3.A — Hemp growers

  • Licensing: USDA producer licence required for production outside tribal-plan jurisdiction; tribal-plan licence inside tribal-plan jurisdiction (§7.2, §7.5). No Wisconsin state cultivation licence is currently issuable.
  • Plot registration: FSA acreage report within 30 days of planting with address, geospatial location to the extent practicable, acreage/square footage, and licence number; all production locations reported (§7.4). Source: 7 CFR 990.23 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Pre-harvest sampling: mandatory, by a certified sampling agent (not the producer), no more than 30 days before anticipated harvest, flowering tops 5–8 inches, 95%-confidence method (§11.1.A). Source: 7 CFR 990.24 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Indoor/outdoor rules: no Wisconsin hemp-specific indoor/outdoor distinction was located, and 7 CFR part 990 does not impose a separate indoor regime beyond lot/greenhouse/building reporting (acreage or greenhouse/indoor square footage) and sampling-agent access to all production and storage areas. Greenhouse/indoor square footage is reported instead of field acreage where applicable. State this expressly rather than inventing indoor lighting, canopy, or setback rules. [Verify] whether any local zoning ordinance (county/city/village/town) imposes an indoor/outdoor hemp condition — none was located in Run 2, and local rules belong to Run 2 Part 8 / Part 10 cross-reference, not to this section.

11.3.B — Hemp product businesses

Cultivation rules do not apply to a product business that does not grow. A product business that also grows must hold the grower licence above. Wisconsin has no separate cultivation rule for product businesses.

11.3.C — Historic DATCP cultivation rules (historic only)

  • Planting report by 1 July each year and within 30 days of planting, including address of each growing location, variety per lot, GPS coordinates of each lot, maps showing growing locations, lot entrances and boundaries, and acres or greenhouse square footage planted.
  • Final production report by 31 October including total acres/square footage harvested and destroyed, total weight or volume harvested, variety per lot, percentage of harvest sold, and final disposition/destination including destroyed hemp.
  • All lots sampled before harvest; growers had to obtain a fit-for-commerce certificate from DATCP for each lot before transport from the growing location, issued after a successful test showing THC at 0.3% or below; no processor could acquire or process harvested unprocessed hemp without a legible copy of all fit-for-commerce certificates specific to that hemp; hemp from different lots could not be combined until a certificate for each lot was issued; harvested hemp without a required certificate or not sampled as required was subject to seizure by DATCP, destruction at the licensee's expense, and possible suspension or revocation.
  • A lot was defined as a contiguous area in a field, greenhouse, facility, or growing structure containing the same variety or strain throughout.
  • Remediation was rendering a non-compliant lot compliant by removing and destroying flower material while retaining stalk, stems, leaf material, and seeds, or by shredding or grinding the plant material into homogeneous biomass.
  • Source: EmR2111 ATCP 22 — accessed 28 October 2026. All historic; the fit-for-commerce certificate system ended 1 January 2022 and has no current equivalent.

11.3.D — Case note: State v. Syrrakos (scope of historic DATCP rules)

Wisconsin Court of Appeals Nos. 2024AP554-CR and 2024AP556-CR, discussing EmR2111 ATCP 22.09, 22.10, 22.12, and 22.13, held in the passages located that sampling, testing, destruction, and remediation pertain to hemp plants grown in lots, that only a person issued a DATCP grower licence is authorised to grow hemp, and that the obligation to destroy lots with unlawfully high THC and the option to remediate apply only to licensed growers, not to a processor holding only a processor licence. Source: Wisconsin Court of Appeals, Nos. 2024AP554-CR / 2024AP556-CR — accessed 28 October 2026. Flag preserved: Run 1 already flagged the outcome of State v. Syrrakos, No. 2024AP000556, as Verify for Run 2. The appellate passages above do not by themselves close that flag — [Verify] the final disposition before this file states an outcome.

11.4 Security

11.4.A — Hemp growers

No Wisconsin hemp-specific security rule (fence, lock, camera, alarm, guard) was located, and no such rule was located in the USDA sections opened (7 CFR 990.20–990.32). What USDA does require, and this file presents as access/audit rather than as a "security system" rule, is: complete and unrestricted sampling-agent access during business hours to all hemp/cannabis plants, production and storage areas, land, buildings and structures, and all licence locations; and USDA access to any premises where hemp plants may be held, with records available during reasonable business hours. Source: 7 CFR 990.24, 990.28 — accessed 28 October 2026.

11.4.B — Hemp product businesses

Wisconsin has no hemp-specific security rule for product businesses. General food-plant, retail-food, and warehouse sanitation/security provisions in ATCP 70/75/71 apply to licensed food premises generally but are not hemp security rules and were not inventoried as such in Run 2. State the absence expressly. (The detailed security, transport, and inventory rules in failed 2025 AB 606 / SB 681 are PROPOSED AND FAILED — do not present them as law.)

11.5 Waste disposal, including non-compliant hemp destruction

11.5.A — Hemp growers (current USDA regime)

  • Non-compliant plants (exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level) constitute marijuana, a Schedule I controlled substance. Disposal must be either via a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement, or on-site disposal at the farm or hemp production facility. The producer must notify USDA of intent to dispose of or remediate and verify disposal/remediation with required documentation. Source: 7 CFR 990.27 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Remediation (where chosen) requires additional sampling and testing of the post-remediated crop; post-harvest samples are only for remediated biomass. Source: 7 CFR 990.26–990.27 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Laboratory disposal: labs must have an effective disposal procedure for non-compliant samples. Source: 7 CFR 990.25 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • General agricultural/plant waste (compliant hemp stalks, stems, leaves, etc.): no Wisconsin hemp-specific waste rule was located beyond the non-compliant destruction rule above. General waste, burning, and composting law was not searched to completion for Run 2 — [Verify/Unavailable] rather than assumed.

11.5.B — Hemp product businesses

  • Non-compliant hemp destruction: no hemp-specific product-business destruction rule was located. A product business holding non-compliant (over-THC) material is holding marijuana under state/federal law, not hemp waste — this file must not describe it as ordinary waste. The lawful route for a grower is §11.5.A; for a non-grower product business, [Verify] the applicable disposal route (law enforcement / DEA reverse distributor) before publishing any instruction.
  • Food waste / distressed food: ATCP 70's distressed-food provisions require records of distressed food handled, including description, source/cause, receipt date, reprocessing/reconditioning performed, and final disposition including the name and address of any waste disposal firm or site operator receiving it; reprocessed/reconditioned food sold at wholesale requires written notice to the purchaser/recipient. These are general food rules, not hemp-specific. Source: Wis. Admin. Code § ATCP 70.24 (distressed food, via the ATCP 70 page cited in §11.1.B) — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Historic DATCP destruction reporting (historic only): destruction reports included GPS coordinates for the centre of each growing location, variety, total acreage, destruction method, completion date, and a signed licensee certification; if the licensee failed to destroy or used a non-compliant method, DATCP could destroy and invoice all costs. Source: EmR2111 ATCP 22 — accessed 28 October 2026.

11.6 Environmental rules: water, pesticide limits, energy

11.6.A — Hemp growers

  • Water: no Wisconsin hemp-specific water-use, irrigation, or water-discharge rule was located in the USDA sections opened or in Run 1. General Wisconsin water law (e.g. DNR water-use, well, and discharge rules) was not searched to completion for Run 2 — [Verify/Unavailable]; do not state that hemp growers are exempt from general water law.
  • Pesticides — partially verified, with a conflict to flag: DATCP document "Pesticides Licensed for Use in Wisconsin for Hemp," developed under Wis. Stat. ch. 94.69 and Wis. Admin. Code ch. ATCP 29.50, lists products including Regalia Biofungicide / Regalia CG / Regalia Rx, cold-pressed neem oil products, and 25(b) minimum-risk products. It states it is for information purposes only and does not constitute legal advice, and requests to add products may be made by manufacturers/registrants only. Source: DATCP, datcp.wi.gov — Pesticides for Hemp (PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026. Conflict: an older 2019 Extension meeting note stated no pesticides were registered for industrial hemp and only 25(b) products, which must be registered in Wisconsin, could be used. The current DATCP list's date was not confirmed in the extracts obtained — [Verify] the list's date and current status before publication; do not publish the product list as current until dated. No hemp-specific pesticide residue limit, pre-harvest interval, or application-rate rule beyond label directions and general ch. 94.69 / ATCP 29 law was located in Run 2. General pesticide applicator licensing law was not searched to completion — [Verify].
  • Energy: no Wisconsin hemp-specific energy-use rule (lighting, HVAC, energy reporting) was located. State this expressly. (Energy provisions in failed 2025 bills are PROPOSED AND FAILED, not law.)

11.6.B — Hemp product businesses

Water, pesticides, energy: no hemp-specific rules located. General food-plant water-supply, sanitation, and facility rules in ATCP 70 apply to licensed food plants generally (e.g. ample water/steam for cleaning in canning operations, sanitizer concentration limits) but are not hemp environmental rules. Source: Wis. Admin. Code ch. ATCP 70 — accessed 28 October 2026.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts, promotions

11.7.A — Hemp growers

  • Sampling (of the crop): covered in §11.1.A — mandatory pre-harvest sampling by a certified agent; producers may not self-sample. No separate "promotional sample" rule for growers was located.
  • Giveaways, discounts, promotions of hemp product by a grower-seller: no current Wisconsin hemp-specific rule located. General advertising, food-labelling, and trade-practice law applies generally but was not inventoried as a hemp rule in Run 2.

11.7.B — Hemp product businesses

  • Wisconsin has no current hemp-specific rule for product sampling, giveaways, discounts, or promotions. State this expressly.
  • The only detailed rules located are PROPOSED AND FAILED (2025 AB 606 / SB 681), including: a proposed salesperson permit allowing a sample of a brand to a Class "A" or Class "HDC-A" licensee who had not previously purchased that brand; distributor same-price and single-transaction / single-delivery / single-invoice discount rules; manufacturer/distributor business-entertainment limits of $500 in one day and no more than 8 days in a calendar year; and sweepstakes/contest promotion conditions. All failed 23 March 2026. Label PROPOSED AND FAILED wherever mentioned; never present as current promotion law. Source: 2025 SB 681 proposal text — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Local rules are not state rules: local age/display/buffer rules from Run 1 — City of Madison General Ordinances § 23.202 and Wood County Ordinance 236 — are local, not state, product-business rules, and are cross-referenced to Run 1 Part 3 / Run 2 Part 8, not restated here.

11.8 Drug testing and background-check rules for licence holders and staff

11.8.A — Hemp growers

  • Licence holders / key participants: criminal history report within 60 days of submission; 10-year controlled-substance felony bar with the pre-20-December-2018 § 7606 exception; material falsification = ineligibility (§7.3). Source: 7 CFR 990.20–990.22 — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Staff / employees of a grower: no USDA hemp-specific drug-testing or background-check rule for grower staff (beyond key participants) was located in 7 CFR part 990, and no Wisconsin hemp-specific staff rule was located. State this expressly.
  • Drug testing of the grower personally: no hemp-grower drug-testing requirement was located. (Drug testing in this context means testing of people, not THC testing of plants — the plant-testing regime is §11.1.A.)

11.8.B — Hemp product businesses

  • Licence holders and staff: no hemp-specific drug-testing or background-check rule was located in current Wisconsin law for product-business owners or staff. General employment law applies: Run 1 Part 1.8 already found no Wisconsin employment protection for marijuana use or a positive marijuana drug test and no general private-sector drug-testing restriction statute located. That Run 1 finding stands and is cross-referenced, not re-litigated here.
  • Historic DATCP background check (historic only): lifetime controlled-substances bar via DOJ criminal history search for initial licensure (§7.3). Historic only.

11.9 Research and university work — only verified programmes, with sources

Scope note: education/certificate offerings are distinguished from research programmes and are not presented as research.

UW–Madison — VERIFIED research

  • Shelby Ellison, Department of Plant and Agroecosystem Sciences, leads hemp genetics and breeding work, feral cannabis germplasm collection for a U.S. hemp seedbank, and the Midwestern Hemp Research Collaborative trialling high-cannabinoid, grain, and fibre cultivars.
  • UW–Madison Extension hemp work and Arlington Agricultural Research Station (Columbia County) research plots for high-cannabinoid / low-THC hemp; a smaller high-cannabinoid/low-THC plot focused on best growing practices and variety compliance in Wisconsin, and a larger industrial/feral hemp plot focused on grain/fibre genetics.
  • Bruce, Connelly, and Ellison, "Different Fertility Approaches in Organic Hemp Production," Sustainability (2022), finding higher organic fertility increased biomass without changing the CBD:THC ratio.
  • UW–Madison / Extension hemp economics: Paul Mitchell (Department of Agricultural and Applied Economics; UW–Division of Extension state specialist; Renk Agribusiness Institute director) led a hemp research team with the College of Agricultural and Life Sciences researching the economic side of hemp.
  • Sources: UW–Madison News, news.wisc.edu — UW–Madison hemp project — accessed 28 October 2026; UW–Madison Extension hemp field-day materials (Division of Extension, 2020) — accessed 28 October 2026; Sustainability 2022 article as cited — accessed 28 October 2026; Shepherd Express, "Agricultural Economics Professor Sheds Insight on Wisconsin's Hemp Crops" (secondary, for the Mitchell/Renk attribution only), shepherdexpress.com — hemp economics — accessed 28 October 2026.

UW–River Falls — VERIFIED (marketing/economics study; no bench hemp research programme verified)

  • 2019 Wisconsin Hemp Marketing Study, authored by Shaheer Burney, PhD, and Amber Remble, PhD, Department of Agricultural Economics, University of Wisconsin–River Falls; funding from the Renk Agribusiness Institute at UW–Madison; based on the 2018 Wisconsin DATCP Survey of 143 growers and processors, the 2019 Wisconsin Hemp Marketing Survey of 140 growers and processors, and grower/processor/extension interviews and farm visits. The report itself cautions that estimates are a broad reflection of the industry, not standards or recommendations, given wide variation in yields, costs, prices, and returns. Source (official university source): Renk Agribusiness Institute, UW–Madison, renk.aae.wisc.edu — 2019 Wisconsin Hemp Marketing Study — accessed 28 October 2026 (page opened directly; authorship, funding, and survey bases quoted above).
  • Burney also appeared as a speaker at the Division of Extension UW–Madison Industrial Hemp Virtual Field Day (18 September 2020) alongside UW–Madison hemp researchers. Source: Division of Extension field-day flyer via DATCP GovDelivery, content.govdelivery.com — Extension Hemp Field Day flyer (PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026.
  • Not verified: no UW–River Falls hemp field-trial, breeding, or laboratory research programme was located in Run 2. A UW–River Falls faculty mention in connection with a UW–Stout low-cost wireless farm-field sensor network is not a hemp programme and must not be presented as one.

UW–Platteville — VERIFIED as education certificates, NOT a research programme

  • UW–Platteville Continuing Education Institute expanded its cannabis-related certificates (announced 5 October 2020) to include an Agriculture and Horticulture Certificate: students learn to care for cannabis plants, study cannabis botany and genetics, and explore plant growth techniques in a six-month programme offered in partnership with Green Flower. The suite also included Business of Cannabis, Cannabis Law and Policy, and Cannabis Healthcare and Medicine; each certificate comprised three eight-week courses (24 weeks), instructor-led, 100% online. The programme was later refreshed as online Cannabis Career Certificates with Green Flower (announced November 2025, classes beginning 13 January 2026 per secondary coverage). Source (official university source, opened directly): UW–Platteville News, 5 October 2020, uwplatt.edu — cannabis certificates — accessed 28 October 2026. Secondary refresh coverage: Wisconsin Ag Connection, 26 November 2025, wisconsinagconnection.com — UW–Platteville cannabis certificates — accessed 28 October 2026 (secondary; use only for the refresh, not for law).
  • Express finding: no UW–Platteville hemp research programme (field trials, breeding, phytoremediation, or laboratory research) was located in Run 2. Describe Platteville under education/workforce, not research, or omit it from a research list with a note.

UW–Stevens Point — VERIFIED research (phytoremediation)

  • Phytoremediation research using hemp and alfalfa for soil pollutants including heavy metals such as lead and arsenic.
  • 2023 Universities of Wisconsin Innovation Grant, $175,000, January 2024 through December 2025, for "Phytoremediation of PFAS in Wisconsin Soils using Hemp and Alfalfa," team: Brian Barringer, Ann Impullitti, Joseph Mondloch, Shannon Riha, and Bryant Scharenbroch (UW–Stevens Point) plus Shelby Ellison (UW–Madison), studying how hemp and alfalfa remove PFAS, where PFAS is stored in plant tissues, and how soil quality influences remediation. Source: Universities of Wisconsin Innovation Grant announcement / UW–Stevens Point programme materials — accessed 28 October 2026. [Verify] the grant's current status and any published results before this file states outcomes beyond the grant's stated aims — the grant period ended December 2025 and results were not verified in Run 2.

12.1 Paraphernalia / shops

§961.571(1)(a) definition turns on use/design/primary intent for a controlled substance "in violation of this chapter" [Verify current page]; no WI court holding on hemp-device paraphernalia located [Verify/Unavailable]. Tobacco/vape retailer licence §134.65: licence from municipal clerk required for cigarettes/tobacco (later form §134.65(1d) adds electronic vaping devices); fee not less than $5 nor more than $100/year, non-transferable, 2-year records [all Verify current §134.65 text — retrieved text mixes versions]. No state "smoke shop" or hemp-shop licence located. §101.123 Clean Indoor Air / smoking-lounge rules not verified [Verify].

12.2 Minimum age

State law: none located (consistent with Run 1). Local age-21 rules cross-referenced only (Madison §23.202, Wood County Ord. 236, Dane County OA-016 — details/gaps as in Run 1). DHS September 2024 SCAODA report recommended an under-21 hemp-derived cannabinoid ban — recommendation, not law (source: dhs.wisconsin.gov — SCAODA derived psychoactive cannabis products report (PDF) — accessed 28 October 2026).

12.3 Driving

§346.63(1)(a) impairment limb and §346.63(1)(am) restricted-substance limb ("detectable amount," no impairment proof on its face) [Verify from directly opened current §346.63]. §340.01(50m) definition reconciled: (a) Schedule I other than a THC; (am) 6-monoacetylmorphine; (b) analog of (a); (c) cocaine/metabolites; (d) methamphetamine; (e) delta-9-THC, excluding precursors/metabolites, at ≥1 ng/mL blood — so Wisconsin is not pure zero-tolerance for delta-9; inactive carboxy-THC alone does not prove a (1)(am) charge; 2019 Act 68 made this change (WI JI-Criminal 1266; State v. Reel, Nos. 2024AP233-CR/2024AP234-CR, unpublished). State v. Smet 2005 WI App 263 / State v. Luedtke strict-liability holdings are secondary summaries [Verify against opinions].

Prescription defence §346.63(1)(d) (corroborated by three search excerpts, still [Verify]): valid prescription for delta-9 THC is a defence in a (1)(am) meth/GHB/delta-9 case — a §961.32(2m) certification is not on its face a prescription; whether FDA-approved or out-of-state prescriptions satisfy it is [Verify/Unavailable] — do not advise certified-CBD users are protected.

Implied consent §343.305 (search excerpts/secondary, [Verify]): refusal revocation 1 year first / 2 / 3 years subsequent, doubled with a passenger under 16 (§343.305(10)); ignition interlock under §343.301(1g); administrative suspension track [Verify §§343.305(7)–(8)].

2025 Wisconsin Act 99 (enacted 13 Mar 2026, eff. 15 Mar 2026, originally SB 678; secondary analyses, Act text [Verify]): amends §343.303 preliminary screening (not §343.305, not §346.63) to allow breath or oral fluid; result admissible only for probable cause / to prove a chemical test was properly required; refusing the preliminary swab does not itself trigger §343.305 revocation; sample must be destroyed/returned after test, not kept for DNA.

DRE: Wisconsin has a DRE programme via WisDOT Division of State Patrol, "nearly 400" DREs (WisDOT) — use that figure; ARIDE counts conflict (6,600 vs 8,000+, do not pick one); no DRE statute located.

OWI penalties §346.65: secondary summaries only [Verify every figure before publishing] — 1st $150–$300 forfeiture, 6–9 month revocation; escalation through Class H/G/F/E felonies at 4th/5th–6th/7th–9th/10th+; minor-passenger, injury, great bodily harm, homicide tiers all [Verify]; surcharge amounts conflict ($435 vs $535) [Verify]. 2025 Act 210 interlock overhaul is NOT in effect until 1 Sept 2027 — do not publish as current.

12.4 Expungement

§973.015(1m)(a)1. verbatim retrieved via annotation page (current page not opened, [Verify]): court may order expungement at sentencing if person was under 25 at commission, offence max imprisonment ≤6 years, court finds benefit/no harm, sentence successfully completed. No marijuana-specific provision in §973.015 located; no post-sentence petition route in current-law text retrieved (2023 SB 70/SB 78/AB 43/AB 37/SB 38 petition proposals and 2023 SB 1113 §973.016 are drafts, not law). Forfeitures cannot be expunged under §973.015 (State v. Michaels; Kenosha County v. Frett) — municipal tickets are not expungement candidates on retrieved law; how ordinance records are cleared is [Unavailable/Verify]. DOT records carved out. Class I felony bar at §973.015(1m)(a)3.b. where a prior felony exists in lifetime [Verify interaction with (3g)(e) counting].

New tension flagged, not resolved: §961.47 conditional discharge as retrieved covers possession under §961.41(3g)(b), while THC possession is (3g)(e) — whether conditional discharge is available for a THC charge is [Verify]; do not assume it applies, and do not conflate conditional discharge (no judgment, dismissal on fulfilment) with expungement. §974.06 is a collateral-attack route, not expungement [Verify scope].

Pardon/clemency: Wis. Const. art. V, §6; §§304.09–304.10 procedure; Evers-era eligibility (felony only, off paper ≥5 years, etc.) is secondary [Verify — administration changes Jan 2027]; a pardon does not expunge/seal; Gov. Evers' final application deadline was 5 October 2026 and has passed as of 28 Oct 2026 (announced 24 Sept 2026; 2,237 pardons granted incl. eligible marijuana felonies, secondary); successor process [Unavailable]. No categorical marijuana pardon programme located.

12.5 Landlord / tenant

Numbering resolved — no-opportunity-to-remedy rule is at §704.17(3m), not §704.19(7)(a) (older print). §704.17(3m)(b)1. verbatim retrieved: landlord may terminate without opportunity to remedy on 5 days' notice for criminal activity threatening health/safety/peaceful enjoyment or drug-related criminal activity on/near premises; no arrest/conviction needed ((3m)(b)2.). Definition flag [Verify current §704.17(3m)(a)]: current compilation reads "'Drug-related criminal activity' means criminal activity that involves the manufacture or distribution of a controlled substance" (older 2015 AB 568 draft was broader: manufacture/possession/use/distribution) — if manufacture/distribution-only is correct, simple possession/use alone is not within that limb (though other limbs or lease clauses may apply).

Public housing: Milwaukee City Housing Authority v. Cobb (WI Supreme Court, via State Bar digest) held 42 U.S.C. §1437d(l)(6) preempts §704.17(2)(b) right-to-remedy for drug-related criminal activity evictions — cite Cobb for preemption only, not current subsection numbers; Housing Authority of Milwaukee v. King not fully read [Verify]; Section 8 voucher vs public-housing distinction [Unavailable/Verify]. No WI statute gives a tenant a right to possess/use marijuana; private enforcement rests on lease clauses + §704.17(3m) + nuisance §823.113. Hemp-vs-marijuana tenant analysis (lawful hemp is not a ch. 961 violation) is analysis, not a holding — no WI court decision located.

12.6 Guns (must not be presented as settled legal advice; conflict live/unresolved)

Federal §922(g)(3) baseline as given; baseline does not state §922(g)(3) no longer applies to Schedule III medical users, no ATF safe harbour located, and Wisconsin has no state-licensed medical programme for such an argument to attach to. US v. Hemani (No. 24-1234, decided 18 June 2026) is parent Run 1 federal material, not verified in Part 12 — do not expand into a Wisconsin rule. Wisconsin §941.29(1m) list as retrieved contains no freestanding "unlawful drug user" disqualifier [Verify full paragraph list]; a 2nd-offence THC Class I felony conviction would trigger (1m)(a) on its face; a 1st-offence misdemeanour would not by itself. Pardon exception §941.29(5)(a) [Verify]. Concealed carry §175.60(3): sources retrieved are 2001–2003 bill texts, not a current page [Verify] — key item is (3)(f), no ch. 961 conviction in preceding 3 years. §941.20(1)(bm): Class A misdemeanour to operate/go armed with a firearm with a "detectable amount" of a restricted controlled substance in blood, with the same valid-prescription defence for delta-9; whether (bm) imports the §340.01(50m)(e) ≥1 ng/mL threshold is unresolved [Verify]. Whether lawful hemp-THC use is "unlawful use" for §922(g)(3) is [Unavailable/unresolved] — do not advise hemp users are safe.

12.7 Pets / vets

DATCP guidance (May 2020, P-DARM339; Sept 2020 reissue observed; predates 1 Jan 2022 programme end — current position must be re-checked): "No" — hemp, its byproducts and extracts (e.g. CBD) cannot be used in/on any animal feed, pet food, or pet treats; no post-2020 WI feed approval located [Unavailable]. Veterinary Examining Board Guidance VEB-GD-002 (July 2023), via DATCP document search excerpt [Verify by opening full document]: no FDA/AAFCO-approved cannabis foods for animals; animal products are foods or drugs, not "dietary supplements"; CBD/THC products are drugs; vets may discuss such products and express concerns but administering/prescribing/dispensing/referring/recommending must conform to state/federal law including FDA regulations (§89.07, VE 1.58(7), VE 2.26(9)). No WI statute/rule authorising vets to prescribe/dispense CBD/THC/marijuana to animals located.

12.8 Schools

No Wisconsin DPI statewide CBD guidance located — state this explicitly; do not fill with other states' guidance (Virginia district policies observed in search must be excluded). DPI School Health Laws compilation (11 Sept 2025) lists §§961.32(2m)(b), 961.34(2), 961.38(1n)(a) but no CBD school guidance. General framework §§118.29/118.291 (search excerpts [Verify which governs a given school type]): prescription drugs need practitioner written instructions + parent/guardian written consent + original pharmacy-labelled package; nonprescription products need parent instructions/consent + original manufacturer's package.

District policies conflict — do not generalise: Waunakee Community School District (Dane County) policy as retrieved bans administering CBD at school (lack of FDA approval, inconsistent THC levels; misspellings "Cannibidoil"/"tetrahydrocannainol" are in the original); one further WI district policy is permissive if prescription-medicine requirements are met (district/date/product scope must be verified before naming — do not name on current evidence); two further WI district policies ban CBD (districts not identified in excerpts [Verify before naming]). AWSA commentary is secondary; its "0.3% THC" simplification must not replace Run 1's §94.55 definition. Hemp CBD vs certified CBD vs FDA-approved Epidiolex distinction for schools: no DPI or court ruling located [Unavailable]. Possession rules near schools (Madison §23.20 1,000ft/school bus; §961.49 +5 years for delivery/intent) are not administration rules — do not conflate.

Part 12 "not law" list (never publish as law): 2013 AB 810 §346.63(2p) THC tier; 2023 expungement petition bills; 2023 SB 1113 §973.016; 2021 AB 1140 §941.29 additions; 2025 Act 210 (eff. 1 Sept 2027); DHS Sept 2024 under-21 hemp recommendations; Dane County 2022 Q2; Virginia school policies.
